The Java DSA API Codex

Every class, every method, every signature you will ever reach for while solving Data Structures & Algorithms problems — with input types, return types, time complexity, and the exact situation where you use it.

Chapter 00

How to use this codex

This is not a tutorial you read once. It is a working reference you keep open in a second tab while you solve problems, plus a syllabus you can grind through in order. Both uses are supported deliberately.

The three ways in

1 · Search-first

Type any method name, return type, or complexity into the filter bar at the top. Every table row in the document is filtered live. Press Esc to clear.

Try ceilingKey, O(log n), heap, J21.

2 · Syllabus order

Read Parts I → V in order. That covers 95% of what LeetCode Easy/Medium needs. Parts VI–VIII are for Hard problems and contests.

3 · Problem-first

Jump straight to Chapter 56 · Pattern → API playbook. Find your problem shape, see exactly which APIs solve it.

How to read the method tables

Every API table in this document uses the same four columns. Learning to read them once pays off 1000 times.

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
exampleMethod(int from, int to) boolean O(1) Column 1 is the exact signature — the method name plus every parameter type you must pass, in order. Column 2 is the output type you assign to. Column 3 is worst-case time unless noted as amortised. Column 4 tells you what it does and the DSA situation where you reach for it.
overloaded(Object o) J21 void O(n) Badges mark version and risk. J8 J9 J11 J17 J21 = first version where it is available. HOT = appears constantly in interviews, memorise it. TRAP = has a subtle behaviour that will silently break your solution. LEGACY = works, but do not use it.
◆ The 80/20 of this entire document

If you only ever memorise six things, memorise these. They appear in the majority of all interview solutions:

  1. HashMap — getOrDefault, put, merge, computeIfAbsent, containsKey, entrySet
  2. ArrayList — add, get, size, set, remove(int) vs remove(Object)
  3. ArrayDeque — push/pop/peek as a stack, offer/poll/peek as a queue
  4. PriorityQueue — construction with a Comparator, offer, poll, peek
  5. Arrays — sort, fill, binarySearch, copyOfRange, toString/deepToString
  6. StringBuilder — append, reverse, toString, deleteCharAt, setLength

Everything else in this codex is leverage on top of those six.

A note on complexity notation used here

NotationMeaning in this document
O(1)Constant. Genuinely constant, no hidden loop.
O(1)*Amortised constant. Individual calls can be O(n) (a resize or rehash), but averaged over many calls it is constant. ArrayList.add and HashMap.put live here.
O(1)~Expected constant, assuming a decent hash. Degrades to O(log n) in modern Java when a bucket treeifies, and to O(n) if you write a pathological hashCode.
O(log n)Logarithmic. Balanced-tree or heap operations, binary search.
O(n)Linear in the size of the receiver collection.
O(k)Linear in the size of the argument or of a sub-range, not the whole collection.
O(n log n)Comparison sort territory.
O(n + k)Linear-time counting sort territory, where k is the value range.
◆ Version assumption

All code here compiles on Java 17 (the current long-term-support baseline used by most hiring platforms) and on Java 21. Anything that requires Java 21 specifically is badged J21 and an alternative is always given, because many online judges still run 17. LeetCode currently runs Java 21; Codeforces runs 21; HackerRank and many company platforms sit on 17 or even 11.

Chapter 01

The 12-box mental model

Before any API detail: every DSA problem you will ever see reduces to choosing the right container and the right traversal. There are only about twelve containers that matter. If you can name the right one within ten seconds of reading a problem, you are already most of the way to a solution.

The decision table read the left column, take the middle column

What the problem needs
The Java type you reach for
Why this one and not another
Fixed-size sequence of numbers, indexed access, fastest possible
int[] / long[] / char[]
No boxing, contiguous memory, cache-friendly. Always the first choice when size is known.
Growable sequence, indexed access
ArrayList<T>
O(1) get/set, amortised O(1) append. The default list. Never LinkedList for this.
"Have I seen this before?" / dedupe
HashSet<T>
O(1) contains. For primitives in a small known range, use boolean[] or int[] instead — 10× faster.
Count occurrences / index lookup by key
HashMap<K,V>
O(1) lookup. The single most used data structure in interviews. For char keys use int[26] or int[128].
Keys in sorted order / "next greater key" / range queries
TreeMap<K,V> · TreeSet<T>
O(log n) with floorKey/ceilingKey/higherKey/subMap. The only standard structure with ordered navigation.
Insertion order must be preserved while deduping/mapping
LinkedHashSet · LinkedHashMap
Same O(1) as hash versions, plus a doubly-linked list through the entries. Also the basis of an LRU cache.
LIFO — stack, DFS, matched brackets, monotonic stack
ArrayDeque<T>
push/pop/peek. Faster and cleaner than the legacy Stack (which is synchronised and iterates backwards).
FIFO — BFS, level-order traversal, sliding window of indices
ArrayDeque<T>
offer/poll/peek. Same class as the stack — learn one class, get both.
"Give me the smallest/largest right now, repeatedly"
PriorityQueue<T>
O(log n) insert and extract-min, O(1) peek. Top-K, Dijkstra, merge-K-lists, median (two heaps).
Prefix lookups on strings, autocomplete, word-search
Hand-rolled Trie
Java has no built-in trie. Chapter 48 gives you the canonical implementation to memorise.
"Are these two things connected?" / connected components
Hand-rolled DSU
Union-Find with path compression. Near-O(1) amortised. Chapter 49.
Dense boolean flags over a large range, set algebra
BitSet
64 flags per long. Memory-efficient sieve, bitmask DP, fast AND/OR/XOR of whole sets.
◆ The primitive-array shortcut

Interviewers love this and beginners miss it. Whenever the keys of your map are drawn from a small, known, dense range, throw the map away and use an array:

  • Lowercase English letters → int[26], index with c - 'a'
  • Any ASCII character → int[128], index with c directly
  • All chars including extended → int[256]
  • Digits '0'–'9' → int[10], index with c - '0'
  • Node ids 0..n-1 in a graph → int[n] or List<Integer>[]

You get the same O(1) with zero hashing, zero boxing, and zero object headers. This is usually the difference between a solution that runs in 4 ms and one that runs in 60 ms.

Traversal patterns the other half of every solution

TraversalRecognise it when…
Single pass + HashMap"find a pair / count something / first duplicate". Usually O(n) and usually the intended answer.
Two pointers (same direction)Sorted array, or "remove/partition in place", or fast/slow cycle detection.
Two pointers (opposite ends)Sorted array pair-sum, palindrome check, container-with-most-water, reversing in place.
Sliding window"longest/shortest contiguous subarray or substring satisfying X". Expand right, shrink left.
Binary search on indexSorted array, or rotated sorted array, or "find boundary".
Binary search on answer"minimise the maximum" / "maximum feasible capacity" + a monotone isFeasible(x) predicate.
Prefix sums / difference arrayMany range-sum queries, or many range-update-then-read-once operations.
Monotonic stack"next greater / previous smaller element", histogram areas, trapping rain water.
BFS on a queueShortest path in an unweighted graph or grid, level-by-level tree processing, multi-source spread.
DFS recursion / explicit stackAll-paths enumeration, cycle detection, topological order, tree recursion, flood fill.
Backtracking"generate all permutations/combinations/subsets/valid boards". Choose → recurse → un-choose.
Dynamic programmingOverlapping subproblems + optimal substructure. Memo on the recursion, or fill a table bottom-up.
Heap (top-K)"K largest / K closest / K most frequent". Keep a size-K heap of the opposite polarity.
Union-FindDynamic connectivity, "number of islands" with merging, Kruskal's MST, account merging.

Chapter 56 maps each of these patterns to the exact API calls that implement it.

Chapter 02

Primitives, ranges & overflow

Roughly one in eight "wrong answer" verdicts on hard problems is an integer overflow, and almost all of them are one of three specific mistakes. Knowing the exact ranges is not pedantry; it is defect prevention.

The eight primitive types

TypeBitsRangeDefaultWrapperDSA relevance
byte8−128 … 1270ByteRare. Occasionally for memory-tight grids.
short16−32,768 … 32,7670ShortEssentially never used in interviews.
char160 … 65,535 (unsigned)'\u0000'CharacterConstant. A UTF-16 code unit. Arithmetic-capable: c - 'a' is an int.
int32−2,147,483,648 … 2,147,483,6470IntegerThe workhorse. Max ≈ 2.1×109. Memorise that number.
long64±9,223,372,036,854,775,8070LLong≈ 9.2×1018. Use for sums, products, and anything near 109.
float32~7 decimal digits0.0fFloatAvoid. Not enough precision for geometry problems.
double64~15–17 decimal digits0.0dDoubleGeometry, averages, medians. Exact for integers up to 253.
boolean—true / falsefalseBooleanboolean[] for visited sets and sieves.
▲ The three overflow bugs you will actually write

1 · Binary search midpoint. (lo + hi) / 2 overflows when lo + hi > 231−1. This is a famous bug that sat in the JDK's own Arrays.binarySearch for nine years.

// WRONG — overflows for large indices
int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
// RIGHT — three equivalent safe forms
int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;              // unsigned shift absorbs the overflow
int mid = Math.floorDiv(lo + hi, 2);    // only if lo+hi itself fits

2 · Sum or product of an int array. 105 elements each up to 109 sums to 1014 — nine hundred times past int.

// WRONG
int sum = 0;
for (int x : nums) sum += x;
// RIGHT
long sum = 0;
for (int x : nums) sum += x;
// Also right, and shorter:
long sum = Arrays.stream(nums).asLongStream().sum();
// TRAP: Arrays.stream(nums).sum() returns int and overflows silently!

3 · Multiplying two ints and storing in a long. The multiplication happens in int arithmetic first, then widens. Casting after the fact does nothing.

int a = 100000, b = 100000;
long bad  = a * b;              // 1410065408 — already wrapped before assignment
long good = (long) a * b;       // 10000000000 — promotes a, so the whole op is long
long ok2  = Math.multiplyFull(a, b); // J9: int×int → long, explicit and safe

Sentinel values — use these, not magic numbers

Use caseWrite thisWhy
"No answer yet, looking for a minimum"int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;Any real candidate beats it.
"No answer yet, looking for a maximum"int best = Integer.MIN_VALUE;Do not use 0 — arrays can be all-negative. This is the classic Kadane bug.
Unreachable distance in Dijkstra/BFSInteger.MAX_VALUE / 2 or (int)1e9Leaves headroom so dist + w does not overflow when you relax an edge.
Unreachable in a long DP tableLong.MAX_VALUE / 4Same headroom logic.
"Not computed yet" in a memo table-1 plus Arrays.fill(memo, -1)Works when all real answers are ≥ 0. If 0 and −1 are both legal answers, use an Integer[] and test for null.
Floating-point infinityDouble.POSITIVE_INFINITYArithmetic-safe: INF + x stays INF, no overflow.
▲ Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE) is negative

There is no positive counterpart to −2147483648 in 32-bit two's complement, so Math.abs returns the input unchanged — still negative. Same for Math.abs(Long.MIN_VALUE), and same for -Integer.MIN_VALUE. Problems that ask you to reverse digits or take absolute values of arbitrary ints are specifically designed to hit this.

Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE)      // → -2147483648  (!!)
Math.absExact(Integer.MIN_VALUE) // J15: throws ArithmeticException instead of lying
Math.abs((long) Integer.MIN_VALUE) // → 2147483648 — widen first, always safe

Boxing, caching, and why == betrays you

Integer a = 127, b = 127;
a == b            // true  — both point to the same cached object
Integer c = 128, d = 128;
c == d            // FALSE — outside the cache, two distinct objects
c.equals(d)       // true  — always compare wrappers with equals()

// The JLS mandates a cache for Integer/Short/Byte/Character/Long in [-128, 127]
// and for all Boolean values. Never rely on it; never use == on wrappers.

// Consequence inside collections — this bites constantly:
List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1000, 2000, 3000));
int idx = list.indexOf(2000);   // 1 — indexOf uses equals(), so this is fine
list.remove(Integer.valueOf(2000)); // removes the VALUE 2000
list.remove(1);                  // removes the element at INDEX 1 — different method!
▲ Unboxing a null throws NullPointerException
Map<String,Integer> count = new HashMap<>();
int c = count.get("missing");          // NPE! get() returns null, then auto-unboxes
int c = count.getOrDefault("missing", 0); // 0 — always use this instead

This single substitution — getOrDefault instead of get — eliminates the most common runtime exception in counting problems.

Chapter 03

Integer & Long — the complete API

These two wrapper classes carry the entire bit-manipulation toolkit and all string↔number parsing. Everything below is static unless marked as an instance method. Long has an identical method set with long in place of int, so learn one and you know both.

Constants

FieldType / valueTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Integer.MAX_VALUE HOTint = 2147483647O(1)231−1. Initial value when minimising. Also the upper clamp when a problem says "if it overflows, return MAX_VALUE" (e.g. Reverse Integer, atoi).
Integer.MIN_VALUE HOTint = −2147483648O(1)−231. Initial value when maximising. Kadane's algorithm, max-path-sum in a binary tree, maximum gap.
Integer.SIZEint = 32O(1)Number of bits. Loop bounds in bit-DP: for (int b = 0; b < Integer.SIZE; b++).
Integer.BYTES J8int = 4O(1)Number of bytes. Rarely needed in DSA.
Long.MAX_VALUElong = 9223372036854775807O(1)≈9.2×1018. Distance sentinel in weighted-graph problems where edge weights are large.
Long.MIN_VALUElong = −9223372036854775808O(1)Maximising sentinel for long DP.
Long.SIZE / Long.BYTESint = 64 / 8O(1)Bit width of a long. Manual bitset arithmetic: word index = i / 64, bit = i % 64.

Parsing & conversion

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
String → number
parseInt(String s) HOTintO(len)Parses a signed decimal. Throws NumberFormatException on anything unparseable — including a leading + ? (no, + is allowed), spaces (not allowed), or null. Reading tokens from split input, "Add Strings", version-number comparison.
parseInt(String s, int radix)intO(len)Parses in any base 2–36. parseInt("1011", 2) → 11. Binary/hex conversion problems, base-K arithmetic.
parseInt(CharSequence s, int beg, int end, int radix) J9intO(end−beg)Parses a sub-range without allocating a substring. Hot loops when parsing long comma-separated input; avoids garbage.
parseUnsignedInt(String s) J8intO(len)Accepts 0…4294967295, stores the bit pattern. Niche.
valueOf(String s)IntegerO(len)Like parseInt but returns the boxed object. Needed when you must call list.remove(Integer.valueOf(x)) to remove by value.
valueOf(int i)IntegerO(1)Boxes, using the −128…127 cache. This is exactly what autoboxing compiles to.
decode(String s)IntegerO(len)Understands 0x, 0X, # (hex) and leading 0 (octal) prefixes. Parsing hex colour codes / IP-style input.
Number → String
toString(int i)StringO(digits)Decimal text. String.valueOf(i) and "" + i do the same; prefer String.valueOf for readability.
toString(int i, int radix)StringO(digits)Any base 2–36. "Convert to base 7", "Excel column title", Gray-code output.
toBinaryString(int i) HOTStringO(32)Base-2 text with no leading zeros and no sign — negatives appear as their 32-bit two's-complement pattern. Debugging bitmasks; counting bits via .replace("0","").length() (slow but readable).
toOctalString / toHexString(int i)StringO(1)Base 8 / base 16, lowercase, unsigned. Hex-conversion problems; IPv6 formatting.
toUnsignedString(int i [, int radix]) J8StringO(1)Treats the bit pattern as unsigned. Printing a hash or a 32-bit mask meaningfully.
toUnsignedLong(int i) J8longO(1)Zero-extends instead of sign-extending. Reversing bits of an unsigned 32-bit integer (LeetCode 190) — the canonical use.
Comparison & arithmetic helpers
compare(int x, int y) HOTint (−1 / 0 / 1)O(1)Overflow-safe three-way compare. Always use this inside a Comparator instead of a - b, which overflows when a is large positive and b large negative.
compareUnsigned(int x, int y) J8intO(1)Compares bit patterns as unsigned.
max / min / sum(int a, int b) J8intO(1)Method-reference-friendly forms. map.merge(k, 1, Integer::sum) is the tersest frequency counter in Java.
signum(int i)int (−1 / 0 / 1)O(1)Sign extraction. Direction normalisation when walking a grid or comparing slopes.
divideUnsigned / remainderUnsigned(int,int) J8intO(1)Unsigned division. Niche.
hashCode(int v) J8intO(1)Returns v itself. Useful when hand-writing a hashCode() for a coordinate class.
Instance methods (on a boxed Integer)
intValue() / longValue() / doubleValue()int / long / doubleO(1)Explicit unboxing with widening. Pulling values out of a List<Integer> into long arithmetic.
compareTo(Integer other)intO(1)Natural ordering. Used implicitly by TreeSet<Integer>, Collections.sort, and PriorityQueue without a comparator.
equals(Object o) TRAPbooleanO(1)Value equality. Use this, never ==. Also note Integer.valueOf(1).equals(1L) is false — different classes.

The bit-manipulation core memorise every row in this table

These eight methods replace hand-written loops in almost every bitmask problem. Each one is a single CPU instruction on modern hardware — they are as fast as it is possible to be.

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
bitCount(int i) HOTint 0…32O(1)Population count — how many bits are set. "Counting Bits", "Hamming Distance" (bitCount(x^y)), "Number of 1 Bits", subset-size filters in bitmask DP, "Sort Integers by Number of 1 Bits".
highestOneBit(int i)intO(1)The value of the most significant set bit (a power of two), or 0 for input 0. Largest power of two ≤ n; finding the level of a node in a heap-shaped tree.
lowestOneBit(int i) HOTintO(1)The value of the least significant set bit. Equals i & -i. The core of a Fenwick tree index step; isolating the differing bit in "Single Number III".
numberOfLeadingZeros(int i)int 0…32O(1)Zeros above the top set bit. 31 - numberOfLeadingZeros(n) is an exact integer floor(log₂ n) — far safer than Math.log(n)/Math.log(2), which has rounding errors at powers of two. Essential for sparse-table / binary-lifting sizing.
numberOfTrailingZeros(int i)int 0…32O(1)Zeros below the lowest set bit. Index of the lowest set bit → iterate set bits one at a time; counting factors of 2 in a number.
reverse(int i)intO(1)Reverses all 32 bits. "Reverse Bits" in one call — though interviewers usually want the manual loop.
reverseBytes(int i)intO(1)Endianness swap of the four bytes. Niche.
rotateLeft / rotateRight(int i, int dist)intO(1)Circular shift — bits that fall off one end reappear at the other. Cyclic-string hashing, rotation-invariant bitmask comparison.
◆ Exact integer log₂ — the pattern to memorise
// floor(log2(n)) for n >= 1, exact, no floating point
int log2 = 31 - Integer.numberOfLeadingZeros(n);
int log2L = 63 - Long.numberOfLeadingZeros(n);

// ceil(log2(n)) for n >= 1
int clog2 = 32 - Integer.numberOfLeadingZeros(n - 1);

// smallest power of two >= n  (segment-tree sizing)
int pow2 = Integer.highestOneBit(n) == n ? n : Integer.highestOneBit(n) << 1;

// why not Math.log? because this prints 2, not 3:
System.out.println((int)(Math.log(8) / Math.log(2))); // 2 on many JVMs — rounding
Chapter 04

Character — the complete API

Every string problem is a character problem. This class gives you classification, case-folding, and digit conversion — and critically, it lets you avoid writing fragile comparisons like c >= 'a' && c <= 'z' which quietly fail on Unicode input.

Classification all static, all O(1)

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
isDigit(char c) HOTbooleanO(1)True for '0'–'9' and Unicode decimal digits from other scripts. Tokenising expressions, atoi, "Decode String", version parsing, basic calculator.
isLetter(char c) HOTbooleanO(1)True for any Unicode letter. "Valid Palindrome" — skip non-letters; word extraction.
isLetterOrDigit(char c) HOTbooleanO(1)Alphanumeric test in one call. The exact predicate for LeetCode 125 "Valid Palindrome": while (!Character.isLetterOrDigit(s.charAt(i))) i++;
isAlphabetic(int codePoint) J7booleanO(1)Broader than isLetter — includes letter-numbers and modifiers. Takes a code point, not a char.
isUpperCase / isLowerCase(char c)booleanO(1)Case tests. "Camelcase Matching", "Capitalize the Title", detecting acronyms.
isWhitespace(char c) HOTbooleanO(1)Space, tab, newline, form-feed and Unicode separators, but not non-breaking space. Skipping leading blanks in atoi, "Length of Last Word", manual tokenisation.
isSpaceChar(char c)booleanO(1)Stricter: only Unicode space category. Does not match '\t' or '\n'. Use isWhitespace unless you specifically need this distinction.
isDefined(char c)booleanO(1)Whether the code point is assigned in Unicode. Almost never needed.
isEmoji(int cp) J21booleanO(1)New in 21, along with isEmojiPresentation, isEmojiModifier, isEmojiComponent. Appears in text-processing take-homes, not classic DSA.

Conversion

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
toUpperCase / toLowerCase(char c) HOTcharO(1)Case-folds a single character; returns it unchanged if there is no mapping. Case-insensitive comparison without allocating a new String: Character.toLowerCase(a) == Character.toLowerCase(b).
getNumericValue(char c) HOTintO(1)'7'→7, and also 'a'→10 … 'z'→35. Returns −1 for characters with no numeric value. Digit extraction without arithmetic; base-36 parsing.
digit(char c, int radix)int, or −1O(1)Value of c in the given base, −1 if invalid in that base. Safer than getNumericValue because it validates: Character.digit('f',16)→15 but Character.digit('f',10)→−1.
forDigit(int digit, int radix)charO(1)Inverse of digit: 15,16 → 'f'. Returns '\0' if out of range. Building base-N string output digit by digit.
toString(char c)StringO(1)One-character String. Building map keys of type String from chars; String.valueOf(c) is equivalent.
valueOf(char c)CharacterO(1)Boxes, using the 0–127 cache. Keys in a Map<Character,Integer>.
compare(char x, char y) J7intO(1)Returns x - y, safe because chars are 16-bit and cannot overflow an int. Comparators over characters.
hashCode(char c) J8intO(1)Returns the char value.
Surrogate pairs — only matters if the problem mentions emoji or non-BMP text
isHighSurrogate / isLowSurrogate(char c)booleanO(1)Detects the two halves of a 4-byte UTF-16 character.
isSurrogatePair(char hi, char lo)booleanO(1)Validates a pair before combining.
toCodePoint(char hi, char lo)intO(1)Combines a surrogate pair into a single code point.
charCount(int codePoint)int (1 or 2)O(1)How many chars this code point occupies. Correctly advancing an index when iterating text that may contain emoji.

Char arithmetic — the idioms you will type hundreds of times

// A char participates in int arithmetic. The result is an int.
char c = 'd';
int idx = c - 'a';            // 3 — THE canonical way to index a 26-slot array
char back = (char)('a' + idx);  // 'd' — cast is required, int does not narrow implicitly

// Frequency array for lowercase letters — faster than any Map
int[] freq = new int[26];
for (char ch : s.toCharArray()) freq[ch - 'a']++;

// Frequency array covering ALL ASCII (upper, lower, digits, punctuation)
int[] freq = new int[128];
for (char ch : s.toCharArray()) freq[ch]++;   // no offset needed

// Digit char -> int, and back
int d = c - '0';                // '7' -> 7
char dc = (char)(d + '0');        // 7 -> '7'

// Bitmask of which letters appear (26 bits in one int) — used in word-set problems
int mask = 0;
for (char ch : word.toCharArray()) mask |= 1 << (ch - 'a');
boolean shareNoLetters = (maskA & maskB) == 0;  // "Maximum Product of Word Lengths"

// Case toggle via XOR with 32 — a neat trick, but prefer Character.toLowerCase for clarity
char flipped = (char)(c ^ 32);       // 'a' <-> 'A' (ASCII letters only)
▲ 'a' + 1 is an int, and char + char concatenation surprises
System.out.println('a' + 1);        // 98    — int arithmetic
System.out.println((char)('a' + 1));  // b
System.out.println('a' + 'b');      // 195   — NOT "ab"
System.out.println("" + 'a' + 'b');   // ab    — force String context first
char[] arr = {'a','b'};
System.out.println(arr);            // ab    — println has a char[] overload (!)
System.out.println("" + arr);       // [C@1b6d3586 — concatenation uses toString()

That last pair catches nearly everyone. System.out.println(char[]) prints the characters; every other context prints the array identity. Use new String(arr) or String.valueOf(arr) when you need the text.

Chapter 05

Double, Float & Boolean

You need double for medians, averages, geometry and "closest point" problems. Floating point has three behaviours that break naive code, and all three show up in interviews.

Double — constants & methods

Member — inputsReturns / valueTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Double.MAX_VALUEdouble ≈1.797×10308O(1)Largest finite double.
Double.MIN_VALUE TRAPdouble ≈4.9×10−324O(1)Smallest positive non-zero value — not the most negative double! Unlike Integer.MIN_VALUE. To initialise a maximisation use -Double.MAX_VALUE or Double.NEGATIVE_INFINITY.
Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY HOTdouble ∞O(1)The clean "unreachable" sentinel. Dijkstra distances, minimum-cost DP over doubles — safe because ∞ + x == ∞, no overflow.
Double.NEGATIVE_INFINITYdouble −∞O(1)Maximisation sentinel.
Double.NaN TRAPdoubleO(1)Result of 0.0/0.0, Math.sqrt(-1), ∞ - ∞. NaN == NaN is false — test with Double.isNaN(x).
parseDouble(String s)doubleO(len)Parses decimal or scientific notation. Throws NumberFormatException.
compare(double a, double b) TRAPintO(1)Total ordering, unlike </>: it treats NaN as greater than everything and -0.0 < 0.0. Required inside comparators — (a, b) -> (int)(a - b) is a classic bug that truncates 0.5 to 0.
isNaN(double v)booleanO(1)The only correct NaN test.
isInfinite(double v) / isFinite J8booleanO(1)Overflow detection after a chain of multiplications.
max / min / sum(double,double) J8doubleO(1)Method-reference forms for stream reduction.
doubleToLongBits(double v)longO(1)Raw IEEE-754 bits. Writing a correct hashCode() for a class with double fields.
toString(double d)StringO(1)Shortest representation that round-trips. Prints 1.0E10 for large values — use String.format("%.5f", d) when a judge wants fixed decimals.
▲ The three floating-point rules that break solutions

1 · Never compare doubles with ==. Compare against an epsilon.

static final double EPS = 1e-9;
if (Math.abs(a - b) < EPS) { /* treat as equal */ }
// 0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3  ->  false. It is 0.30000000000000004.

2 · Never cast a difference to int inside a comparator. It truncates toward zero, so any difference in (−1, 1) becomes 0 and your sort silently treats unequal items as equal — which can even throw IllegalArgumentException: Comparison method violates its general contract.

// WRONG
pts.sort((p, q) -> (int)(dist(p) - dist(q)));
// RIGHT
pts.sort((p, q) -> Double.compare(dist(p), dist(q)));
pts.sort(Comparator.comparingDouble(this::dist));   // cleaner still

3 · Avoid double where an integer formulation exists. For "K Closest Points to Origin" compare x*x + y*y as a long, never Math.sqrt. For "average of subarray ≥ K" transform to "sum − K·len ≥ 0" in integers. Exact arithmetic beats epsilon-tuning every time.

Boolean

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
parseBoolean(String s)booleanO(len)Case-insensitive "true" → true, everything else → false. Never throws.
compare(boolean a, boolean b) J7intO(1)false < true. Sorting so that all "false" items come first — partitioning.
logicalAnd / logicalOr / logicalXor(boolean,boolean) J8booleanO(1)Method-reference forms for reduce. stream.reduce(false, Boolean::logicalOr).
Boolean.TRUE / Boolean.FALSEBooleanO(1)The two cached instances. Map<String,Boolean> memo tables where you must distinguish "false" from "not computed" (null).
◆ boolean[] versus Set versus BitSet

For a visited set over indices 0..n-1: use boolean[n]. It is one byte per flag, zero boxing, and the fastest option. Reach for BitSet only when n exceeds a few million (it packs 64 flags per long) or when you need whole-set AND/OR/XOR operations. Reach for HashSet<Integer> only when the index space is sparse or unbounded.

Chapter 06

Math — the complete API

All static. Two entries here — floorMod and floorDiv — solve a bug that appears in every problem involving negative indices or circular arrays, and most candidates have never heard of them.

Core arithmetic

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
abs(int|long|float|double) HOTsame typeO(1)Absolute value. Manhattan distance abs(dx)+abs(dy), "Minimum Absolute Difference", balanced-tree height checks. Trap: returns MIN_VALUE unchanged for MIN_VALUE.
absExact(int|long) J15same typeO(1)Throws ArithmeticException instead of returning a negative. Use when correctness beats silence.
max(a, b) HOTsame typeO(1)Overloaded for int, long, float, double. The single most-called method in dynamic programming: dp[i] = Math.max(dp[i-1], dp[i-2] + v[i]).
min(a, b) HOTsame typeO(1)As above for minimisation DP, edit distance, coin change.
clamp(long v, int lo, int hi) J21int / long / doubleO(1)Constrains a value to a range — replaces Math.max(lo, Math.min(hi, v)). Grid-boundary clamping, cursor positions. Java 21 only; write the nested max/min on Java 17.
floorDiv(int x, int y) HOTint / longO(1)Division that rounds toward negative infinity, unlike / which truncates toward zero. -7/2 == -3 but floorDiv(-7,2) == -4. Needed for grid coordinates that can go negative, and for binary-indexed maths on signed values.
floorMod(int x, int y) HOTint / longO(1)Always returns a result with the sign of the divisor, so for positive y it is always in [0, y). The fix for every circular-array bug: -1 % 5 == -1 (crashes as an index) but floorMod(-1,5) == 4. Circular queues, rotating arrays, clock arithmetic, "Rotate List".
ceilDiv(int x, int y) J18int / longO(1)Rounds toward positive infinity. On Java 17 write (x + y - 1) / y for non-negative values. "How many boats/buses/pages do I need" — Koko Eating Bananas, Capacity to Ship Packages.
pow(double a, double b) TRAPdoubleO(1)Floating-point exponentiation. Do not use for integer powers — (int)Math.pow(10,2) can yield 99 on some platforms. Write a fast-power loop or use BigInteger.pow. Acceptable for Math.pow(2,i) only if you immediately need a double.
sqrt(double a) HOTdoubleO(1)Square root; NaN for negatives. Primality trial division to sqrt(n), geometric distance, "Valid Perfect Square" — but always verify with integer multiplication afterwards: long r = (long)Math.sqrt(n); if (r*r == n) … and check r±1.
cbrt(double a)doubleO(1)Cube root, correct for negatives.
hypot(double x, double y)doubleO(1)√(x²+y²) without intermediate overflow. Euclidean distance in geometry problems.
floor(double a) / ceil(double a)doubleO(1)Round down / up. Both return double — you must cast: (int) Math.ceil(x).
round(double a)longO(1)Round half-up. Note the return type is long for a double input and int for a float input — a frequent compile error.
rint(double a)doubleO(1)Round half-to-even (banker's rounding). rint(2.5)==2.0, rint(3.5)==4.0.
signum(double d)doubleO(1)−1.0 / 0.0 / 1.0. Orientation tests in computational geometry (cross-product sign).
log(double) / log10 / log1p / expdoubleO(1)Natural log, base-10 log, ln(1+x), ex. Number-of-digits via (int)Math.log10(n)+1; base-2 via Math.log(n)/Math.log(2) — but prefer the exact bit trick from Chapter 03.
toRadians / toDegrees(double)doubleO(1)Angle conversion. Geometry / clock-angle problems.
sin / cos / tan / asin / acos / atan / atan2(y,x)doubleO(1)Trigonometry. atan2(y,x) gives the full-circle angle of a point — used for sorting points by polar angle in convex-hull and "Max Points on a Line" problems.
Math.PI / Math.EdoubleO(1)Constants. Circle-area, "Circle and Rectangle Overlapping".
Overflow-checked arithmetic — throws ArithmeticException instead of wrapping silently
addExact(int,int) J8int / longO(1)Throws on overflow. Detecting overflow in "Reverse Integer" / "String to Integer (atoi)" without manual boundary checks — wrap in try/catch and return the clamp value.
subtractExact / multiplyExact / negateExact / incrementExact / decrementExact J8int / longO(1)Same contract for each operation.
toIntExact(long v) J8intO(1)Narrows a long, throwing if it does not fit. Safely returning an int from a long accumulator.
multiplyFull(int x, int y) J9longO(1)int × int → long with no overflow possible. Cleaner and more obvious than (long) x * y.
multiplyHigh(long,long) J9longO(1)Upper 64 bits of a 128-bit product. Montgomery / Barrett modular multiplication in competitive programming.
random()double ∈ [0,1)O(1)Convenience RNG. Randomised quickselect pivots, reservoir sampling. Prefer a Random instance (Chapter 38) when you need nextInt(bound).
◆ Math.floorMod — the one-line fix for circular arrays
// The bug: Java's % keeps the sign of the DIVIDEND.
-1 % 5                      // -1  -> ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException when used as an index
7 % 5                       // 2   -> fine

// Every textbook workaround:
int i = ((cur - 1) % n + n) % n;      // works, ugly, easy to typo
// The library answer:
int i = Math.floorMod(cur - 1, n);      // always in [0, n)

// Real uses:
prev = Math.floorMod(i - 1, n);            // circular array neighbour
int hour = Math.floorMod(h, 12);            // clock arithmetic with negative offsets
grid[Math.floorMod(r, R)][Math.floorMod(c, C)];  // wrap-around torus grid
int shift = Math.floorMod(k, len);          // normalise a rotation amount, k may be huge or negative

Number-theory helpers you must write yourself

Java's standard library has no gcd for primitives, no modular exponentiation, and no sieve. These four are non-negotiable memorisation.

// GCD — Euclid, iterative. O(log min(a,b))
static int gcd(int a, int b) { while (b != 0) { int t = a % b; a = b; b = t; } return a; }
// (BigInteger.valueOf(a).gcd(BigInteger.valueOf(b)).intValue() also works but allocates.)

// LCM — divide first to avoid overflow
static long lcm(long a, long b) { return a / gcd(a, b) * b; }

// Fast modular exponentiation — O(log e). Needed whenever a problem says "mod 1e9+7".
static final int MOD = 1_000_000_007;
static long power(long b, long e, long m) {
    long r = 1; b %= m;
    while (e > 0) {
        if ((e & 1) == 1) r = r * b % m;
        b = b * b % m;
        e >>= 1;
    }
    return r;
}
// Modular inverse when m is prime (Fermat): inv(a) = power(a, m-2, m)

// Sieve of Eratosthenes — all primes below n in O(n log log n)
static boolean[] sieve(int n) {
    boolean[] composite = new boolean[n + 1];
    for (int p = 2; (long) p * p <= n; p++)
        if (!composite[p])
            for (int q = p * p; q <= n; q += p) composite[q] = true;
    return composite;   // !composite[i] && i >= 2  means i is prime
}
Chapter 07

Bit manipulation cookbook

Bit tricks are not optional trivia — an entire family of problems (subset enumeration, bitmask DP, "Single Number", "Sum of Two Integers", travelling-salesman on ≤20 nodes) is unsolvable in the time limit without them. This chapter is every operator and every idiom in one place.

The operators

OperatorNameBehaviour & DSA use
a & bAND1 only where both are 1. Masking, testing a bit, intersecting two sets, x & (x-1) tricks.
a | bOR1 where either is 1. Setting a bit, union of two sets.
a ^ bXOR1 where they differ. Toggling, pairing-cancellation ("Single Number"), swapping without temp, Nim-game Grundy values.
~aNOTFlips all bits. ~a == -a - 1. Clearing a bit via a & ~mask; Arrays.binarySearch returns ~insertionPoint on a miss.
a << kLeft shiftMultiply by 2k. Building masks: 1 << i. Trap: 1 << 35 uses only the low 5 bits of the shift for ints — write 1L << 35.
a >> kArithmetic right shiftDivide by 2k, sign-extending. -8 >> 1 == -4.
a >>> kLogical right shiftShifts in zeros regardless of sign. -8 >>> 1 is a huge positive. Safe binary-search midpoint (lo+hi) >>> 1; iterating the bits of a possibly-negative int.
▲ Two shift traps

Shift distances are taken modulo the operand width. For an int only the low 5 bits of k are used, so 1 << 32 == 1, not 0. For a long, the low 6 bits. Always write 1L << k when k can reach 32 or more.

Shift has lower precedence than + and -. 1 << n - 1 parses as 1 << (n-1), which is usually what you wanted — but x & 1 == 0 parses as x & (1 == 0) and will not compile. Parenthesise every bit expression: (x & 1) == 0.

Single-bit operations on a mask

GoalExpressionNotes
Test whether bit i is set(mask >> i & 1) == 1Or (mask & (1 << i)) != 0. The shift-right form avoids overflow at bit 31.
Set bit imask |= 1 << iIdempotent.
Clear bit imask &= ~(1 << i)Idempotent.
Toggle bit imask ^= 1 << iApplying twice restores the original.
Extract lowest set bit (value)mask & -maskSame as Integer.lowestOneBit. Core of a Fenwick tree.
Remove lowest set bitmask & (mask - 1)Loop on this to visit set bits in O(popcount) rather than O(32).
Index of lowest set bitInteger.numberOfTrailingZeros(mask)O(1) hardware instruction.
Is it a power of two?n > 0 && (n & (n - 1)) == 0The n > 0 guard is mandatory — without it, 0 and MIN_VALUE pass.
Is it a power of four?n > 0 && (n & (n-1)) == 0 && (n & 0x55555555) != 0Power of two whose set bit is at an even position.
Set all bits below i(1 << i) - 1The full mask for an i-element universe.
Clear all bits at/above imask & ((1 << i) - 1)Truncate to i bits.
Even / odd test(n & 1) == 0Correct for negatives, unlike n % 2 == 0… which is also correct, but n % 2 == 1 is false for negative odd numbers. Use the bit test.
Complement within k bitsmask ^ ((1 << k) - 1)"Complement of Base 10 Integer".
Swap two ints without a tempa^=b; b^=a; a^=b;Interview curiosity. Fails if a and b alias the same slot.
Absolute value, branch-free(n ^ (n >> 31)) - (n >> 31)Curiosity; Math.abs compiles to the same thing.

Subset enumeration — the bitmask DP engine

// Iterate ALL 2^n subsets of an n-element set. n must be <= ~22 for this to finish.
for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << n); mask++) {
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if ((mask >> i & 1) == 1) { /* element i is in this subset */ }
}

// Faster: visit only the SET bits, O(popcount) per mask instead of O(n)
for (int m = mask; m != 0; m &= m - 1) {
    int i = Integer.numberOfTrailingZeros(m);   // index of next set bit
    // ... use i
}

// Iterate all SUBMASKS of a given mask, descending, in O(3^n) total across all masks.
// This is the classic "sum over subsets" trick.
for (int sub = mask; ; sub = (sub - 1) & mask) {
    // ... use sub
    if (sub == 0) break;
}

// Complement of a submask, within the universe of n elements
int full = (1 << n) - 1;
int other = full ^ sub;

// Travelling Salesman / "Shortest Path Visiting All Nodes" skeleton
int[][] dp = new int[1 << n][n];
for (int[] row : dp) Arrays.fill(row, Integer.MAX_VALUE / 2);
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dp[1 << i][i] = 0;
for (int mask = 1; mask < (1 << n); mask++)
  for (int last = 0; last < n; last++) {
    if ((mask >> last & 1) == 0 || dp[mask][last] >= Integer.MAX_VALUE / 2) continue;
    for (int nxt = 0; nxt < n; nxt++) {
      if ((mask >> nxt & 1) == 1) continue;
      int nm = mask | (1 << nxt);
      dp[nm][nxt] = Math.min(dp[nm][nxt], dp[mask][last] + cost[last][nxt]);
    }
  }

XOR identities — memorise these five

IdentityConsequence you exploit
a ^ a == 0Duplicates cancel. XOR the whole array → the one unpaired element. "Single Number".
a ^ 0 == a0 is the identity, so it is the correct accumulator seed.
a ^ b ^ b == aXOR is its own inverse. Encode/decode, "Find the Original Array of Prefix Xor", recovering a missing number.
XOR is commutative & associativeOrder does not matter, so you can XOR a whole array in any traversal order — and prefix-XOR works exactly like prefix-sum: xor(l..r) = pre[r+1] ^ pre[l].
a + b == (a ^ b) + 2*(a & b)Sum decomposes into "differing bits" plus "carry bits". This is how you add two numbers without + ("Sum of Two Integers").
 
// Single Number — every element appears twice except one. O(n) time, O(1) space.
int x = 0;
for (int v : nums) x ^= v;
return x;

// Missing Number in 0..n
int x = n;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) x ^= i ^ nums[i];
return x;

// Single Number III — exactly two elements appear once. Split by a differing bit.
int xorAll = 0;
for (int v : nums) xorAll ^= v;
int bit = xorAll & -xorAll;         // lowest bit where the two answers differ
int a = 0, b = 0;
for (int v : nums) { if ((v & bit) != 0) a ^= v; else b ^= v; }
return new int[]{a, b};

// Addition without + or -, using carry propagation
int getSum(int a, int b) {
    while (b != 0) { int carry = (a & b) << 1; a = a ^ b; b = carry; }
    return a;
}
◆ Choosing your mask type

int holds a 32-element universe, long holds 64, BitSet holds as many as you like. In interviews, n ≤ 20 in the constraints is a deliberate signal that the intended solution is bitmask DP over 2n states — 220 ≈ one million, which runs instantly. If you see n ≤ 20, stop looking for a greedy solution.

Chapter 08

String — the complete API

A String is an immutable wrapper around a private byte array. Every method that appears to modify it actually allocates a new object. Internalising that one fact explains every performance trap in this chapter — and explains why StringBuilder exists.

◆ The immutability consequence you must never forget
// O(n^2) — allocates and copies a whole new String on every iteration.
// With n = 100,000 this is ~5 billion char copies and will time out.
String s = "";
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) s += arr[i];     // NEVER DO THIS IN A LOOP

// O(n) — one growable buffer, amortised appends.
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sb.append(arr[i]);
String s = sb.toString();

A single a + b outside a loop is fine — the compiler turns it into a StringBuilder (or an invokedynamic string-concat call on modern JVMs) automatically. The killer is concatenation inside a loop, where a fresh builder is created on every iteration.

Construction & identity

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
new String(char[] value) HOTStringO(n)Builds a String from a char array — copies it, so later mutations of the array do not leak through. The standard finish to any char[]-based algorithm: sort chars → new String(arr) for an anagram key; reverse in place → new String(arr).
new String(char[] v, int offset, int count)StringO(count)Sub-range without an intermediate copy. Extracting a window from a working buffer.
String.valueOf(char[] a) HOTStringO(n)Identical result to new String(a). Also overloaded for int, long, char, double, boolean, Object — the null-safe way to stringify anything (valueOf((Object)null) gives "null" rather than throwing).
String.copyValueOf(char[] a)StringO(n)Legacy synonym of valueOf(char[]). LEGACY
intern()StringO(n)Returns the canonical pooled instance. Almost never needed in DSA; occasionally used to make == work for heavy repeated-key comparison. Understand it for interviews, do not use it in solutions.
hashCode()intO(n) first call, then O(1)Polynomial hash s[0]*31^(n-1) + … + s[n-1], cached after the first computation. Worth knowing because rolling-hash / Rabin-Karp problems ask you to reimplement exactly this.
String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDERComparator<String>—Ready-made case-insensitive comparator. list.sort(String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER).

Inspection & access

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
length() HOTintO(1)Number of char units (not code points). It is a method — s.length() — while an array uses the field arr.length. Mixing these up is the single most common Java compile error in interviews.
charAt(int index) HOTcharO(1)Character at an index; throws StringIndexOutOfBoundsException outside [0, length). The workhorse of every two-pointer and sliding-window string solution.
isEmpty()booleanO(1)length() == 0.
isBlank() J11booleanO(n)Empty or only whitespace. Filtering blank tokens after a split — Arrays.stream(s.split(" ")).filter(t -> !t.isBlank()).
indexOf(int ch) HOTint, or −1O(n)First index of a character. Note the parameter is int (a code point), so indexOf('a') works via widening.
indexOf(String str)int, or −1O(n·m)First index of a substring — naive scan, not KMP. Fine for short needles. If an interviewer asks for O(n+m), they want you to write KMP by hand.
indexOf(String str, int from)int, or −1O(n·m)Search starting at an offset. Counting all non-overlapping occurrences in a while loop.
lastIndexOf(int|String [, int from])int, or −1O(n)Rightmost match, searching backwards. "Length of Last Word", file-extension splitting.
contains(CharSequence s) HOTbooleanO(n·m)indexOf(s) != -1, more readable. Substring membership; "Repeated Substring Pattern" via (s+s).substring(1, 2n-1).contains(s).
startsWith(String p) / startsWith(String p, int off)booleanO(m)Prefix test, optionally at an offset. Word-break DP: if (s.startsWith(word, i)) avoids allocating a substring — significantly faster than s.substring(i).startsWith(word).
endsWith(String suffix)booleanO(m)Suffix test.
regionMatches(int off, String o, int ooff, int len)booleanO(len)Compares two sub-ranges without allocating. An overload takes a leading boolean ignoreCase. Zero-garbage substring comparison in hot loops — the professional alternative to s.substring(a,b).equals(t.substring(c,d)).
contentEquals(CharSequence cs)booleanO(n)Compares against a StringBuilder or other CharSequence without calling toString(). s.contentEquals(sb) instead of s.equals(sb.toString()).

Comparison

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
equals(Object o) HOTbooleanO(n)Character-by-character equality. Always use this, never == — == compares references and only appears to work for compile-time literals thanks to the string pool.
equalsIgnoreCase(String s)booleanO(n)Case-insensitive equality without allocating lowercase copies.
compareTo(String s) HOTintO(n)Lexicographic ordering: negative / 0 / positive. The value is the char difference at the first mismatch, or the length difference if one is a prefix of the other. Sorting strings, "Largest Number" custom comparator, finding the lexicographically smallest rotation.
compareToIgnoreCase(String s)intO(n)Case-folded ordering.
matches(String regex)booleanO(regex)Whole-string regex match (implicitly anchored at both ends). Input validation problems: "Valid Number", "Valid IP Address". Recompiles the pattern every call — hoist a Pattern out of the loop if it is hot.

Extraction & transformation all return NEW strings

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
substring(int begin) HOTStringO(n−begin)From begin to the end. s.substring(s.length()) legally returns "".
substring(int begin, int end) TRAPStringO(end−begin)begin inclusive, end exclusive. Length of the result is exactly end - begin. Palindromic-substring problems, "Decode String", word extraction. Since Java 7 this copies (it no longer shares the backing array), so calling it inside a loop is O(n²) — prefer index pairs or regionMatches in hot paths.
subSequence(int b, int e)CharSequenceO(e−b)Interface-typed synonym of substring. Rarely used directly.
toCharArray() HOTchar[]O(n)A fresh mutable copy of the characters. The gateway to in-place algorithms: reverse, sort for anagrams, two-pointer swaps, and faster iteration than repeated charAt in tight loops.
getBytes() / getBytes(Charset)byte[]O(n)Encoded bytes. Only for encode/decode problems; use StandardCharsets.UTF_8 explicitly.
toUpperCase() / toLowerCase() HOTStringO(n)Case-folds the whole string using the default locale. Normalising before comparison. Pass Locale.ROOT if you want to avoid the Turkish dotless-i surprise.
trim()StringO(n)Removes leading/trailing characters with code ≤ U+0020. Cleaning judge input.
strip() J11StringO(n)Unicode-aware trim — also removes ideographic spaces. Prefer this over trim on Java 11+. Siblings: stripLeading(), stripTrailing().
replace(char old, char nw) HOTStringO(n)Replaces every occurrence of a character. Not a regex — literal.
replace(CharSequence t, CharSequence r)StringO(n·m)Literal substring replacement — safe with ., *, ( and other metacharacters. Use this, not replaceAll, unless you genuinely need a pattern.
replaceAll(String regex, String repl) TRAPStringO(regex)Regex replacement. Both arguments are special: the first is a pattern, and in the second $1 is a group reference and \ is an escape. Stripping non-alphanumerics: s.replaceAll("[^a-zA-Z0-9]", "") — the canonical "Valid Palindrome" one-liner.
replaceFirst(String regex, String r)StringO(regex)Replaces only the first match. Stripping leading zeros: s.replaceFirst("^0+(?!$)", "").
concat(String s)StringO(n+m)Same as + but throws on null instead of producing "null".
repeat(int count) J11StringO(n·count)Concatenates the string with itself. Building test input, padding: "0".repeat(k) + s, drawing grid borders. Replaces awkward StringBuilder loops.
indent(int n) J12StringO(n)Prefixes every line with spaces. Pretty-printing a tree for debugging.
transform(Function<String,R> f) J12RO(f)Applies a function to the string, enabling fluent chains: s.transform(String::trim).transform(Integer::parseInt).
formatted(Object... args) J15StringO(n)Instance form of String.format: "%d-%d".formatted(a, b). Compact composite map keys.
String.format(String fmt, Object... args) HOTStringO(n)printf-style formatting. Building composite keys String.format("%d,%d", r, c), fixed-decimal output %.5f, zero-padding %03d. Slow — avoid in inner loops; string concatenation of ints is 10× faster.

Splitting & joining

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
split(String regex) TRAPString[]O(n)Splits on a regex, and discards trailing empty strings. Tokenising sentences, parsing CSV, version comparison. See the trap box below — this method has three separate gotchas.
split(String regex, int limit)String[]O(n)limit > 0: at most that many pieces, the last holding the remainder. limit == -1: keeps trailing empty strings. limit == 0: default behaviour. split(",", -1) is what you want for CSV with empty fields.
splitWithDelimiters(String rx, int lim) J21String[]O(n)Returns tokens and the separators that split them, interleaved. Expression parsing — "3+4*2".splitWithDelimiters("[-+*/]", -1) yields numbers and operators in order, which is exactly what a calculator problem needs. Huge quality-of-life win on Java 21.
String.join(CharSequence d, CharSequence... el) HOTStringO(total)Joins with a delimiter, no trailing separator. Serialising a path or a list of words. Second overload takes an Iterable<? extends CharSequence>, so String.join("->", listOfStrings) works directly.
lines() J11Stream<String>O(n) lazySplits on line terminators, lazily and without a regex. Processing multi-line input or text blocks.
chars() J8IntStreamO(n) lazyStream of char values widened to int. One-liner frequency maps and filters: s.chars().filter(Character::isDigit).count(). Remember the elements are int — cast with (char) c to print them.
codePoints() J8IntStreamO(n) lazyLike chars() but combines surrogate pairs into single code points. Correct for emoji.
▲ split — three gotchas in one method

1 · The argument is a regex, not a literal. Splitting on a dot or a pipe silently does the wrong thing.

"a.b.c".split(".")      // [] — "." matches EVERY character
"a.b.c".split("\\.")    // [a, b, c] — escaped, correct
"a|b".split("|")        // [a, |, b] — "|" is alternation
"a|b".split("\\|")      // [a, b]
Pattern.quote(".")         // "\Q.\E" — programmatic escaping of any literal

2 · Trailing empty strings vanish; leading ones do not.

"a,b,,,".split(",").length      // 2  — trailing empties dropped
"a,b,,,".split(",", -1).length  // 5  — all kept
",,a,b".split(",").length      // 4  — leading empties ALWAYS kept: ["", "", "a", "b"]

3 · Splitting on a single space breaks on multiple spaces. Use \\s+, and strip() first so you do not get a leading empty token.

"hello   world".split(" ")        // [hello, , , world] — three tokens of junk
"hello   world".split("\\s+")     // [hello, world]
"  hi there".split("\\s+")        // ["", hi, there] — note the leading ""
"  hi there".strip().split("\\s+") // [hi, there] — the correct idiom
▲ == versus equals and the string pool
String a = "hello";
String b = "hello";
a == b                          // true  — both are the same pooled literal

String c = new String("hello");
a == c                          // FALSE — c is a distinct heap object
a.equals(c)                     // true

String d = "hel" + "lo";            // folded at COMPILE time into the pooled literal
a == d                          // true

String part = "hel";
String e = part + "lo";            // computed at RUNTIME -> new object
a == e                          // FALSE — this is the one that burns people
a == e.intern()                  // true

The lesson is mechanical: never use == on strings. It will pass your hand-written test with literals and fail the judge with computed input.

Idiom gallery the string manipulations that recur in every interview

// Reverse a string
String r = new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString();

// Palindrome check, two pointers, no allocation
boolean isPal(String s, int i, int j) {
    while (i < j) if (s.charAt(i++) != s.charAt(j--)) return false;
    return true;
}

// Palindrome ignoring case and punctuation (LeetCode 125)
int i = 0, j = s.length() - 1;
while (i < j) {
    while (i < j && !Character.isLetterOrDigit(s.charAt(i))) i++;
    while (i < j && !Character.isLetterOrDigit(s.charAt(j))) j--;
    if (Character.toLowerCase(s.charAt(i++)) != Character.toLowerCase(s.charAt(j--))) return false;
}

// Anagram key by sorting — O(n log n) per word
char[] a = s.toCharArray(); Arrays.sort(a);
String key = new String(a);

// Anagram key by counting — O(n) per word, faster for "Group Anagrams"
int[] cnt = new int[26];
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) cnt[c - 'a']++;
String key = Arrays.toString(cnt);     // or build a compact "a2b1c3" signature

// Character frequency map, three equivalent ways
Map<Character,Integer> f1 = new HashMap<>();
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) f1.merge(c, 1, Integer::sum);

Map<Character,Long> f2 = s.chars()
    .mapToObj(c -> (char) c)
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, Collectors.counting()));

int[] f3 = new int[26];              // fastest — use this when input is lowercase a-z
for (char c : s.toCharArray()) f3[c - 'a']++;

// Count occurrences of a substring (non-overlapping)
int count = 0, idx = 0;
while ((idx = s.indexOf(sub, idx)) != -1) { count++; idx += sub.length(); }

// Capitalise each word
String title = Arrays.stream(s.split("\\s+"))
    .map(w -> Character.toUpperCase(w.charAt(0)) + w.substring(1).toLowerCase())
    .collect(Collectors.joining(" "));

// Left-pad with zeros to width 5
String p1 = String.format("%05d", n);
String p2 = "0".repeat(Math.max(0, 5 - s.length())) + s;   // J11, faster

// Longest common prefix of an array of strings
String pre = strs[0];
for (String t : strs) { while (!t.startsWith(pre)) pre = pre.substring(0, pre.length() - 1); }
Chapter 09

StringBuilder — the complete API

The mutable counterpart to String and one of the six APIs you must know cold. Every method that changes the buffer returns this, which is why chaining works. StringBuffer is the identical but synchronised version — never use it in single-threaded interview code.

Construction

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
new StringBuilder() HOTStringBuilderO(1)Empty buffer, capacity 16.
new StringBuilder(int capacity)StringBuilderO(1)Pre-sized buffer. Pass the known final length to avoid every intermediate resize — a real constant-factor win when building megabyte-scale output.
new StringBuilder(String s) HOTStringBuilderO(n)Seeded with existing text. new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString() is the one-line string reverse.
new StringBuilder(CharSequence cs)StringBuilderO(n)Accepts another builder or any CharSequence.

Mutation

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
append(String|char|int|long|double|float|boolean|char[]|Object|CharSequence) HOTStringBuilder (this)O(k)*Appends at the end. Overloaded for every primitive plus Object (which calls String.valueOf, so null becomes "null"). The universal accumulator: backtracking output, tree serialisation, run-length encoding, joining results.
append(char[] str, int off, int len)thisO(len)Appends a sub-range of a char array without an intermediate String.
append(CharSequence cs, int start, int end)thisO(end−start)Appends a substring with zero allocation. Strictly better than append(s.substring(a,b)) in a loop.
appendCodePoint(int cp)thisO(1)*Appends a code point, expanding to a surrogate pair if needed.
insert(int offset, X) HOTthisO(n)Inserts before the given index; same overload family as append. O(n) because it shifts the tail. insert(0, c) builds a string front-to-back — used in "Integer to Roman", base conversion, and digit-by-digit construction. For long strings prefer appending then reverse(), which is O(n) total instead of O(n²).
deleteCharAt(int index) HOTthisO(n)Removes one character. The un-choose step in backtracking: sb.append(c); dfs(...); sb.deleteCharAt(sb.length()-1);
delete(int start, int end)thisO(n)Removes [start, end). end may exceed the length. Trimming a trailing delimiter: sb.delete(sb.length()-2, sb.length()).
replace(int s, int e, String str)thisO(n)Swaps a range for new text of any length.
setCharAt(int i, char c) HOTvoidO(1)In-place single-character write. Returns void, so it does not chain. In-place string mutation without converting to char[].
reverse() HOTthisO(n)Reverses in place, surrogate-pair aware. String reversal, "Reverse Words in a String", palindrome construction, digit reversal.
setLength(int n) HOTvoidO(1) to shrinkTruncates or zero-pads. sb.setLength(0) is the fast buffer reset — reuse one builder across many iterations instead of allocating a new one. Also the cleanest backtracking undo when you appended several characters: remember the old length, restore it.
repeat(CharSequence cs, int n) J21thisO(n·len)Appends a sequence repeatedly. Also repeat(int codePoint, int count). Padding and separator lines in one call.

Inspection & output

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
length() HOTintO(1)Current character count. Used in every backtracking undo: sb.deleteCharAt(sb.length()-1).
charAt(int i)charO(1)Read a character.
isEmpty() J15booleanO(1)length() == 0. On Java 17+ this exists; before that compare the length.
toString() HOTStringO(n)Copies the buffer into a new immutable String. Call it once at the end — calling it inside a loop reintroduces the O(n²) you were avoiding.
substring(int s [, int e])StringO(len)Extracts text without disturbing the buffer.
indexOf(String s [, int from]) / lastIndexOfint, or −1O(n·m)Search inside the buffer without converting to String.
capacity() / ensureCapacity(int) / trimToSize()int / void / voidO(1) / O(n) / O(n)Buffer-size management. Growth doubles-plus-two. Only matters for very large outputs.
chars() / codePoints()IntStreamO(n) lazyStream the buffer contents.
compareTo(StringBuilder o) J11intO(n)Lexicographic comparison of two builders without toString().
◆ The backtracking-with-StringBuilder pattern

This shape appears in "Generate Parentheses", "Letter Combinations of a Phone Number", "Palindrome Partitioning", "Word Search", "Restore IP Addresses" and dozens more. Learn it once.

void dfs(int pos, StringBuilder sb, List<String> out) {
    if (pos == n) { out.add(sb.toString()); return; }   // snapshot ONLY at a leaf
    for (char c : choicesAt(pos)) {
        sb.append(c);                      // choose
        dfs(pos + 1, sb, out);              // explore
        sb.deleteCharAt(sb.length() - 1);   // UN-CHOOSE — forgetting this is the #1 bug
    }
}

// When a choice appends MORE than one character, restore by length instead:
int mark = sb.length();
sb.append(segment);
dfs(...);
sb.setLength(mark);                    // undoes any number of appends

The same two rules apply to a List accumulator: snapshot with new ArrayList<>(path) at the leaf (never add the live list — every entry would alias the same object and end up empty), and undo with path.remove(path.size()-1).

StringBuilder vs StringBuffer vs String

AspectStringStringBuilderStringBuffer
MutableNoYesYes
Thread-safeYes (immutable)NoYes (synchronised)
SpeedSlow for buildingFastest~10–20% slower than Builder
Since1.01.51.0
Use in interviewsFinal results, map keysAlways this oneOnly if asked about thread safety
Chapter 10

char[] — the fast path

When a problem is character-heavy and the input is large, converting to char[] once and working on the array is measurably faster than repeated charAt calls, and it is the only way to mutate in place. This is also the representation interviewers use in the classic "do it in O(1) extra space" follow-up.

Crossing between String and char[]

Direction — expressionReturnsTimeNotes
s.toCharArray()char[]O(n)String → fresh mutable array.
new String(arr)StringO(n)Array → String, copying.
String.valueOf(arr)StringO(n)Identical; some prefer it for symmetry with other valueOf calls.
new String(arr, off, len)StringO(len)Array sub-range → String.
Arrays.sort(arr)voidO(n log n)Dual-pivot quicksort on the chars. Anagram canonicalisation.
Arrays.fill(arr, c)voidO(n)Initialising a board of '.' in N-Queens.
Arrays.equals(a, b)booleanO(n)Element-wise. Comparing two frequency signatures.
String.valueOf(c) / Character.toString(c)StringO(1)Single char → String.
new String(new char[]{a, b})StringO(1)Two chars → a 2-character String. Building pair keys.

In-place algorithms on char[]

// Reverse in place — O(1) extra space
void reverse(char[] a, int i, int j) {
    while (i < j) { char t = a[i]; a[i++] = a[j]; a[j--] = t; }
}

// "Reverse Words in a String II" — reverse whole, then reverse each word. O(1) space.
reverse(a, 0, a.length - 1);
int start = 0;
for (int i = 0; i <= a.length; i++)
    if (i == a.length || a[i] == ' ') { reverse(a, start, i - 1); start = i + 1; }

// Counting sort of characters — O(n + 26), beats Arrays.sort for lowercase input
int[] cnt = new int[26];
for (char c : a) cnt[c - 'a']++;
int k = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < 26; i++)
    while (cnt[i]-- > 0) a[k++] = (char)('a' + i);

// 2-D grid of characters (the shape of nearly every matrix problem)
char[][] grid = new char[R][C];
for (char[] row : grid) Arrays.fill(row, '.');
// grid[r][c] — row first, then column. Always write R/C not m/n to stay oriented.

// Reading a grid from String[] input — the LeetCode convention
char[][] g = new char[board.length][];
for (int r = 0; r < board.length; r++) g[r] = board[r].toCharArray();

// In-place "visited" marking on a grid — saves a whole boolean[][]
char saved = grid[r][c];
grid[r][c] = '#';          // mark
dfs(grid, r + 1, c);
grid[r][c] = saved;        // restore (only needed for backtracking, not flood fill)
◆ When char[] is worth it
  • Yes: the algorithm mutates characters; you iterate the same string many times; the interviewer asked for O(1) extra space; you need Arrays.sort.
  • No: you only read each character once (charAt in a single pass is already fast and avoids an O(n) copy); you need substring semantics; the string is tiny.
Chapter 11

Regex & tokenising

Regex is rarely the intended solution to a DSA problem, but it is the fastest way to clean input, and a handful of problems ("Valid Number", "Regular Expression Matching" as a contrast case, log parsing) are about it directly. Know these, and know when to avoid them.

Pattern & Matcher

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Pattern.compile(String regex)PatternO(regex)Compiles once. Hoist this to a static final field if you use it in a loop — String.matches/replaceAll recompile on every call and can dominate the runtime.
Pattern.compile(String rx, int flags)PatternO(regex)Flags: CASE_INSENSITIVE, MULTILINE, DOTALL.
Pattern.matcher(CharSequence in)MatcherO(1)Creates a stateful matcher over the input.
Pattern.quote(String literal) HOTStringO(n)Escapes every metacharacter. The safe way to split or replace on user-supplied text: s.split(Pattern.quote(delim)).
Pattern.split(CharSequence in [, int limit])String[]O(n)Same semantics as String.split, but with the pattern compiled once.
Matcher.matches()booleanO(regex)Must match the entire input.
Matcher.find()booleanO(regex)Finds the next match anywhere; call in a while loop to iterate all matches. Extracting all numbers from a string.
Matcher.group() / group(int n) / group(String name)StringO(len)Whole match, or the n-th capture group (1-indexed; group 0 is the whole match), or a named group (?<name>…).
Matcher.start() / end()intO(1)Index bounds of the last match — end is exclusive. Locating tokens for a follow-up scan.
Matcher.groupCount()intO(1)Number of capture groups in the pattern.
Matcher.replaceAll(String r) / replaceFirstStringO(n)Replacement with $1 group references.
Matcher.results() J9Stream<MatchResult>O(n) lazyAll matches as a stream. m.results().map(MatchResult::group).toList() — the cleanest "extract all tokens".
Matcher.reset() / reset(CharSequence)MatcherO(1)Reuse one matcher across many inputs — avoids re-allocation in hot loops.

The patterns worth memorising

RegexMatchesTypical use
\\s+One or more whitespaceWord splitting. Always pair with strip().
\\d+One or more digitsExtracting numbers from mixed text.
[^a-zA-Z0-9]Any non-alphanumericreplaceAll(…, "") to clean before a palindrome check.
[^a-z]Non-lowercaseSame, after toLowerCase().
\\W+Non-word charactersShorthand for [^a-zA-Z0-9_] — note it keeps underscores.
\\.A literal dotSplitting version strings and IPs.
^0+(?!$)Leading zeros, but not the last oneNormalising "0012" → "12" while keeping "0" as "0".
(.)\\1+A character repeatedRun-length detection / "String Compression".
\\bWord boundaryWhole-word matching.
[-+*/]An arithmetic operatorExpression tokenising. Put - first or last inside [] so it is literal.
 
// Extract every integer from a messy string
static final Pattern NUM = Pattern.compile("-?\\d+");
List<Integer> nums = NUM.matcher(s).results()
    .map(MatchResult::group).map(Integer::parseInt).toList();

// Tokenise an arithmetic expression, keeping the operators (J21)
String[] parts = "12+7*3".splitWithDelimiters("[-+*/]", -1);
// -> ["12", "+", "7", "*", "3"]

// Java 17 equivalent, hand-rolled — and honestly what an interviewer wants to see
List<String> toks = new ArrayList<>();
int i = 0;
while (i < s.length()) {
    char c = s.charAt(i);
    if (Character.isDigit(c)) {
        int j = i;
        while (j < s.length() && Character.isDigit(s.charAt(j))) j++;
        toks.add(s.substring(i, j)); i = j;
    } else if (!Character.isWhitespace(c)) {
        toks.add(String.valueOf(c)); i++;
    } else i++;
}
▲ Catastrophic backtracking

Nested quantifiers such as (a+)+b can take exponential time on an input of all as. If a regex-based solution suddenly times out on a long input, this is why. Rewrite as a hand-rolled scan — which is what the problem wanted anyway.

Also: String.matches, replaceAll, replaceFirst and split all recompile the pattern on every invocation. Calling any of them inside an O(n) loop turns your solution into O(n·regex-compile), which is a common and invisible cause of TLE.

Chapter 12

Array language rules

Arrays are the fastest container in Java and the one you should default to whenever the size is known. They are also the only container with language-level syntax, which brings a handful of rules that do not apply to anything else.

Declaration & initialisation

// Five equivalent-ish declarations
int[] a = new int[10];              // 10 zeros
int[] b = {1, 2, 3};                  // only valid at the declaration site
int[] c = new int[]{1, 2, 3};         // valid anywhere, e.g. as an argument or a return
int d[] = new int[10];              // legal C-style, but never write this
var e = new int[10];                // J10+ inference — fine, e is int[]

return new int[]{lo, hi};           // how you return a pair of ints from a method

// Default values — you never need to zero-fill manually
int[]/long[]/short[]/byte[]   -> 0
double[]/float[]             -> 0.0
char[]                      -> '\u0000'  (prints as a blank, is NOT ' ')
boolean[]                   -> false
Object[] / Integer[] / String[] -> null

// length is a FIELD on arrays, a METHOD on String and on collections
arr.length        // array      — no parentheses
str.length()      // String     — parentheses
list.size()       // Collection — different name entirely

Copying — four ways, and when to use each

ExpressionReturnsTimeWhen to use it
arr.clone()same array typeO(n)Shortest full copy of a 1-D array. Snapshotting a row during backtracking. Shallow for 2-D — grid.clone() copies the row references, so mutating copy[0][0] also changes grid[0][0].
Arrays.copyOf(arr, newLen) HOTnew arrayO(newLen)Copy with resize — truncates or zero-pads. Manual dynamic-array growth; returning a right-sized result from an oversized buffer: return Arrays.copyOf(res, k);
Arrays.copyOfRange(arr, from, to) HOTnew arrayO(to−from)Sub-array, from inclusive and to exclusive. Splitting an array for merge sort or divide-and-conquer.
System.arraycopy(src, sPos, dst, dPos, len)voidO(len)The fastest copy — an intrinsic that compiles to a memory move. Works even when source and destination overlap. Shifting elements in place, merging two sorted halves, implementing your own ArrayList.
▲ 2-D "copies" are shallow
int[][] grid = {{1,2},{3,4}};
int[][] shallow = grid.clone();        // or Arrays.copyOf(grid, grid.length)
shallow[0][0] = 99;
grid[0][0]                             // 99 — they share the same row arrays!

// Deep copy — you must copy each row
int[][] deep = new int[grid.length][];
for (int r = 0; r < grid.length; r++) deep[r] = grid[r].clone();
// Or, in one expression:
int[][] deep2 = Arrays.stream(grid).map(int[]::clone).toArray(int[][]::new);

This bites hardest in DP problems where you save a "best state so far" and it silently mutates underneath you.

▲ Array covariance and ArrayStoreException
Object[] objs = new String[3];    // legal — arrays are covariant
objs[0] = Integer.valueOf(1);     // compiles, but throws ArrayStoreException at RUNTIME

// And you cannot create an array of a generic type:
// List<Integer>[] bad = new List<Integer>[10];   // compile error
List<Integer>[] adj = new List[n];       // the standard workaround: raw type + warning
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj[i] = new ArrayList<>();
// This is THE idiom for graph adjacency lists — see Chapter 52.

Iteration forms

// Indexed — use when you need i, or when you mutate
for (int i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) arr[i] *= 2;

// Enhanced for — read-only. Assigning to the loop variable does NOTHING to the array.
for (int v : arr) v *= 2;            // no effect on arr — a classic beginner bug

// Enhanced for over rows of a 2-D array — the row IS a reference, so this DOES mutate
for (int[] row : grid) Arrays.fill(row, -1);   // works, and is the standard memo init

// Backwards — for in-place shifting, or right-to-left DP
for (int i = arr.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) { ... }

// Two arrays in lockstep
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) merged[i] = a[i] + b[i];
Chapter 13

java.util.Arrays — the complete API

All static. Every method here is overloaded for int[] long[] double[] float[] char[] byte[] short[] boolean[] and Object[] unless noted. Four of them — sort, fill, binarySearch, toString — are in the permanent top tier of interview APIs.

Sorting

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
sort(int[] a) HOTvoid (in place)O(n log n)Dual-pivot quicksort for primitives — unstable, but stability is meaningless for primitives. Ascending only. There is no descending overload for primitive arrays — see the trap box.
sort(int[] a, int from, int to) HOTvoidO(k log k)Sorts only [from, to). Sorting a sub-segment — "Sort Colors" variants, partial sorting after a merge.
sort(T[] a)voidO(n log n)TimSort on objects — stable, and O(n) on already-sorted or reverse-sorted input. Requires T implements Comparable or throws ClassCastException.
sort(T[] a, Comparator<? super T> c) HOTvoidO(n log n)Stable sort with a custom order. The single most important sorting call in interviews: Arrays.sort(intervals, (x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[0], y[0])) — sorting a int[][] by first column for merge-intervals, meeting-rooms, and every sweep-line problem.
sort(T[] a, int from, int to, Comparator c)voidO(k log k)Sub-range with a comparator.
parallelSort(int[] a) J8voidO(n log n / p)Fork-join parallel sort. Only pays off above roughly 213 elements. Irrelevant on single-threaded judges; occasionally useful in take-homes.
▲ You cannot sort an int[] in descending order directly

Arrays.sort(int[], Comparator) does not exist. A Comparator<Integer> cannot apply to primitives. Three real options:

// 1 — box it (simplest, allocates)
Integer[] boxed = Arrays.stream(nums).boxed().toArray(Integer[]::new);
Arrays.sort(boxed, Comparator.reverseOrder());

// 2 — sort ascending then reverse in place (fastest, keeps int[])
Arrays.sort(nums);
for (int i = 0, j = nums.length - 1; i < j; i++, j--) {
    int t = nums[i]; nums[i] = nums[j]; nums[j] = t;
}

// 3 — negate, sort, negate (cute; breaks on Integer.MIN_VALUE)
for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) nums[i] = -nums[i];
Arrays.sort(nums);
for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) nums[i] = -nums[i];

Related and more serious: Arrays.sort(int[]) is quicksort, so its worst case is O(n²), and adversarial test data exists for exactly this on competitive-programming judges. If you are on Codeforces and a sort-based solution is mysteriously timing out, box to Integer[] (TimSort, guaranteed O(n log n)) or shuffle the array before sorting.

Searching

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
binarySearch(int[] a, int key) TRAPintO(log n)Array must already be sorted or the result is undefined. On a hit returns some matching index (not necessarily the first when there are duplicates). On a miss returns -(insertionPoint) - 1, a negative number. Recover the insertion point with int ip = ~ret; or -(ret + 1) — that is the index where the key would go, which is exactly the "lower bound" you want for LIS and for counting elements less than x.
binarySearch(int[] a, int from, int to, int key)intO(log k)Searches a sorted sub-range. Patience-sorting LIS where only a prefix of the tails array is valid: Arrays.binarySearch(tails, 0, len, x).
binarySearch(T[] a, T key [, Comparator c])intO(log n)Object version. The comparator must match the one used to sort.
mismatch(int[] a, int[] b) J9int, or −1O(n)Index of the first differing element, or −1 if one is a prefix of the other and they are otherwise equal. Finding the longest common prefix of two arrays in one call.
compare(int[] a, int[] b) J9intO(n)Lexicographic comparison of two arrays. Makes int[] sortable as a unit: Arrays.sort(pairs, Arrays::compare) sorts by first element, then second — a very tidy way to sort a int[][].
compareUnsigned(int[] a, int[] b) J9intO(n)Unsigned lexicographic comparison.
◆ Reading binarySearch's negative return — the LIS pattern
int[] a = {10, 20, 30, 40};
Arrays.binarySearch(a, 30)   // 2  — found at index 2
Arrays.binarySearch(a, 25)   // -3 — not found; would insert at index 2
int ret = Arrays.binarySearch(a, 25);
int insertAt = ret >= 0 ? ret : ~ret;   // ~(-3) == 2. Clean and idiomatic.

// Longest Increasing Subsequence in O(n log n) — patience sorting
int[] tails = new int[nums.length];
int len = 0;
for (int x : nums) {
    int i = Arrays.binarySearch(tails, 0, len, x);
    if (i < 0) i = ~i;          // convert miss to insertion point
    tails[i] = x;                // overwrite the first tail >= x
    if (i == len) len++;         // extended the sequence
}
return len;

Filling & generating

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
fill(int[] a, int val) HOTvoidO(n)Sets every element. Arrays.fill(memo, -1) to mark a DP table as uncomputed; Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE) before Dijkstra; Arrays.fill(parent, -1) before DSU.
fill(int[] a, int from, int to, int v)voidO(k)Fills a range, to exclusive.
setAll(int[] a, IntUnaryOperator f) J8voidO(n)Sets a[i] = f(i) for every index. Arrays.setAll(parent, i -> i) is the one-line DSU initialiser — every node is its own parent. Also Arrays.setAll(a, i -> i + 1) for a 1..n array.
setAll(T[] a, IntFunction<T> f) J8voidO(n)Object version. Arrays.setAll(adj, i -> new ArrayList<>()) initialises a whole adjacency list in one line.
parallelPrefix(int[] a, IntBinaryOperator op) J8voidO(n)In-place cumulative fold — Arrays.parallelPrefix(a, Integer::sum) turns an array into its own prefix-sum array. Elegant, but write the explicit loop in interviews so your intent is visible.

Comparison, hashing & printing

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
equals(int[] a, int[] b) HOTbooleanO(n)Element-wise equality. Necessary because a.equals(b) on arrays is reference identity and is almost always the bug. Use this for comparing frequency signatures in sliding-window anagram problems.
deepEquals(Object[] a, Object[] b)booleanO(total)Recursive equality for nested arrays. Comparing two int[][] grids.
hashCode(int[] a)intO(n)Content-based hash. Needed if you want to use array content as a hash key — but note you cannot put a raw int[] in a HashSet and get value semantics; convert to a String or a List<Integer> instead.
deepHashCode(Object[] a)intO(total)Recursive hash for nested arrays.
toString(int[] a) HOTStringO(n)Renders as [1, 2, 3]. The debugging workhorse. System.out.println(arr) prints [I@6d06d69c, which tells you nothing. Also doubles as a cheap canonical key for a frequency array.
deepToString(Object[] a) HOTStringO(total)Renders nested arrays as [[1, 2], [3, 4]]. The only sane way to print a int[][] — Arrays.toString(grid) gives you a list of object identities.

Bridging to collections & streams

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
asList(T... a) TRAPList<T>O(1)A fixed-size view backed by the array — add/remove throw UnsupportedOperationException, and set writes through to the array. Fine for quick literals and for contains checks. Wrap it when you need to mutate: new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(a)). And it does not work on int[] — see the trap box.
stream(int[] a) HOTIntStreamO(1) lazyPrimitive stream over an int[]. Arrays.stream(nums).sum(), .max().getAsInt(), .distinct().count(), .boxed().toList().
stream(int[] a, int from, int to)IntStreamO(1) lazyStream over a sub-range — no copy.
stream(T[] a)Stream<T>O(1) lazyObject stream. Arrays.stream(grid).map(int[]::clone).toArray(int[][]::new) for a deep copy.
▲ Arrays.asList(int[]) does not do what you expect
int[] prim = {1, 2, 3};
List<int[]> oops = Arrays.asList(prim);   // a List of ONE element (the array itself)!
oops.size()                                // 1, not 3

// Correct conversions, int[] -> List<Integer>
List<Integer> ok1 = Arrays.stream(prim).boxed().toList();              // J16+, immutable
