List<Integer> ok2 = Arrays.stream(prim).boxed()
                          .collect(Collectors.toList());                // mutable
List<Integer> ok3 = new ArrayList<>();
for (int v : prim) ok3.add(v);                                     // explicit, fastest to read

// And back again, List<Integer> -> int[]
int[] back = list.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray();
// mapToInt(i -> i) also works; Integer::intValue is explicit about the unboxing.

// Integer[] -> int[] (no direct cast exists)
int[] u = Arrays.stream(boxedArr).mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray();

The root cause: asList is a varargs generic method, and a primitive array cannot be a T..., so the whole array becomes the single element. With Integer[] it behaves as you expect.

Chapter 14

2-D arrays, grids & matrices

Grid problems are their own genre — islands, flood fill, shortest path in a maze, spiral traversal, rotation, game-of-life. They all share the same handful of mechanics, and getting the boundary check right the first time is most of the battle.

Declaration & the jagged truth

// A "2-D array" in Java is really an array of references to 1-D arrays.
int[][] g = new int[R][C];        // R rows, each a new int[C], all zeros
int[][] lit = {{1,2,3},{4,5,6}};  // literal
int[][] jag = new int[R][];         // rows are null — you must allocate each one
for (int r = 0; r < R; r++) jag[r] = new int[r + 1];   // triangular, e.g. Pascal / interval DP

// Dimensions — note the asymmetry
int rows = g.length;             // number of rows
int cols = g[0].length;          // columns IN ROW 0 — rows may differ in a jagged array!
// Guard against an empty grid before touching g[0]:
if (g == null || g.length == 0 || g[0].length == 0) return 0;

// Initialising a whole 2-D DP table
int[][] dp = new int[n + 1][m + 1];
for (int[] row : dp) Arrays.fill(row, -1);        // memo: -1 = not computed

// 3-D, for DP with an extra state dimension (e.g. "at most k transactions")
int[][][] dp3 = new int[n][k + 1][2];
for (int[][] plane : dp3) for (int[] row : plane) Arrays.fill(row, -1);

The direction-array pattern memorise this; it appears in every grid problem

// 4-directional (up, down, left, right) — two equivalent styles
static final int[][] DIRS = {{-1,0},{1,0},{0,-1},{0,1}};
static final int[] DR = {-1, 1, 0, 0}, DC = {0, 0, -1, 1};   // parallel arrays, faster

for (int d = 0; d < 4; d++) {
    int nr = r + DR[d], nc = c + DC[d];
    if (nr < 0 || nr >= R || nc < 0 || nc >= C) continue;   // bounds FIRST
    if (visited[nr][nc] || grid[nr][nc] == '0') continue;      // then validity
    // ... process (nr, nc)
}

// 8-directional (includes diagonals) — Game of Life, Minesweeper, word search
static final int[] DR8 = {-1,-1,-1,0,0,1,1,1};
static final int[] DC8 = {-1,0,1,-1,1,-1,0,1};
// Or generate them: for (int dr=-1;dr<=1;dr++) for (int dc=-1;dc<=1;dc++) if (dr!=0||dc!=0)

// Clockwise spiral order: right, down, left, up. The cycle is DR={0,1,0,-1}, DC={1,0,-1,0}.
// Turn right with: d = (d + 1) % 4.  Turn left with: d = (d + 3) % 4.

Encoding a cell as a single value for HashSet / HashMap keys and BFS queues

// The problem: int[]{r,c} in a HashSet uses reference equality, so it NEVER dedupes.
Set<int[]> broken = new HashSet<>();       // silently useless

// Fix 1 — encode as a single int. Fastest. Requires knowing C.
int code = r * C + c;
int r2 = code / C, c2 = code % C;
Set<Integer> seen = new HashSet<>();
boolean[] seenFast = new boolean[R * C];       // better still: no boxing at all

// Fix 2 — encode as a long when the grid is huge or coordinates can be negative
long key = ((long) r << 32) | (c & 0xFFFFFFFFL);
int rr = (int)(key >> 32), cc = (int) key;

// Fix 3 — a String key. Clear, slower, allocates. Acceptable when n is small.
String key = r + "," + c;

// Fix 4 — a record (J16+). Correct equals/hashCode for free, and self-documenting.
record Cell(int r, int c) {}
Set<Cell> seen = new HashSet<>();
seen.add(new Cell(r, c));                   // works correctly — see Chapter 41

Matrix transformations

// Rotate 90 degrees CLOCKWISE, in place (square matrix): transpose, then reverse each row
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
    for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {          // j starts at i+1 — upper triangle only
        int t = m[i][j]; m[i][j] = m[j][i]; m[j][i] = t;
    }
for (int[] row : m)
    for (int a = 0, b = n - 1; a < b; a++, b--) { int t = row[a]; row[a] = row[b]; row[b] = t; }

// Rotate 90 COUNTER-clockwise: transpose, then reverse the ORDER OF ROWS
// Rotate 180: reverse rows and reverse each row

// Transpose a non-square matrix (produces a new C x R array)
int[][] t = new int[C][R];
for (int r = 0; r < R; r++) for (int c = 0; c < C; c++) t[c][r] = g[r][c];

// Diagonal identities — worth memorising for "Diagonal Traverse" and N-Queens
//   cells on the same MAIN diagonal (top-left to bottom-right): r - c is constant
//   cells on the same ANTI diagonal (top-right to bottom-left): r + c is constant
// Index the main diagonals with (r - c + C - 1) to keep it non-negative: 0 .. R+C-2
boolean[] colUsed = new boolean[n], diag = new boolean[2*n], anti = new boolean[2*n];
// N-Queens conflict test: colUsed[c] || diag[r - c + n] || anti[r + c]

// 2-D prefix sums — answer any rectangle-sum query in O(1) after O(RC) preprocessing
int[][] p = new int[R + 1][C + 1];
for (int r = 0; r < R; r++)
    for (int c = 0; c < C; c++)
        p[r+1][c+1] = g[r][c] + p[r][c+1] + p[r+1][c] - p[r][c];
// sum of the rectangle (r1,c1)..(r2,c2) inclusive:
int sum = p[r2+1][c2+1] - p[r1][c2+1] - p[r2+1][c1] + p[r1][c1];
◆ Sorting a int[][] — the interval-problem opener
// By the first column ascending — merge intervals, meeting rooms, non-overlapping intervals
Arrays.sort(iv, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
Arrays.sort(iv, Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[0]));            // same, more readable

// By the SECOND column — the greedy key for "maximum non-overlapping intervals"
Arrays.sort(iv, Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[1]));

// First column ascending, then second DESCENDING — "Russian Doll Envelopes"
Arrays.sort(iv, (a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? a[0] - b[0] : b[1] - a[1]);
// safer against overflow:
Arrays.sort(iv, Comparator.<int[]>comparingInt(a -> a[0])
                       .thenComparing(a -> -a[1]));

// Full lexicographic order in one method reference (J9+)
Arrays.sort(iv, Arrays::compare);

Note: Arrays.sort(int[][], cmp) works because a int[][] is an Object[] whose elements are int[] objects — so the object overload applies. This is the one place where array covariance helps you instead of hurting you.

Chapter 15

The collections type hierarchy

Java's collections look sprawling but the structure is simple: two root interfaces (Collection and Map), four sub-shapes (List, Set, Queue/Deque, Map), and for each shape a hash-based implementation, a tree-based one, and an array-or-link-based one. Learn the grid and every class falls into place.

                          Iterable<T>
                              │
                        Collection<T>  ────────────────────────────┐
                    ┌─────────┼─────────────────┐                  │
                 List<T>    Set<T>           Queue<T>        SequencedCollection<T> J21
                    │          │                 │
      ┌─────────────┤    ┌─────┴──────┐     ┌────┴──────────┐
 ArrayList    LinkedList  HashSet   SortedSet   Deque<T>      PriorityQueue
 (array)      (doubly     │        (NavigableSet)   │          (binary heap)
              linked)  LinkedHashSet    │      ┌────┴─────────┐
                                    TreeSet   ArrayDeque   LinkedList
                                            (circular array)

                           Map<K,V>   (NOT a Collection)
                    ┌─────────┼──────────────┐
                HashMap    SortedMap       EnumMap
                    │     (NavigableMap)
             LinkedHashMap      │
                              TreeMap

 Legacy, do not use: Vector, Stack, Hashtable, Enumeration

The implementation grid the table to internalise

ClassBacking structureOrderingnull allowed?Reach for it when…
ArrayListResizable Object[]Insertion / indexYesDefault list. Indexed access, append-heavy.
LinkedListDoubly-linked nodesInsertion / indexYesAlmost never. Use ArrayDeque for queue/stack behaviour.
HashSetHashMap with dummy valuesNone (unpredictable)One nullMembership testing, dedupe.
LinkedHashSetHash + doubly-linked listInsertion orderOne nullDedupe while preserving first-seen order.
TreeSetRed-black treeSorted (natural or comparator)NoSorted iteration, floor/ceiling queries, ranges.
HashMapBuckets of nodes, treeified at 8NoneOne null key, many null valuesThe default map. Counting, indexing, memoising.
LinkedHashMapHash + doubly-linked listInsertion, or access orderYesOrdered iteration; LRU cache via accessOrder.
TreeMapRed-black treeSorted by keyNo null keysOrdered keys, floor/ceiling/higher, sub-map ranges.
ArrayDequeCircular arrayInsertion (both ends)NoStacks, queues, sliding-window deques. The workhorse.
PriorityQueueBinary min-heap in an arrayHeap order — iteration is NOT sortedNoTop-K, Dijkstra, scheduling, two-heap median.
EnumMapArray indexed by ordinalEnum declaration orderNo keysKeys are an enum. Faster and tighter than HashMap.
BitSetlong[] bit vectorIndex order—Dense boolean flags over a huge range; set algebra.
Vector / Stack / HashtableSynchronised legacy——Never. Present only for backward compatibility.

Choosing a construction idiom

// Empty, then fill — the normal case. Always declare to the INTERFACE type.
List<Integer>      list = new ArrayList<>();
Set<String>        set  = new HashSet<>();
Map<String,Integer> map  = new HashMap<>();
Deque<Integer>     stack = new ArrayDeque<>();

// Pre-sized — avoids rehashing when you know roughly how many entries are coming
List<Integer> l = new ArrayList<>(n);
Map<String,Integer> m = new HashMap<>(n * 4 / 3 + 1);        // beat the 0.75 load factor
Map<String,Integer> m2 = HashMap.newHashMap(n);            // J19+: does that maths for you

// Copy from another collection
List<Integer> copy = new ArrayList<>(other);              // mutable copy — THE snapshot idiom
Set<Integer>  uniq = new HashSet<>(list);                // dedupe in one line
List<Integer> sorted = new ArrayList<>(new TreeSet<>(list));  // dedupe AND sort

// Immutable literals (J9+) — great for lookup tables and test data
List<Integer> fixed = List.of(1, 2, 3);
Set<String>   vowels = Set.of("a","e","i","o","u");
Map<String,Integer> roman = Map.of("I",1, "V",5, "X",10);      // up to 10 pairs
Map<String,Integer> big = Map.ofEntries(Map.entry("I",1), Map.entry("V",5));
// TRAP: these are IMMUTABLE. add/put/remove throw UnsupportedOperationException,
// and List.of / Set.of / Map.of REJECT nulls with NullPointerException.

// Sorted set / map with a custom comparator
TreeSet<String> byLen = new TreeSet<>(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length)
                                            .thenComparing(Comparator.naturalOrder()));
// Max-heap
PriorityQueue<Integer> maxHeap = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
▲ List.of() vs Arrays.asList() vs new ArrayList<>()
List.of(…)Arrays.asList(…)new ArrayList<>(…)
add / removeThrowsThrowsWorks
set(i, v)ThrowsWorks (writes through to the array!)Works
Accepts null elementsNo — NPEYesYes
Backed by the source arrayNo, copiesYes — a live viewNo, copies
SinceJava 9Java 1.2Java 1.2
Use forConstants, lookup tablesQuick literals you will not mutateAnything you will modify

The practical rule: if a collection is going to be modified, build it with new ArrayList<>(…). A surprising number of "why does my solution throw UnsupportedOperationException" moments trace back to a List.of or a stream().toList() being handed to code that sorts or appends.

Chapter 16

Collection & Iterable — the shared API

Every List, Set, Queue and Deque inherits these methods. Learn them once and they apply to every container you will ever use. Complexities below are for a typical ArrayList/HashSet; consult the per-class chapters for exceptions.

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Size & membership
size() HOTintO(1)Element count. A method, and named size — not length, not length().
isEmpty() HOTbooleanO(1)size() == 0. Loop guards: while (!queue.isEmpty()) in every BFS.
contains(Object o) HOTbooleanO(1)~ set · O(n) listMembership via equals. The most common accidental O(n²): calling list.contains inside a loop. If you are testing membership, the container should be a Set.
containsAll(Collection<?> c)booleanO(n·cost)Subset test. "Does this window contain all required characters" — though a frequency map is usually faster.
Adding & removing
add(E e) HOTbooleanO(1)*Appends (List) or inserts (Set). Returns false for a Set when the element was already present — a free "was this new?" test: if (!seen.add(x)) { /* duplicate found */ }. For a List it always returns true.
addAll(Collection<? extends E> c) HOTbooleanO(k)Bulk insert; returns whether anything changed. Flattening results, merging neighbour lists, result.addAll(subResult) in divide-and-conquer.
remove(Object o) TRAPbooleanO(1)~ set · O(n) listRemoves the first element equal to o. On a List<Integer> this collides with remove(int index) — see the trap box in Chapter 17.
removeAll(Collection<?> c)booleanO(n·cost)Set difference — removes everything present in c. setA.removeAll(setB) gives A−B in one call.
retainAll(Collection<?> c) HOTbooleanO(n·cost)Set intersection — keeps only what is also in c. setA.retainAll(setB) is the one-line answer to "Intersection of Two Arrays". Mutates the receiver, so copy first if you need the original.
removeIf(Predicate<? super E> p) J8 HOTbooleanO(n)Removes every matching element safely during a single pass. The correct way to filter a collection in place — using an explicit loop with remove throws ConcurrentModificationException.
clear()voidO(n)Empties the collection. Reusing one buffer across many test cases or BFS levels.
Iteration & conversion
iterator() HOTIterator<E>O(1)Explicit iterator; what the enhanced for loop uses under the hood. Needed when you must call it.remove(), or advance two collections in lockstep.
forEach(Consumer<? super E> a) J8voidO(n)Internal iteration. Readable for side effects; you cannot break out of it, which is why the classic for loop is still better for search.
toArray()Object[]O(n)Loses the element type — you get Object[], not E[]. Rarely what you want.
toArray(T[] a) HOTT[]O(n)Typed array. The required final step when a problem's signature returns String[] or int[][]: list.toArray(new int[0][]). Passing a zero-length array is the idiomatic and fastest form.
toArray(IntFunction<T[]> gen) J11T[]O(n)Generator form: list.toArray(String[]::new). Cleanest modern syntax.
stream() J8 HOTStream<E>O(1) lazyOpens a stream pipeline. See Part V.
parallelStream() J8Stream<E>O(1) lazyParallel pipeline. Never use in interview solutions — unpredictable and usually slower for typical input sizes.
spliterator() J8Spliterator<E>O(1)Splittable iterator for parallel streams. Internal machinery; you will not call it.
◆ Set algebra in three lines
Set<Integer> a = new HashSet<>(List.of(1,2,3,4));
Set<Integer> b = new HashSet<>(List.of(3,4,5));

Set<Integer> union = new HashSet<>(a); union.addAll(b);        // {1,2,3,4,5}
Set<Integer> inter = new HashSet<>(a); inter.retainAll(b);     // {3,4}
Set<Integer> diff  = new HashSet<>(a); diff.removeAll(b);      // {1,2}

// Symmetric difference (in one but not both)
Set<Integer> sym = new HashSet<>(a); sym.addAll(b);
Set<Integer> tmp = new HashSet<>(a); tmp.retainAll(b);
sym.removeAll(tmp);                                        // {1,2,5}

// Note the defensive copy on every line — these methods MUTATE the receiver.
// Forgetting the copy and destroying your input set is a common silent bug.
▲ ConcurrentModificationException

Structurally modifying a collection while iterating it — with an enhanced for, a stream, or forEach — throws at the next iteration step. Java uses a modification counter, not a lock, so the failure is fast but not guaranteed (removing the second-to-last element can escape detection and silently skip an element instead).

// BROKEN
for (Integer x : list) if (x % 2 == 0) list.remove(x);     // CME

// FIX 1 — removeIf. Cleanest, and what you should write.
list.removeIf(x -> x % 2 == 0);

// FIX 2 — explicit Iterator, when the condition is complex or you need the index
Iterator<Integer> it = list.iterator();
while (it.hasNext()) if (it.next() % 2 == 0) it.remove();

// FIX 3 — build a new collection instead of mutating
List<Integer> odds = list.stream().filter(x -> x % 2 != 0).toList();

// FIX 4 — iterate backwards by index (valid for Lists; indices below i are unaffected)
for (int i = list.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) if (cond) list.remove(i);

// Same rule for maps — this is the correct way to prune entries:
map.entrySet().removeIf(e -> e.getValue() == 0);
// and for keys, iterate the keySet's iterator and call it.remove()

Note: replacing a value via map.put(existingKey, newValue) or list.set(i, v) is not a structural modification and is safe during iteration. Only adding and removing count.

Chapter 17

List · ArrayList · LinkedList

ArrayList is the default sequence in Java and one of the six APIs to know cold. LinkedList is its famous alternative and, in practice, almost always the wrong choice — this chapter explains exactly why, because interviewers ask.

List-specific methods on top of everything in Chapter 16

Method signature — inputsReturnsTime (ArrayList)What it does & where you use it
Indexed access
get(int index) HOTEO(1) · O(n) LinkedListElement at an index; throws IndexOutOfBoundsException outside [0, size). This single difference is why you never write for (i…) linkedList.get(i) — that loop is O(n²).
set(int i, E e) HOTE (the old value)O(1) · O(n) LLReplaces in place. Does not shift anything. Returns what was there before, which is occasionally handy.
add(int i, E e)voidO(n)Inserts at an index, shifting everything right. list.add(0, x) prepends — O(n) each time, so building a list front-to-back this way is O(n²). Append and reverse instead, or use an ArrayDeque.
remove(int index) TRAPE (the removed element)O(n)Removes by position, shifting left. list.remove(list.size()-1) is the O(1) backtracking pop — the last index needs no shifting.
indexOf(Object o)int, or −1O(n)First index whose element equals the argument.
lastIndexOf(Object o)int, or −1O(n)Last such index.
subList(int from, int to) TRAPList<E> (a live view)O(1)Not a copy — a window onto the original. Mutating either side affects the other, and structurally modifying the parent invalidates the view. list.subList(a,b).clear() is a neat O(n) range delete. For a real copy: new ArrayList<>(list.subList(a,b)).
Bulk operations
sort(Comparator<? super E> c) J8 HOTvoid (in place)O(n log n)Stable TimSort. Pass null for natural ordering. Preferred over Collections.sort(list, c) — it is the same thing with less typing.
replaceAll(UnaryOperator<E> op) J8voidO(n)Maps every element in place. list.replaceAll(String::toLowerCase) — normalising without allocating a new list.
addAll(int i, Collection<? extends E> c)booleanO(n+k)Bulk insert at a position.
listIterator() / listIterator(int i)ListIterator<E>O(1)Bidirectional iterator that can also set and add. See Chapter 28.
Factories & views
List.of(E... e) J9List<E> immutableO(n)Compact literal. Rejects null. Constant tables, expected results in tests.
List.copyOf(Collection<E> c) J10List<E> immutableO(n)Defensive immutable snapshot.
reversed() J21List<E> (reversed view)O(1)From SequencedCollection. A live view — no copy. for (var x : list.reversed()) replaces the manual downward loop. See Chapter 29.
ArrayList-specific
ensureCapacity(int min)voidO(n)Pre-grows the backing array. Micro-optimisation when you know the final size and the list is large.
trimToSize()voidO(n)Shrinks the backing array to size(). Memory-only concern.
LinkedList-specific (it also implements Deque)
addFirst / addLast(E e)voidO(1)End insertion. The only thing LinkedList is genuinely good at — and ArrayDeque does it faster.
getFirst / getLast()EO(1)Peek at either end; throws NoSuchElementException when empty.
removeFirst / removeLast()EO(1)Pop from either end.
offer / poll / peek / push / popsee Chapter 23O(1)Queue and stack faces. Use ArrayDeque for these instead — same API, better constant factor and cache behaviour.
descendingIterator()Iterator<E>O(1)Back-to-front iteration.
▲ remove(int) versus remove(Object) — the classic Java collections trap
List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(10, 20, 30));

list.remove(1);                      // removes INDEX 1 -> removes 20. Calls remove(int).
list.remove(Integer.valueOf(10));     // removes the VALUE 10. Calls remove(Object).
list.remove((Integer) 30);            // also removes by value — the cast picks the overload

// So this "obvious" line does the wrong thing:
List<Integer> l = new ArrayList<>(List.of(5, 3, 1));
l.remove(3);   // IndexOutOfBoundsException — index 3 does not exist. You meant the value 3.

The overload is chosen at compile time by the static type of the argument: an int literal picks remove(int), a boxed Integer picks remove(Object). Only List<Integer> is affected — a List<String> has no ambiguity. When in doubt, be explicit with Integer.valueOf(x) or list.remove(list.indexOf(x)).

ArrayList vs LinkedList — the honest comparison

OperationArrayListLinkedListWhat actually matters
get(i) / set(i, v)O(1)O(n)Decisive. Any index-based loop is O(n²) on a LinkedList.
add(e) at the endO(1) amortisedO(1)Tie on paper; ArrayList wins in practice (no node allocation).
add(0, e) at the frontO(n)O(1)LinkedList's only real win — and ArrayDeque also does it in O(1).
remove(i) in the middleO(n) shiftO(n) traversalBoth O(n). ArrayList's shift is a fast memory move; LinkedList's pointer-chase is cache-hostile. ArrayList usually wins.
Remove via an IteratorO(n) shiftO(1)LinkedList's second real win, and it is narrow.
Memory per element~4–8 bytes + slack~40 bytes (node + 2 pointers)LinkedList uses roughly 5× the memory.
Cache localityContiguous, prefetch-friendlyScattered, a cache miss per nodeOften a 5–10× real-world difference on iteration.
◆ The verdict, and the interview answer

Use ArrayList for lists. Use ArrayDeque for queues and stacks. Use LinkedList essentially never.

If an interviewer asks "when would you use a LinkedList?", the strong answer is: "Rarely. It wins only when I am removing through an iterator while traversing, or inserting at the front — and for the front-insertion case ArrayDeque is faster because it avoids per-node allocation and has far better cache locality. LinkedList's theoretical O(1) insert is dominated in practice by the O(n) traversal needed to reach the insertion point, plus ~40 bytes of overhead per element. The concept of a linked list is essential — the JDK class is not."

Note that "linked list problems" on LeetCode never use java.util.LinkedList at all. They give you a bare ListNode class with a next pointer. See Chapter 47.

List idioms

// Backtracking path — snapshot at the leaf, undo after the recursive call
void dfs(int start, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> res) {
    res.add(new ArrayList<>(path));        // COPY — adding `path` itself aliases one object
    for (int i = start; i < n; i++) {
        path.add(nums[i]);
        dfs(i + 1, path, res);
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);        // O(1) — removing the LAST index needs no shift
    }
}

// Reverse a list
Collections.reverse(list);                   // in place, O(n)
var view = list.reversed();                  // J21: a live view, O(1), original untouched

// 2-D list ("List of Lists") — the shape of most LeetCode return types
List<List<Integer>> res = new ArrayList<>();
res.add(new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2)));
res.get(0).add(3);                          // mutates the inner list

// Pre-filling a list of lists (Pascal's Triangle, level-order output, bucket sort)
List<List<Integer>> buckets = new ArrayList<>();
for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) buckets.add(new ArrayList<>());
// Now buckets.get(freq).add(value) is safe for any freq in 0..n

// List -> array, for LeetCode signatures that demand one
int[] a  = list.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray();
String[] s = strList.toArray(new String[0]);
int[][] pairs = listOfPairs.toArray(new int[0][]);        // note the [0][] shape

// List<List<Integer>> -> int[][]
int[][] grid = res.stream()
    .map(row -> row.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray())
    .toArray(int[][]::new);

// Sorting a list of int[] pairs by the second element descending
list.sort((x, y) -> Integer.compare(y[1], x[1]));

// Dedupe while preserving order
List<Integer> uniq = new ArrayList<>(new LinkedHashSet<>(list));
Chapter 18

Set · HashSet · LinkedHashSet

A Set adds no methods to Collection — it only adds the uniqueness guarantee. What makes it powerful in interviews is the return value of add, which tells you whether the element was new, giving you duplicate detection for free in a single pass.

The API everything from Chapter 16, with set semantics

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
add(E e) HOTboolean — false if already presentO(1)~This return value is the whole trick: if (!seen.add(x)) return true; // duplicate solves "Contains Duplicate", "Linked List Cycle" with a set, "Happy Number" cycle detection, and every "first repeating element" problem in one pass.
contains(Object o) HOTbooleanO(1)~Membership. "Two Sum" complement lookup, "Longest Consecutive Sequence" (check x-1 absent to find a run start), valid-word checks.
remove(Object o)booleanO(1)~Unambiguous here — a Set has no index-based overload, so set.remove(5) on a Set<Integer> removes the value 5 as you would expect.
retainAll / removeAll / addAllbooleanO(k)~Intersection / difference / union. See the set-algebra box in Chapter 16.
Set.of(E... e) J9Set<E> immutableO(n)Literal set. Throws IllegalArgumentException on duplicate arguments and NPE on null. Constant lookup tables: Set.of('a','e','i','o','u').
Set.copyOf(Collection) J10Set<E> immutableO(n)Immutable snapshot; duplicates in the source are collapsed rather than rejected.
new HashSet<>(Collection c) HOTHashSet<E>O(n)The one-line dedupe: new HashSet<>(list).size() counts distinct elements.
new LinkedHashSet<>(Collection c)LinkedHashSet<E>O(n)Dedupe preserving first-seen order — which plain HashSet destroys. Essential whenever the expected output order is the input order.
▲ HashSet iteration order is not insertion order, and not random either
Set<Integer> s = new HashSet<>();
s.add(100); s.add(1); s.add(50);
System.out.println(s);          // [1, 100, 50] — bucket order, looks arbitrary

// For small Integers it often LOOKS sorted, because hashCode() == value and the
// bucket index is value % capacity. This accidental ordering fools people into
// relying on it, and then the solution fails on larger inputs.

// If you need order, say so explicitly:
Set<Integer> ordered = new LinkedHashSet<>();   // insertion order
Set<Integer> sorted  = new TreeSet<>();         // ascending order
▲ Custom objects in a HashSet need equals AND hashCode

A HashSet finds the bucket with hashCode() and then compares within it using equals(). Override only one and the set silently misbehaves: duplicates appear, and contains returns false for an object that is logically present.

// BROKEN — default identity hashCode/equals, so every Point is "unique"
class Point { int x, y; }
Set<Point> s = new HashSet<>();
s.add(new Point(1,2)); s.add(new Point(1,2));
s.size()                                // 2 — not what you wanted

// FIX 1 — a record. Java generates correct equals, hashCode and toString. Use this.
record Point(int x, int y) {}
Set<Point> s = new HashSet<>();
s.add(new Point(1,2)); s.add(new Point(1,2));
s.size()                                // 1 — correct

// FIX 2 — hand-written, for when the interviewer wants to see it
class Point {
    final int x, y;
    Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        if (this == o) return true;
        if (!(o instanceof Point p)) return false;   // J16 pattern instanceof
        return x == p.x && y == p.y;
    }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(x, y); }
}

// FIX 3 — encode to a primitive key and skip the object entirely. Fastest.
Set<Long> s = new HashSet<>();
s.add(((long) x << 32) | (y & 0xFFFFFFFFL));

The contract: equal objects must have equal hash codes. Unequal objects may share a hash code (that is just a collision). And never mutate a field that participates in hashCode while the object sits in a hash container — it becomes unreachable, because you will look for it in the wrong bucket.

Set idioms

// Contains Duplicate — one pass, early exit
Set<Integer> seen = new HashSet<>();
for (int x : nums) if (!seen.add(x)) return true;
return false;

// Or, fully declarative:
return Arrays.stream(nums).distinct().count() < nums.length;

// Longest Consecutive Sequence — O(n) using a set, no sorting
Set<Integer> s = new HashSet<>();
for (int x : nums) s.add(x);
int best = 0;
for (int x : s) {
    if (s.contains(x - 1)) continue;        // only start counting at a run's beginning
    int len = 1;
    while (s.contains(x + len)) len++;
    best = Math.max(best, len);
}

// Cycle detection in a linked list / a functional graph
Set<ListNode> visited = new HashSet<>();   // identity is fine here, nodes are unique objects
while (head != null) {
    if (!visited.add(head)) return true;
    head = head.next;
}
// (Floyd's tortoise-and-hare is the O(1)-space answer the interviewer wants.)

// Visited set for graph/grid BFS — prefer a primitive array when ids are dense
boolean[] visited = new boolean[n];        // node ids 0..n-1 — fastest
Set<String> visitedWords = new HashSet<>();  // word-ladder states — must be a Set

// Sliding window of distinct characters ("Longest Substring Without Repeating")
Set<Character> win = new HashSet<>();
int l = 0, best = 0;
for (int r = 0; r < s.length(); r++) {
    while (!win.add(s.charAt(r))) win.remove(s.charAt(l++));
    best = Math.max(best, r - l + 1);
}
Chapter 19

TreeSet & NavigableSet

A red-black tree. Everything is O(log n) instead of O(1) — and in exchange you get ordered navigation, which no hash structure can give you. The four navigation methods (floor, ceiling, higher, lower) are the reason this class exists, and they turn several otherwise-hard problems into ten-line solutions.

Navigation — the four methods to memorise

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
floor(E e) HOTE, or nullO(log n)Greatest element ≤ e. "Largest value not exceeding x" — calendar booking, "My Calendar I", best price below a budget, predecessor queries.
ceiling(E e) HOTE, or nullO(log n)Smallest element ≥ e. "Next available slot" — the workhorse of interval-insertion problems.
lower(E e)E, or nullO(log n)Greatest element < e — strictly less.
higher(E e)E, or nullO(log n)Smallest element > e — strictly greater. "Next Greater Element" when values arrive dynamically.
first() / last()EO(log n)Minimum / maximum. Throws NoSuchElementException when empty — guard with isEmpty().
pollFirst() / pollLast() HOTE, or nullO(log n)Retrieve and remove the min / max, returning null when empty. Makes a TreeSet act as a double-ended priority queue — something PriorityQueue cannot do. Used for "Sliding Window Median" and "find and consume the smallest available slot".
◆ Remembering which is which

floor and ceiling are inclusive; lower and higher are strict. The mnemonic: in maths, the floor of 3.0 is 3 — inclusive. "Higher than me" excludes me — strict.

TreeSet<Integer> t = new TreeSet<>(List.of(10, 20, 30, 40));
t.floor(30)    // 30  (<= 30, inclusive)
t.lower(30)    // 20  (<  30, strict)
t.ceiling(30)  // 30  (>= 30, inclusive)
t.higher(30)   // 40  (>  30, strict)
t.floor(5)     // null — nothing is <= 5. ALWAYS null-check these.

Range views & ordering

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
headSet(E to [, boolean incl])NavigableSet<E> (view)O(1) view, O(log n) per opEverything below to. Default is exclusive.
tailSet(E from [, boolean incl])NavigableSet<E> (view)O(1) viewEverything from from up. Default is inclusive — note the asymmetry with headSet.
subSet(E a, E b) HOTNavigableSet<E> (view)O(1) view[a, b) by default; the 4-arg form subSet(a, true, b, true) gives full control. Range counting and range existence: "Contains Duplicate III" (is there a value within t of x?) becomes !set.subSet(x-t, true, x+t, true).isEmpty(). A live view, so subSet(...).clear() deletes a whole range.
descendingSet()NavigableSet<E> (view)O(1)Reverse-ordered view sharing the same data.
descendingIterator()Iterator<E>O(1)Iterate largest → smallest.
comparator()Comparator<? super E>, or nullO(1)The comparator in use; null means natural ordering.
new TreeSet<>(Comparator<? super E> c) HOTTreeSet<E>O(1)Custom ordering. Critical subtlety: a TreeSet decides equality by compare(a,b) == 0, not by equals. A comparator that only looks at one field will treat different objects as duplicates and silently drop them. Always break ties down to a unique key.
▲ A TreeSet comparator that is not total will eat your elements
// BROKEN — two different strings of the same length are "equal" to this comparator
TreeSet<String> t = new TreeSet<>(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length));
t.add("cat"); t.add("dog");
t.size()                     // 1 !! "dog" was rejected as a duplicate

// FIXED — always add a tie-breaker that makes the ordering total
TreeSet<String> t = new TreeSet<>(
    Comparator.comparingInt(String::length).thenComparing(Comparator.naturalOrder()));
t.size()                     // 2 — correct

The same rule applies to TreeMap keys. This is not a problem for PriorityQueue, which happily holds items that compare equal — a difference worth mentioning if an interviewer probes.

Where a TreeSet is the right answer

Dynamic predecessor / successor

Values arrive one at a time and you must query "nearest below / nearest above" after each insertion. A sorted array cannot do this in O(log n) because insertion is O(n).

// Contains Duplicate III: is any prior value within t of x,
// among the last k elements?
TreeSet<Long> win = new TreeSet<>();
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    long x = nums[i];
    Long lo = win.floor(x), hi = win.ceiling(x);
    if (lo != null && x - lo <= t) return true;
    if (hi != null && hi - x <= t) return true;
    win.add(x);
    if (i >= k) win.remove((long) nums[i - k]);
}
Sorted multiset (with counts)

A TreeSet cannot hold duplicates. When you need a sorted multiset — "Sliding Window Median", "Longest Continuous Subarray With Absolute Diff ≤ Limit" — use a TreeMap<E,Integer> of value → count.

TreeMap<Integer,Integer> ms = new TreeMap<>();
ms.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum);        // add
if (ms.merge(v, -1, Integer::sum) == 0)
    ms.remove(v);                   // remove one occurrence
int min = ms.firstKey(), max = ms.lastKey();
◆ TreeSet vs sorting an array

If all the data is available up front and you only query after that, sort an array once (O(n log n)) and use Arrays.binarySearch — it is several times faster than a TreeSet and uses a fraction of the memory. Reach for TreeSet only when insertions and queries are interleaved, so no single sorted snapshot would do.

Chapter 20

Map & HashMap — the complete API

If you learn one API in this entire document, learn this one. HashMap appears in more interview solutions than every other data structure combined. And four of its methods — getOrDefault, merge, computeIfAbsent, putIfAbsent — replace six lines of boilerplate with one, and are the clearest signal that a candidate actually knows Java.

Core operations

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
put(K k, V v) HOTV — the previous value, or nullO(1)~Inserts or overwrites. The return value is genuinely useful: if (map.put(k, i) != null) { /* k was seen before */ }.
get(Object k) TRAPV, or nullO(1)~Lookup. Returns null for a missing key, which throws NPE the moment you unbox it into an int. Prefer getOrDefault almost always.
getOrDefault(Object k, V def) J8 HOTVO(1)~Lookup with a fallback, without inserting anything. The single most useful map method in interviews: map.put(c, map.getOrDefault(c, 0) + 1) is the canonical frequency counter.
containsKey(Object k) HOTbooleanO(1)~Key existence. Use this rather than get(k) != null when null is a legitimate stored value.
containsValue(Object v)booleanO(n)Scans every entry. If you need this often, you need a second reverse map.
remove(Object k)V — the removed value, or nullO(1)~Deletes an entry.
remove(Object k, Object v) J8booleanO(1)~Removes only if the key currently maps to that exact value.
size() / isEmpty() / clear()int / boolean / voidO(1) / O(1) / O(n)Standard. map.size() counts distinct keys — "Number of Unique Elements" in one call.
putAll(Map<? extends K,? extends V> m)voidO(k)~Bulk copy, overwriting collisions.

The Java 8 methods that eliminate boilerplate learn all six

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
merge(K k, V val, BiFunction<V,V,V> f) J8 HOTV — the new value, or nullO(1)~If the key is absent, stores val; otherwise stores f(oldValue, val). If f returns null, the entry is removed. map.merge(c, 1, Integer::sum) is the tersest and fastest frequency counter in Java. The removal-on-null behaviour makes decrementing a multiset a one-liner.
computeIfAbsent(K k, Function<K,V> f) J8 HOTV — existing or newly computedO(1)~Inserts f(k) only if the key is absent, then returns the value either way. The idiomatic multimap builder: map.computeIfAbsent(key, x -> new ArrayList<>()).add(v) — this one line replaces a containsKey/put/get block. Used for graph adjacency lists, "Group Anagrams", bucketing by any key.
computeIfPresent(K k, BiFunction<K,V,V> f) J8V, or nullO(1)~Recomputes only if present; returning null removes the entry.
compute(K k, BiFunction<K,V,V> f) J8V, or nullO(1)~Always invokes f(key, currentValueOrNull). The most general form; use when the update depends on both key and old value.
putIfAbsent(K k, V v) J8V — the existing value, or nullO(1)~Writes only when absent. "Record the first index at which each value appears" — firstIdx.putIfAbsent(v, i).
replace(K k, V v) / replace(k, oldV, newV) J8V / booleanO(1)~Updates only an existing key — never inserts.
replaceAll(BiFunction<K,V,V> f) J8voidO(n)Transforms every value in place.
forEach(BiConsumer<K,V> a) J8voidO(n)Iterate key and value without touching entrySet. map.forEach((k, v) -> …) — the most readable map iteration when you do not need to break.
◆ The four ways to count, ranked
// 1 — merge. Shortest, one hash lookup, no boxing of an intermediate. Best.
map.merge(key, 1, Integer::sum);

// 2 — getOrDefault. Equally correct, two lookups, universally recognised.
map.put(key, map.getOrDefault(key, 0) + 1);

// 3 — compute. Verbose; only worth it when the update logic is non-trivial.
map.compute(key, (k, v) -> v == null ? 1 : v + 1);

// 4 — containsKey + put. What people write before they learn 1 and 2. Avoid.
if (map.containsKey(key)) map.put(key, map.get(key) + 1);
else map.put(key, 1);

// And for DECREMENTING a multiset, merge's null-removal is the killer feature:
map.merge(key, -1, (a, b) -> a + b == 0 ? null : a + b);
// the entry vanishes exactly when the count hits zero — no manual cleanup,
// which matters because map.size() must reflect "distinct values currently present".

// Fastest of all, when keys are lowercase letters — skip the map entirely:
int[] freq = new int[26];
freq[c - 'a']++;

Views & entries

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
keySet() HOTSet<K> (live view)O(1)All keys. Backed by the map — keySet().remove(k) removes the entry, and keySet().retainAll(c) prunes the map. new ArrayList<>(map.keySet()) to get a sortable copy.
values() HOTCollection<V> (live view)O(1)All values, with duplicates. Collections.max(map.values()) finds the highest frequency; map.values().stream().mapToInt(i -> i).sum() totals them.
entrySet() HOTSet<Map.Entry<K,V>> (live view)O(1)Key-value pairs. The correct way to iterate a map — one hash lookup per entry instead of two. Also the basis of sorting a map by value.
Entry.getKey() / getValue()K / VO(1)Accessors on an entry.
Entry.setValue(V v)V (old)O(1)Writes through to the map — the only safe way to modify values while iterating.
Map.entry(K k, V v) J9Map.Entry<K,V> immutableO(1)A standalone pair. A ready-made Pair class — Java has no Pair, and this fills the gap without writing one.
Entry.comparingByKey() / comparingByValue() J8Comparator<Entry>—Ready-made entry comparators. Map.Entry.comparingByValue() is exactly what "Top K Frequent Elements" needs.
Map.of(k1,v1, …) J9Map immutableO(n)Up to 10 pairs. Rejects nulls and duplicate keys.
Map.ofEntries(Entry...) J9Map immutableO(n)Unlimited pairs. The Roman-numeral table, keypad letter mappings, and other constant lookups.
Map.copyOf(Map) J10Map immutableO(n)Immutable snapshot.
HashMap.newHashMap(int n) J19HashMapO(1)Pre-sized for n entries without rehashing — does the /0.75 arithmetic for you. On Java 17 write new HashMap<>(n * 4 / 3 + 1).

Map iteration & sorting idioms

// Iterate — entrySet is the right default (one lookup per entry, not two)
for (Map.Entry<String,Integer> e : map.entrySet())
    System.out.println(e.getKey() + "=" + e.getValue());

for (var e : map.entrySet()) { ... }              // J10 var — less noise
map.forEach((k, v) -> { ... });                   // J8, cannot break out

// AVOID this — it does a second hash lookup for every key
for (String k : map.keySet()) { int v = map.get(k); }

// Find the key with the maximum value
String best = Collections.max(map.entrySet(), Map.Entry.comparingByValue()).getKey();

// Sort a map by value descending, then by key ascending — "Top K Frequent Words"
List<Map.Entry<String,Integer>> es = new ArrayList<>(map.entrySet());
es.sort(Map.Entry.<String,Integer>comparingByValue().reversed()
        .thenComparing(Map.Entry::getKey));

// Top K frequent, with a size-K MIN heap — O(n log k), better than sorting everything
PriorityQueue<Map.Entry<Integer,Integer>> pq =
    new PriorityQueue<>(Map.Entry.comparingByValue());   // min-heap by count
for (var e : map.entrySet()) {
    pq.offer(e);
    if (pq.size() > k) pq.poll();                  // evict the SMALLEST count
}
// pq now holds the k largest. Counter-intuitive but correct: to keep the k LARGEST,
// use a MIN heap so the weakest survivor is always the one you can cheaply discard.

// Invert a map (value -> list of keys)
Map<Integer,List<String>> inv = new HashMap<>();
map.forEach((k, v) -> inv.computeIfAbsent(v, x -> new ArrayList<>()).add(k));

// Group Anagrams — the archetypal computeIfAbsent problem
Map<String,List<String>> groups = new HashMap<>();
for (String w : words) {
    char[] a = w.toCharArray(); Arrays.sort(a);
    groups.computeIfAbsent(new String(a), x -> new ArrayList<>()).add(w);
}
return new ArrayList<>(groups.values());

// Nested map, when you need a 2-level index
Map<String, Map<String,Integer>> nested = new HashMap<>();
nested.computeIfAbsent(a, x -> new HashMap<>()).merge(b, 1, Integer::sum);
◆ How HashMap actually works — the standard follow-up question
  • Array of buckets, length always a power of two (default 16). Bucket index is (n - 1) & hash, which is a cheap substitute for hash % n.
  • Hash spreading: Java applies h ^ (h >>> 16) to your hashCode so that high bits influence the low-bit bucket index. Without this, keys differing only in their high bits would all collide.
  • Collisions form a linked list in the bucket. Since Java 8, a bucket with 8 or more entries converts to a red-black tree, so worst-case lookup is O(log n) rather than O(n). It untreeifies below 6.
  • Load factor 0.75: when size > 0.75 × capacity, the table doubles and every entry is rehashed — an O(n) pause. Pre-size the map if you know the element count.
  • Worst case is still O(n) if you supply a deliberately terrible hashCode (e.g. one that returns a constant) with keys that are not Comparable.
  • Null: one null key is allowed (it lives in bucket 0), and any number of null values. Hashtable and ConcurrentHashMap allow neither.
  • Not thread-safe. Concurrent writes could cause an infinite loop in Java 7's linked-list resize; Java 8 fixed the loop but concurrent use still corrupts state. Use ConcurrentHashMap when asked.
Chapter 21

LinkedHashMap & the LRU cache

A HashMap with a doubly-linked list threaded through every entry. Same O(1) operations, plus predictable iteration order. Its accessOrder mode makes it the shortest correct answer to "design an LRU cache" — a top-20 interview question.

Constructors & the hook method

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
new LinkedHashMap<>() HOTLinkedHashMapO(1)Iterates in insertion order. Whenever a problem's expected output must preserve the order keys were first seen.
new LinkedHashMap<>(int cap, float load, boolean accessOrder) HOTLinkedHashMapO(1)With accessOrder = true, every get and put moves that entry to the end of the list. The head is therefore always the least-recently-used entry. This is the entire LRU mechanism.
removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry eldest)booleanO(1)A protected hook called after every insertion. Return true and the JDK evicts the head entry for you. Override it in an anonymous subclass and your LRU cache is five lines long.
All Map methods—O(1)~Everything from Chapter 20 applies unchanged.
Sequenced methods J21—O(1)On Java 21 it also gains firstEntry, lastEntry, pollFirstEntry, pollLastEntry, reversed. See Chapter 29 — these make manual LRU code much cleaner.

LRU Cache — three implementations, in increasing interview credit

1 · The five-line version (great to know, usually "too easy")

class LRUCache extends LinkedHashMap<Integer,Integer> {
    private final int capacity;
    LRUCache(int capacity) {
        super(capacity, 0.75f, true);            // true = ACCESS order
        this.capacity = capacity;
    }
    @Override protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<Integer,Integer> e) {
        return size() > capacity;                 // evict the head when over capacity
    }
    public int get(int key) { return getOrDefault(key, -1); }
    public void put2(int k, int v) { put(k, v); }
}

2 · Hand-rolled HashMap + doubly-linked list (what interviewers actually want)

class LRUCache {
    private static class Node {
        int key, val; Node prev, next;
        Node(int k, int v) { key = k; val = v; }
    }
    private final int cap;
    private final Map<Integer,Node> map = new HashMap<>();
    private final Node head = new Node(0,0), tail = new Node(0,0);  // sentinels

    LRUCache(int capacity) {
        cap = capacity;
        head.next = tail; tail.prev = head;   // sentinels remove ALL null checks
    }
    private void unlink(Node n) { n.prev.next = n.next; n.next.prev = n.prev; }
    private void pushFront(Node n) {
        n.next = head.next; n.prev = head;
        head.next.prev = n; head.next = n;
    }
    public int get(int key) {
        Node n = map.get(key);
        if (n == null) return -1;
        unlink(n); pushFront(n);                  // mark as most-recently-used
        return n.val;
    }
    public void put(int key, int value) {
        Node n = map.get(key);
        if (n != null) { n.val = value; unlink(n); pushFront(n); return; }
        if (map.size() == cap) {
            Node lru = tail.prev;               // least recently used sits before tail
            unlink(lru); map.remove(lru.key);
        }
        Node fresh = new Node(key, value);
        map.put(key, fresh); pushFront(fresh);
    }
}
// Both get and put are O(1). The sentinel head/tail nodes are the trick that makes
// unlink() branch-free — mention them out loud, interviewers notice.

3 · LFU cache — the harder follow-up

// Least-Frequently-Used: evict the lowest frequency, breaking ties by least-recent.
// Structure: freq -> LinkedHashSet of keys (insertion-ordered = recency within a tier),
// plus key -> value, key -> freq, and a running minFreq.
class LFUCache {
    private final Map<Integer,Integer> vals = new HashMap<>(), counts = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<Integer,LinkedHashSet<Integer>> tiers = new HashMap<>();
    private final int cap; private int minFreq = 0;

    LFUCache(int capacity) { cap = capacity; }

    public int get(int key) {
        if (!vals.containsKey(key)) return -1;
        bump(key);
        return vals.get(key);
    }
    private void bump(int key) {
        int f = counts.get(key);
        counts.put(key, f + 1);
        tiers.get(f).remove(key);
        if (tiers.get(f).isEmpty() && f == minFreq) minFreq++;
        tiers.computeIfAbsent(f + 1, x -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);
    }
    public void put(int key, int value) {
        if (cap == 0) return;
        if (vals.containsKey(key)) { vals.put(key, value); bump(key); return; }
        if (vals.size() == cap) {
            int evict = tiers.get(minFreq).iterator().next();   // oldest in the lowest tier
            tiers.get(minFreq).remove(evict);
            vals.remove(evict); counts.remove(evict);
        }
        vals.put(key, value); counts.put(key, 1);
        tiers.computeIfAbsent(1, x -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);
        minFreq = 1;                                  // a brand-new key always has freq 1
    }
}
◆ Why LinkedHashSet in the LFU tiers?

Within a frequency tier you still need to evict the least recently used. A LinkedHashSet gives you O(1) remove(key) and insertion-ordered iteration, so iterator().next() is the oldest key in that tier. A plain HashSet has no order; a List has O(n) removal. This is the single design decision the problem is testing.

Chapter 22

TreeMap & NavigableMap

The map counterpart of TreeSet: a red-black tree keyed in sorted order, with the same four navigation methods plus entry-returning variants. This is the structure behind interval-scheduling, "My Calendar", stock-span, range-count, and multiset problems — and there is no hash-based substitute.

Navigation — keys and entries

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
floorKey(K k) HOTK, or nullO(log n)Greatest key ≤ k. "Which interval starts at or before this point?" — the core of every sweep over a sorted timeline.
ceilingKey(K k) HOTK, or nullO(log n)Smallest key ≥ k. "Next event at or after this time."
lowerKey(K k) / higherKey(K k)K, or nullO(log n)Strict versions — < k and > k.
floorEntry / ceilingEntry / lowerEntry / higherEntry(K k) HOTMap.Entry<K,V>, or nullO(log n)Same four queries, returning the whole entry so you get the value in the same O(log n) — no second lookup. Prefer these when you need both key and value, which is usually.
firstKey() / lastKey()KO(log n)Min / max key. Throws NoSuchElementException when empty.
firstEntry() / lastEntry()Map.Entry, or nullO(log n)Same, but null-returning rather than throwing — safer in loops.
pollFirstEntry() / pollLastEntry() HOTMap.Entry, or nullO(log n)Retrieve and remove the min / max entry. Turns a TreeMap into a double-ended priority queue keyed by value — the structure for "Sliding Window Median" and "Maximum Performance of a Team".

Range views

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
headMap(K to [, boolean incl])NavigableMap (live view)O(1) viewKeys below to (exclusive by default).
tailMap(K from [, boolean incl])NavigableMap (view)O(1) viewKeys from from up (inclusive by default).
subMap(K a, K b) HOTNavigableMap (view)O(1) view[a, b); the 4-arg form controls both bounds. Range queries: map.subMap(lo, true, hi, true).size() counts keys in a range; .values() lets you aggregate them; .clear() deletes the whole range in O(k + log n).
descendingMap() / descendingKeySet()NavigableMap / NavigableSet (view)O(1)Reverse-ordered views.
navigableKeySet()NavigableSet<K>O(1)The key set with navigation methods attached.
new TreeMap<>(Comparator<? super K> c)TreeMapO(1)Custom key ordering. Same total-ordering caveat as TreeSet — keys that compare equal are considered the same key and will overwrite each other.
new TreeMap<>(Map m)TreeMapO(n log n)Sort a HashMap by key in one line: new TreeMap<>(hashMap).
◆ The three canonical TreeMap patterns

1 · Sorted multiset — a sorted collection that allows duplicates, with O(log n) min, max, insert and delete.

TreeMap<Integer,Integer> ms = new TreeMap<>();     // value -> count
void add(int v)    { ms.merge(v, 1, Integer::sum); }
void remove(int v) { if (ms.merge(v, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) ms.remove(v); }
int min() { return ms.firstKey(); }
int max() { return ms.lastKey(); }
// Sliding window max/min with arbitrary removals — "Longest Continuous Subarray
// With Absolute Diff Less Than or Equal to Limit".

2 · Interval / calendar booking — check for overlap in O(log n) using the neighbours of the proposed start.

TreeMap<Integer,Integer> cal = new TreeMap<>();    // start -> end
boolean book(int start, int end) {
    Integer prev = cal.floorKey(start);       // booking that starts at/before us
    Integer next = cal.ceilingKey(start);     // booking that starts at/after us
    if (prev != null && cal.get(prev) > start) return false;  // prev still running
    if (next != null && end > next)            return false;  // we run into next
    cal.put(start, end);
    return true;
}

3 · Difference array / sweep line on a sparse timeline — the answer to "Car Pooling", "Meeting Rooms II", "My Calendar III", "Number of Flowers in Full Bloom".

TreeMap<Integer,Integer> delta = new TreeMap<>();
for (int[] iv : intervals) {
    delta.merge(iv[0],  1, Integer::sum);        // +1 when an interval starts
    delta.merge(iv[1], -1, Integer::sum);        // -1 when it ends
}
int cur = 0, peak = 0;
for (int d : delta.values()) {                // values() iterates in KEY order
    cur += d;
    peak = Math.max(peak, cur);                 // max concurrent intervals
}
// A TreeMap beats an array here whenever coordinates are large or sparse
// (timestamps up to 1e9) — you pay O(k log k) in the number of EVENTS,
// not O(range).

HashMap vs TreeMap vs LinkedHashMap — the decision

HashMapLinkedHashMapTreeMap
get / put / removeO(1)~O(1)~O(log n)
Iteration orderUnpredictableInsertion (or access)Sorted by key
floor / ceiling / higher / lowerNoNoYes, O(log n)
Range views (subMap)NoNoYes
First / last entryNoJ21 onlyYes
null keysOne allowedOne allowedNullPointerException
Memory per entryLowest+2 pointersHighest (tree node)
Constant factorFastestSlightly slower~3–5× slower than HashMap
Pick it whenDefault. Counting, indexing, memoising.Output order must match input order; LRU.You need ordering or navigation.
◆ EnumMap — the specialist worth one line

When keys are enum constants, EnumMap is backed by a plain array indexed by ordinal(). No hashing, no boxing, iterates in declaration order, and it is faster and smaller than any HashMap. Rare in LeetCode, but the right answer if an interviewer sets up a state machine with an enum. Its sibling EnumSet is a bitmask over the enum constants — EnumSet.of(…), EnumSet.allOf(…), EnumSet.complementOf(…).

Chapter 23

Queue, Deque & ArrayDeque

ArrayDeque is the most underrated class in the JDK. It is simultaneously the best stack, the best queue, and the best double-ended queue in Java — which means one class covers BFS, DFS, monotonic stacks, sliding-window maxima and bracket matching. Learn its three naming families and you have covered a large share of all interview problems.

The three naming families the table that unlocks Deque

Deque exposes the same six underlying operations under three sets of names: an explicit First/Last family, a Queue family, and a Stack family. The confusion this causes is why people avoid the class — so here is the whole mapping in one place.

What you wantExplicit nameQueue nameStack nameOn failure
Insert at the frontaddFirst(e)offerFirst(e)push(e)add throws, offer returns false
Insert at the backaddLast(e)offer(e) / add(e)—add throws, offer returns false
Remove from the frontremoveFirst()poll()pop()remove/pop throw, poll returns null
Remove from the backremoveLast()pollLast()—remove throws, poll returns null
Look at the frontgetFirst()peek()peek()get throws, peek returns null
Look at the backgetLast()peekLast()—get throws, peek returns null
◆ The two rules that make this simple

Rule 1 — pick one family per use and never mix. Using a deque as a stack? Only push / pop / peek. As a queue? Only offer / poll / peek. As a true double-ended structure (sliding-window maximum)? Only the explicit First/Last names. Mixing families is how people end up with a stack that behaves like a queue.

Rule 2 — push adds to the FRONT. That is what makes push/pop/peek all operate on the same end, giving you LIFO. Meanwhile offer adds to the BACK while poll removes from the front, giving you FIFO. One class, two behaviours, entirely determined by which end you insert at.

The complete Deque API

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Stack face — LIFO
push(E e) HOTvoidO(1)*Insert at the front. DFS frontier, monotonic stack, bracket matching, expression evaluation, "Decode String".
pop() HOTEO(1)Remove and return the front. Throws NoSuchElementException when empty — guard with isEmpty().
peek() HOTE, or nullO(1)Look at the top without removing. Returns null when empty, unlike pop. The monotonic-stack condition: while (!st.isEmpty() && nums[st.peek()] < nums[i]) st.pop();
Queue face — FIFO
offer(E e) HOTbooleanO(1)*Insert at the back. BFS enqueue. add(e) is equivalent for an unbounded deque; offer is the habit to build because it is the safe form for bounded queues.
poll() HOTE, or nullO(1)Remove and return the front. Returns null when empty — the safe counterpart to remove(). BFS dequeue.
element() / remove()EO(1)Throwing versions of peek / poll. Prefer the null-returning forms.
Explicit ends — for genuine double-ended use
addFirst / addLast(E e) HOTvoidO(1)*Unambiguous insertion. Use these in sliding-window-maximum code so the reader can see exactly which end you mean.
pollFirst / pollLast() HOTE, or nullO(1)Unambiguous removal.
peekFirst / peekLast() HOTE, or nullO(1)Unambiguous inspection. peekFirst() is the current window maximum; peekLast() is the element you compare against before appending.
removeFirst / removeLast()EO(1)Throwing versions.
getFirst / getLast()EO(1)Throwing inspection.
Other
size() / isEmpty() / clear()int / boolean / voidO(1)size() before a BFS inner loop is how you process exactly one level at a time.
contains(Object o)booleanO(n)Linear scan. Keep a companion HashSet if you need membership testing on a queue.
iterator() / descendingIterator()Iterator<E>O(1)Front-to-back and back-to-front traversal. For a deque used as a stack, iterator() goes top → bottom, which is usually what you want when printing a path.
removeFirstOccurrence / removeLastOccurrence(Object)booleanO(n)Targeted removal. Rarely needed.
▲ ArrayDeque rejects null

null is the sentinel that poll and peek use to signal "empty", so storing one would be ambiguous. Adding null throws NullPointerException immediately. If your BFS genuinely needs a level separator, use a sentinel object, or better, use the size() loop pattern below.

The four patterns you will write constantly

1 · BFS with level tracking

Deque<TreeNode> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
q.offer(root);
int depth = 0;
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    int levelSize = q.size();          // SNAPSHOT before the inner loop — the key line
    List<Integer> level = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < levelSize; i++) {
        TreeNode node = q.poll();
        level.add(node.val);
        if (node.left  != null) q.offer(node.left);
        if (node.right != null) q.offer(node.right);
    }
    result.add(level);
    depth++;
}
// Capturing q.size() first is what separates "this level" from "the next level".
// Without it, the loop would consume nodes you just enqueued.

2 · Iterative DFS with an explicit stack

Deque<TreeNode> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
st.push(root);
while (!st.isEmpty()) {
    TreeNode n = st.pop();
    visit(n);
    if (n.right != null) st.push(n.right);   // push RIGHT first...
    if (n.left  != null) st.push(n.left);    // ...so LEFT pops first (pre-order)
}

// In-order traversal iteratively — the version worth memorising
Deque<TreeNode> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
TreeNode cur = root;
while (cur != null || !st.isEmpty()) {
    while (cur != null) { st.push(cur); cur = cur.left; }   // dive left
    cur = st.pop();
    visit(cur);                                            // visit on the way up
    cur = cur.right;                                       // then go right
}

3 · Monotonic stack — "next greater element"

// For each i, find the index of the next element greater than nums[i].
int[] res = new int[n];
Arrays.fill(res, -1);
Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();      // holds INDICES, decreasing by value
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    while (!st.isEmpty() && nums[st.peek()] < nums[i])
        res[st.pop()] = i;                       // nums[i] resolves everything smaller
    st.push(i);
}
// Total O(n): every index is pushed once and popped at most once.
// Store INDICES not values — you almost always need the position too.
// Flip the comparison to '>' for "next smaller element".
// Same skeleton solves: Daily Temperatures, Largest Rectangle in Histogram,
// Trapping Rain Water, Remove K Digits, Sum of Subarray Minimums.

4 · Sliding window maximum — the true double-ended use

// Maximum of every window of size k, in O(n) total.
int[] out = new int[n - k + 1];
Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();      // indices, values DECREASING front->back
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    if (!dq.isEmpty() && dq.peekFirst() <= i - k)
        dq.pollFirst();                          // front has slid out of the window
    while (!dq.isEmpty() && nums[dq.peekLast()] <= nums[i])
        dq.pollLast();                           // anything smaller can never be the max
    dq.offerLast(i);
    if (i >= k - 1) out[i - k + 1] = nums[dq.peekFirst()];
}
// The front is always the window's maximum. This is the ONE problem where you
// genuinely need both ends, and it is why ArrayDeque exists.
◆ ArrayDeque internals — why it is fast

A circular array with head and tail indices. Inserting at the front decrements head (wrapping via a bitmask, since capacity is always a power of two); inserting at the back increments tail. No node allocation, no pointer chasing, and it grows by doubling. Compared with LinkedList it uses roughly one-fifth the memory and is several times faster to iterate. Compared with Stack it avoids synchronisation on every call. There is essentially no reason to use anything else for stack or queue behaviour.

Chapter 24

PriorityQueue — heaps

A binary min-heap stored in an array. By default the smallest element is at the head. This is the structure behind Dijkstra, top-K, merge-K-sorted-lists, task scheduling and the two-heap median trick — and its two big traps (unordered iteration, and no max-heap by default) catch almost everyone the first time.

The complete API

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Construction
new PriorityQueue<>() HOTPriorityQueue<E>O(1)Min-heap using natural ordering. Elements must be Comparable or you get a ClassCastException on the second insertion (the first never compares).
new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator<? super E> c) HOTPriorityQueue<E>O(1)Custom priority. This is how you get a max-heap: new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder()). Also how you prioritise by a field: Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[1]).
new PriorityQueue<>(int cap, Comparator c)PriorityQueue<E>O(1)Pre-sized with a comparator. The capacity must be ≥ 1 — passing 0 throws IllegalArgumentException, a surprisingly common crash when the input can be empty.
new PriorityQueue<>(Collection<? extends E> c) HOTPriorityQueue<E>O(n)Bulk heapify in linear time — strictly faster than n separate offer calls (O(n log n)). Use this whenever all elements are known up front. Note it only accepts a Collection, so a comparator cannot be supplied in the same call; build empty-with-comparator then addAll if you need both.
Core operations
offer(E e) HOTboolean (always true)O(log n)Insert, then sift up. add(e) is identical for this class.
poll() HOTE, or nullO(log n)Remove and return the head (the minimum, or whatever your comparator ranks first), then sift down. Returns null when empty. The extract step of every heap algorithm.
peek() HOTE, or nullO(1)Look at the head without removing. Free to call — use it liberally in loop conditions: while (!pq.isEmpty() && pq.peek()[0] < t).
remove() / element()EO(log n) / O(1)Throwing versions of poll / peek.
remove(Object o) TRAPbooleanO(n)Removes an arbitrary element — but it must scan to find it first. If your algorithm needs arbitrary deletion, a PriorityQueue is the wrong structure. Use a TreeMap multiset, or use lazy deletion: keep a "deleted" count map and discard stale heads when you poll them.
contains(Object o)booleanO(n)Linear scan. Same advice as above.
size() / isEmpty() / clear()int / boolean / voidO(1)if (pq.size() > k) pq.poll(); is the whole top-K algorithm.
addAll(Collection c)booleanO(k log n)Bulk insert — note this is not the linear heapify; only the constructor gets that.
iterator() TRAPIterator<E>O(1)Iterates in ARRAY order, not sorted order. See the trap box.
toArray() / toArray(T[])Object[] / T[]O(n)Also unsorted — it is the raw heap array.
comparator()Comparator, or nullO(1)The comparator in use; null means natural ordering.
▲ Printing or iterating a PriorityQueue does not give sorted output
PriorityQueue<Integer> pq = new PriorityQueue<>(List.of(5, 1, 3, 2, 4));
System.out.println(pq);            // [1, 2, 3, 5, 4] — heap array order, NOT sorted
pq.peek()                          // 1 — only the HEAD is guaranteed correct

for (int x : pq) ...                 // arbitrary order — do not rely on it

// To get sorted order you must DRAIN the heap:
List<Integer> sorted = new ArrayList<>();
while (!pq.isEmpty()) sorted.add(pq.poll());   // O(n log n), and empties the heap

// Consequence for top-K problems: after you have your size-K heap, the RESULT
// still needs draining (and usually reversing) if the expected output is ordered.
int[] res = new int[k];
for (int i = k - 1; i >= 0; i--) res[i] = pq.poll();   // fill backwards = descending

The heap invariant is only "every parent outranks its children". That is enough for O(1) peek and O(log n) poll, and it is deliberately weaker than full sorting — which is exactly why a heap is cheaper to build than a sorted array.

Building the comparator you need

// MIN-heap (default) — smallest first
new PriorityQueue<Integer>();

// MAX-heap — four equivalent spellings, in order of preference
new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.<Integer>reverseOrder());   // clearest
new PriorityQueue<>(Collections.<Integer>reverseOrder());
new PriorityQueue<Integer>((a, b) -> b - a);            // OVERFLOWS on extreme values
new PriorityQueue<Integer>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(b, a)); // safe manual form

// By one field of an int[]  (e.g. {node, distance} for Dijkstra)
new PriorityQueue<int[]>(Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[1]));

// Two-level: primary ascending, tie-break descending
new PriorityQueue<int[]>(Comparator.<int[]>comparingInt(a -> a[0])
                                 .thenComparingInt(a -> -a[1]));

// By map value, then by key — "Top K Frequent Words"
new PriorityQueue<String>((a, b) -> freq.get(a).equals(freq.get(b))
    ? b.compareTo(a)                       // reverse lexicographic on ties
    : freq.get(a) - freq.get(b));          // min-heap by frequency

// On a record — clean and self-documenting (see Chapter 41)
record Edge(int to, int w) {}
new PriorityQueue<Edge>(Comparator.comparingInt(Edge::w));

// By distance from origin, without sqrt — "K Closest Points to Origin"
new PriorityQueue<int[]>((a, b) ->
    Long.compare((long) b[0]*b[0] + (long) b[1]*b[1],
                 (long) a[0]*a[0] + (long) a[1]*a[1]));   // MAX-heap of size k

The five heap patterns

1 · Top-K — use the opposite-polarity heap of size K

// K LARGEST elements -> keep a MIN-heap of size k.
// The head is the weakest survivor, so it is the cheapest thing to evict.
PriorityQueue<Integer> pq = new PriorityQueue<>();      // MIN-heap
for (int x : nums) {
    pq.offer(x);
    if (pq.size() > k) pq.poll();                  // drop the smallest
}
// pq.peek() is now the k-th largest element. O(n log k) time, O(k) space —
// better than sorting (O(n log n)) when k is much smaller than n.

// K SMALLEST -> keep a MAX-heap of size k. Mirror image.
PriorityQueue<Integer> pq = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
◆ The rule people get backwards

To keep the K largest, use a MIN-heap. To keep the K smallest, use a MAX-heap. The intuition: the heap's head must be the element you are most willing to throw away. When hunting for the largest values, the one you want to discard is the smallest — so the smallest must be at the head, which means a min-heap. Say this out loud in an interview; it demonstrates you understand the mechanism rather than having memorised a template.

2 · Dijkstra — shortest path with non-negative weights

// graph: adj[u] = list of {v, weight}
int[] dist = new int[n];
Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
dist[src] = 0;
PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[1]));
pq.offer(new int[]{src, 0});
while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
    int[] top = pq.poll();
    int u = top[0], d = top[1];
    if (d > dist[u]) continue;              // LAZY DELETION — skip stale entries
    for (int[] e : adj[u]) {
        int v = e[0], nd = d + e[1];
        if (nd < dist[v]) { dist[v] = nd; pq.offer(new int[]{v, nd}); }
    }
}
// The `if (d > dist[u]) continue;` line is essential. Java's PriorityQueue has no
// decrease-key operation, so instead of updating an existing entry you push a new,
// better one and ignore the obsolete copy when it surfaces. This is the standard
// workaround and it keeps the complexity at O(E log V).

3 · Merge K sorted lists / arrays

PriorityQueue<ListNode> pq = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a.val));
for (ListNode head : lists) if (head != null) pq.offer(head);
ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0), tail = dummy;
while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
    ListNode n = pq.poll();
    tail.next = n; tail = n;
    if (n.next != null) pq.offer(n.next);    // refill from the list we just drew from
}
return dummy.next;
// O(N log k) where N is the total element count and k the number of lists.

4 · Two heaps — running median

// A MAX-heap for the lower half, a MIN-heap for the upper half.
// Invariant: lo.size() == hi.size(), or lo.size() == hi.size() + 1.
PriorityQueue<Integer> lo = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());  // max-heap
PriorityQueue<Integer> hi = new PriorityQueue<>();                          // min-heap

void addNum(int x) {
    lo.offer(x);                 // always push to lo first...
    hi.offer(lo.poll());        // ...then move lo's max across, keeping order correct
    if (hi.size() > lo.size()) lo.offer(hi.poll());   // rebalance
}
double findMedian() {
    return lo.size() > hi.size() ? lo.peek() : (lo.peek() + hi.peek()) / 2.0;
}
// The three-line addNum is worth memorising exactly — the push-then-transfer
// order is what guarantees every element in lo is <= every element in hi.

5 · Lazy deletion — a heap with removals

// When you must "remove" elements a heap cannot reach, count them instead.
PriorityQueue<Integer> pq = new PriorityQueue<>();
Map<Integer,Integer> pendingRemoval = new HashMap<>();

void lazyRemove(int x) { pendingRemoval.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum); }

void prune() {                                // call before every peek/poll
    while (!pq.isEmpty() && pendingRemoval.getOrDefault(pq.peek(), 0) > 0) {
        int top = pq.poll();
        if (pendingRemoval.merge(top, -1, Integer::sum) == 0)
            pendingRemoval.remove(top);
    }
}
// Amortised O(log n) per operation — each element is pruned at most once.
// This is how "Sliding Window Median" and "Maximum Performance of a Team"
// are solved without a TreeMap.
◆ Heap internals — the array layout

A complete binary tree flattened into an array with no pointers. For the node at index i (0-based): parent is (i - 1) / 2, left child is 2i + 1, right child is 2i + 2. offer appends at the end and sifts up while it outranks its parent; poll takes index 0, moves the last element there, and sifts down. Both are O(log n) because the tree height is ⌊log₂n⌋. Bulk construction is O(n), not O(n log n), because sift-down from the middle backwards does most of its work on short subtrees — a nice result to be able to state if asked.

Chapter 25

Stack & the legacy classes

You will see Stack in older tutorials and in a lot of LeetCode discussion posts. It still works, but it is a Java 1.0 class with real defects. Know it well enough to read other people's code, and write ArrayDeque in your own.

java.util.Stack API

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
push(E e) LEGACYE (the item)O(1)*Pushes onto the top. Note it returns the item, unlike Deque.push which returns void.
pop() LEGACYEO(1)Removes and returns the top. Throws EmptyStackException — note this is not NoSuchElementException.
peek() LEGACYEO(1)Throws EmptyStackException when empty, whereas Deque.peek() returns null. This difference silently changes the behaviour of copy-pasted code.
empty()booleanO(1)The legacy spelling. isEmpty() also works (inherited from Vector).
search(Object o)int — 1-based distance from the top, or −1O(n)Unusual and rarely useful.
get(int i) / size()E / intO(1)Inherited from Vector. Index 0 is the bottom of the stack.
▲ Why Stack is discouraged
  • It extends Vector, so every single method is synchronized. You pay for locking you will never use.
  • It leaks an indexed API. Because it is a Vector, callers can do stack.get(3) or stack.add(0, x) — operations a stack should not expose.
  • Its iteration order is bottom-to-top, the opposite of what almost everyone expects from a stack. ArrayDeque's iterator goes top-to-bottom. Printing a Stack shows [bottom, …, top]; printing an ArrayDeque used as a stack shows [top, …, bottom]. This bites when you build a result by iterating.
  • peek throws instead of returning null, so guard conditions differ.
  • It is measurably slower than ArrayDeque for the same workload.
// Side by side — the same logic, two classes
Stack<Integer> s = new Stack<>();
s.push(1); s.push(2); s.push(3);
System.out.println(s);          // [1, 2, 3]  — bottom first

Deque<Integer> d = new ArrayDeque<>();
d.push(1); d.push(2); d.push(3);
System.out.println(d);          // [3, 2, 1]  — top first

// Both pop 3 first. Only the ITERATION differs — and that is exactly the kind of
// difference that produces a reversed answer you cannot explain.

The interview answer: "I use ArrayDeque because Stack extends Vector, so it is synchronised, it exposes indexed access that breaks the stack abstraction, and it iterates bottom-to-top. ArrayDeque is the JDK's own documented recommendation."

The other legacy classes, in one table

Legacy classUse insteadWhy
VectorArrayListSynchronised on every method; no benefit single-threaded.
StackArrayDequeSee above.
HashtableHashMapSynchronised; rejects null keys and values; predates the collections framework.
EnumerationIteratorNo remove; awkward method names (hasMoreElements / nextElement).
StringBufferStringBuilderSynchronised; ~10–20% slower.
Date / CalendarLocalDate / InstantMutable and error-prone. Irrelevant to DSA but asked about.
◆ When synchronised collections are the answer

If an interviewer moves to concurrency, the modern choices are ConcurrentHashMap (lock-striped, with atomic compute/merge), CopyOnWriteArrayList (read-heavy, write-rare), ConcurrentLinkedQueue (lock-free FIFO), LinkedBlockingQueue / ArrayBlockingQueue (producer–consumer with blocking put/take), and ConcurrentSkipListMap (the concurrent answer to TreeMap). Collections.synchronizedMap(map) exists but only wraps each method in a lock — it does not make compound operations atomic, so if (!m.containsKey(k)) m.put(k, v) is still a race.

Chapter 26

java.util.Collections — the complete API

A static utility class (note the s — Collections the helper, versus Collection the interface). Half of it has been superseded by default methods and streams; the other half contains several one-liners that are still the shortest correct way to do the job.

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Ordering & rearranging
sort(List<T> l) HOTvoid (in place)O(n log n)Stable natural-order sort. Modern equivalent: list.sort(null). Both are fine; list.sort(cmp) is preferred in new code.
sort(List<T> l, Comparator<? super T> c)voidO(n log n)Same as list.sort(c).
reverse(List<?> l) HOTvoid (in place)O(n)Reverses a list. After building a path bottom-up in a DFS, or after collecting digits least-significant-first. On Java 21, list.reversed() gives a zero-copy view instead.
shuffle(List<?> l [, Random r]) HOTvoidO(n)Fisher–Yates shuffle. "Shuffle an Array"; randomising input to dodge quicksort's O(n²) worst case; randomised pivot selection.
swap(List<?> l, int i, int j) HOTvoidO(1)Exchanges two elements. The core of permutation backtracking — swap, recurse, swap back.
rotate(List<?> l, int dist)voidO(n)Cyclic shift right by dist (negative shifts left). "Rotate Array" in one call — though interviewers want the three-reversal trick.
fill(List<T> l, T v)voidO(n)Overwrites every element. Does not change the size.
replaceAll(List<T> l, T old, T nw)booleanO(n)Value substitution.
Searching & aggregating
binarySearch(List<T> l, T key [, Comparator])int, or -(ip)-1O(log n)Same negative-return convention as Arrays.binarySearch. Requires a sorted list, and requires random access — on a LinkedList it degrades to O(n).
max(Collection<T> c) HOTTO(n)Largest element. Throws NoSuchElementException on an empty collection. Collections.max(map.values()) — highest frequency in one line.
max(Collection<T> c, Comparator cmp) HOTTO(n)Collections.max(map.entrySet(), Map.Entry.comparingByValue()).getKey() — the most frequent key, in one line.
min(Collection<T> [, Comparator])TO(n)Mirror of max.
frequency(Collection<?> c, Object o) HOTintO(n)Counts occurrences. Quick "majority element" verification; do not call it in a loop — that is O(n²), build a frequency map instead.
disjoint(Collection a, Collection b)booleanO(n·cost)True when they share no elements. "Do these two groups overlap?" without building an intersection.
indexOfSubList / lastIndexOfSubList(List, List)int, or −1O(n·m)Sublist search. Rarely used.
addAll(Collection<? super T> c, T... el)booleanO(k)Varargs bulk add — Collections.addAll(set, 1, 2, 3). Handy for seeding.
Comparators & factories
reverseOrder() HOTComparator<T>O(1)Descending natural order. Max-heaps, descending sorts. Comparator.reverseOrder() is the modern synonym.
reverseOrder(Comparator<T> c)Comparator<T>O(1)Inverts any comparator. Same as c.reversed().
emptyList() / emptySet() / emptyMap()immutable empty collectionO(1)Allocation-free empty returns. Early-exit branches: if (n == 0) return Collections.emptyList();
singletonList(T o) / singleton(T) / singletonMap(k,v)immutable 1-element collectionO(1)A one-element result with no allocation of a backing array. List.of(x) is the modern equivalent.
