nCopies(int n, T o) HOTList<T> immutableO(1)An n-element list of the same reference, stored compactly. Initialising a DP list: new ArrayList<>(Collections.nCopies(n, 0)) — the list equivalent of Arrays.fill.
unmodifiableList / unmodifiableSet / unmodifiableMap(c)immutable viewO(1)A read-only wrapper — the underlying collection can still change. List.copyOf makes a true immutable snapshot instead.
synchronizedList / synchronizedMap(c)thread-safe wrapperO(1)Per-method locking only. Prefer the java.util.concurrent classes.
newSetFromMap(Map<E,Boolean> m)Set<E>O(1)Builds a set from any map. The standard way to get a concurrent set: Collections.newSetFromMap(new ConcurrentHashMap<>()).
◆ The five one-liners worth memorising from this class
Collections.reverse(list);                            // reverse in place
Collections.swap(list, i, j);                         // permutation backtracking
Collections.max(map.values());                        // highest frequency
Collections.max(map.entrySet(),
    Map.Entry.comparingByValue()).getKey();            // most frequent key
new ArrayList<>(Collections.nCopies(n, 0));           // a zero-filled mutable list
Chapter 27

Comparator & Comparable

Sorting with a custom order appears in a large share of interview problems, and the Comparator factory methods let you express almost any ordering declaratively. This chapter also covers the a - b overflow bug and the "comparison method violates its general contract" exception — two failures that look mysterious the first time you hit them.

Comparable — natural ordering, implemented by the class itself

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
compareTo(T other)int: <0, 0, or >0O(1) typicallyDefines the ordering for a type. Implemented by Integer, String, Character, Double, LocalDate, every enum, and anything you write. Required for TreeSet/TreeMap/PriorityQueue without an explicit comparator, and for Collections.sort(list).
 
class Task implements Comparable<Task> {
    int priority; String name;
    @Override public int compareTo(Task o) {
        return Integer.compare(priority, o.priority);   // NOT priority - o.priority
    }
}
// A record can implement Comparable too — the compact form is very readable:
record Task(int priority, String name) implements Comparable<Task> {
    public int compareTo(Task o) { return Integer.compare(priority, o.priority); }
}

Comparator — external ordering, composable

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Static factories — how you build one
Comparator.naturalOrder() J8Comparator<T>O(1)Uses compareTo. Useful as an explicit tie-breaker: .thenComparing(Comparator.naturalOrder()).
Comparator.reverseOrder() J8 HOTComparator<T>O(1)Descending. The clean max-heap: new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder()).
Comparator.comparing(Function<T,U> key) J8 HOTComparator<T>O(1)Orders by an extracted Comparable key. Comparator.comparing(Person::getName). Boxes the key on every comparison — use the primitive variants below for int/long/double keys in hot code.
Comparator.comparing(Function key, Comparator keyCmp) J8Comparator<T>O(1)Extract a key and say how to compare it. Comparator.comparing(Person::getName, String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER).
Comparator.comparingInt(ToIntFunction<T>) J8 HOTComparator<T>O(1)No boxing, and overflow-safe because it calls Integer.compare internally. The one to reach for: Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[0]) for sorting a int[][].
Comparator.comparingLong / comparingDouble J8Comparator<T>O(1)Same for long and double keys. comparingDouble is the correct way to sort by a distance or an average.
Comparator.nullsFirst / nullsLast(Comparator) J8Comparator<T>O(1)Wraps a comparator to tolerate nulls instead of throwing NPE.
Instance methods — how you compose one
thenComparing(Comparator<T> other) J8 HOTComparator<T>O(1)Tie-breaker — applied only when the first comparison returns 0. Chainable indefinitely: sort by length, then alphabetically, then by id.
thenComparing(Function<T,U> key) J8Comparator<T>O(1)Tie-break by an extracted key.
thenComparingInt / thenComparingLong / thenComparingDouble J8Comparator<T>O(1)Primitive tie-breakers, no boxing.
reversed() J8 HOTComparator<T>O(1)Flips the whole chain built so far. Placement matters — see the trap box.
compare(T a, T b)int—The single abstract method. A lambda (a, b) -> … is this method.
◆ The comparator contract — three rules a sort depends on
  1. Sign convention: return negative if a should come before b, zero if their order does not matter, positive if a should come after b. Mnemonic: "negative means a is smaller, and smaller things come first."
  2. Antisymmetry: sgn(compare(a,b)) == -sgn(compare(b,a)) for every pair.
  3. Transitivity: if a < b and b < c then a < c. Also for equality: if compare(a,b)==0 then compare(a,c) must equal compare(b,c).

Break rule 2 or 3 and Arrays.sort/List.sort may throw IllegalArgumentException: Comparison method violates its general contract! — TimSort detects the inconsistency mid-merge. The usual causes are integer overflow from a - b, truncating a double difference to int, or a comparator that reads mutable state that changes during the sort.

▲ Never write (a, b) -> a - b
// Looks fine, works for small numbers, catastrophically wrong at the extremes:
Comparator<Integer> bad = (a, b) -> a - b;
bad.compare(Integer.MAX_VALUE, -1);   // 2147483647 - (-1) overflows to a NEGATIVE
// So MAX_VALUE is reported as being SMALLER than -1, and the sort is corrupted.

// Always:
Comparator<Integer> good = (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a, b);
Comparator<Integer> best = Comparator.naturalOrder();
Comparator<int[]>  byFirst = Comparator.comparingInt(x -> x[0]);

// Same problem with doubles, plus truncation:
Comparator<Point> bad2 = (a, b) -> (int)(a.dist - b.dist);  // 0.5 becomes 0!
Comparator<Point> ok2  = Comparator.comparingDouble(p -> p.dist);

// And with longs:
Comparator<Long> bad3 = (a, b) -> (int)(a - b);       // narrowing loses the sign
Comparator<Long> ok3  = Long::compare;

When is a - b safe? Only when both values are guaranteed non-negative and bounded — for example comparing array indices, string lengths, or character codes. Many strong solutions use it for exactly those cases. But the habit of writing Integer.compare costs nothing and can never be wrong.

▲ Where you put .reversed() changes the meaning
// Sort by length DESCENDING, then alphabetically ASCENDING — the usual intent
words.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length).reversed()
                  .thenComparing(Comparator.naturalOrder()));

// This instead reverses EVERYTHING — length ascending AND alphabetical descending:
words.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length)
                  .thenComparing(Comparator.naturalOrder()).reversed());

// reversed() inverts the whole chain constructed SO FAR. Attach it immediately
// after the part you want flipped, or express the reversal inside the key:
words.sort(Comparator.comparingInt((String s) -> -s.length())    // negate the key
                  .thenComparing(Comparator.naturalOrder()));

Comparator recipes for real problems

// Sort intervals by start — Merge Intervals, Meeting Rooms, Insert Interval
Arrays.sort(iv, Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[0]));

// Sort by end — the greedy key for "Non-overlapping Intervals" / activity selection
Arrays.sort(iv, Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[1]));

// Largest Number: concatenate digits to form the biggest number.
// Compare b+a against a+b — the classic non-obvious comparator.
String[] s = Arrays.stream(nums).mapToObj(String::valueOf).toArray(String[]::new);
Arrays.sort(s, (a, b) -> (b + a).compareTo(a + b));
return s[0].equals("0") ? "0" : String.join("", s);

// Sort people by height descending, then by k ascending — "Queue Reconstruction"
Arrays.sort(people, (a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? b[0] - a[0] : a[1] - b[1]);

// Custom alphabet ordering — "Verifying an Alien Dictionary"
int[] rank = new int[26];
for (int i = 0; i < order.length(); i++) rank[order.charAt(i) - 'a'] = i;
Comparator<String> alien = (x, y) -> {
    int n = Math.min(x.length(), y.length());
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        int d = rank[x.charAt(i) - 'a'] - rank[y.charAt(i) - 'a'];
        if (d != 0) return d;
    }
    return x.length() - y.length();          // prefix comes first
};

// Sort a List<List<Integer>> by the first element, then size
res.sort(Comparator.<List<Integer>, Integer>comparing(l -> l.get(0))
                 .thenComparingInt(List::size));

// Sort map entries by value descending — Top K Frequent
entries.sort(Map.Entry.<String,Integer>comparingByValue().reversed());

// Sorting a char[] by frequency (Sort Characters By Frequency) — box it first,
// because Arrays.sort(char[], Comparator) does not exist
Character[] boxed = ...;
Arrays.sort(boxed, (a, b) -> freq[b] - freq[a]);
◆ A generic-inference gotcha in comparator chains

When the first link in a chain is Comparator.comparing with a lambda, Java sometimes cannot infer the element type and you get a confusing "cannot resolve method" error. The fix is an explicit type witness or an explicit lambda parameter type:

// FAILS to infer
list.sort(Comparator.comparing(a -> a[0]).thenComparing(a -> a[1]));

// FIX 1 — type witness on the static call
list.sort(Comparator.<int[]>comparingInt(a -> a[0]).thenComparingInt(a -> a[1]));

// FIX 2 — annotate the lambda parameter
list.sort(Comparator.comparingInt((int[] a) -> a[0]).thenComparingInt(a -> a[1]));

// FIX 3 — assign to a typed local first (clearest under interview pressure)
Comparator<int[]> cmp = Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[0]);
cmp = cmp.thenComparingInt(a -> a[1]);
list.sort(cmp);
Chapter 28

Iterator & ListIterator

Usually invisible behind the enhanced for loop. You need it explicitly in exactly three situations: removing while traversing, walking two collections in lockstep, and implementing a custom iterator — which is itself a recurring design question ("Flatten Nested List Iterator", "BST Iterator", "Peeking Iterator").

Iterator<E>

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
hasNext()booleanO(1)Whether another element remains. Must not advance.
next()EO(1)Returns the next element and advances. Throws NoSuchElementException past the end.
remove() HOTvoidO(1) LinkedList · O(n) ArrayListRemoves the element last returned by next. The only safe way to delete during iteration. Throws IllegalStateException if called before next or twice in a row.
forEachRemaining(Consumer<E>) J8voidO(n)Drains the rest into a consumer. Useful after partially consuming an iterator.

ListIterator<E> — bidirectional, and it can write

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
hasPrevious() / previous()boolean / EO(1)Backwards traversal. Comparing a list against its own reverse without copying.
nextIndex() / previousIndex()intO(1)The cursor position. previousIndex() is the index of the element previous() would return; it is −1 at the start.
set(E e)voidO(1)Replaces the element last returned. In-place transformation with access to the index.
add(E e)voidO(1) LL · O(n) ALInserts before the implicit cursor, so a subsequent next() is unaffected. Splicing elements in mid-traversal — one of the very few places a LinkedList genuinely wins.
remove()void—As Iterator.remove.
list.listIterator(int index)ListIterator<E>O(1) ALStart the cursor mid-list. list.listIterator(list.size()) then previous() walks backwards from the end.

Writing your own iterator — a recurring design question

// "Binary Search Tree Iterator" — in-order traversal in O(1) amortised next(),
// O(h) space. The idiomatic answer: keep a stack of the left spine.
class BSTIterator {
    private final Deque<TreeNode> st = new ArrayDeque<>();

    BSTIterator(TreeNode root) { pushLeft(root); }

    private void pushLeft(TreeNode n) {
        while (n != null) { st.push(n); n = n.left; }
    }
    public boolean hasNext() { return !st.isEmpty(); }

    public int next() {
        TreeNode n = st.pop();
        pushLeft(n.right);        // the next-smallest lives in the right subtree's left spine
        return n.val;
    }
}
// Each node is pushed and popped exactly once across the whole traversal,
// so next() is O(1) amortised even though a single call can be O(h).

// "Peeking Iterator" — add one-element lookahead to any iterator
class PeekingIterator implements Iterator<Integer> {
    private final Iterator<Integer> it;
    private Integer cached;                   // null means "nothing buffered"

    PeekingIterator(Iterator<Integer> it) { this.it = it; }

    public Integer peek() {
        if (cached == null) cached = it.next();
        return cached;
    }
    @Override public Integer next() {
        if (cached != null) { Integer r = cached; cached = null; return r; }
        return it.next();
    }
    @Override public boolean hasNext() { return cached != null || it.hasNext(); }
}

// Making your own class usable in a for-each loop: implement Iterable
class Range implements Iterable<Integer> {
    private final int lo, hi;
    Range(int lo, int hi) { this.lo = lo; this.hi = hi; }
    @Override public Iterator<Integer> iterator() {
        return new Iterator<>() {                  // J9+ diamond on an anonymous class
            private int cur = lo;
            public boolean hasNext() { return cur < hi; }
            public Integer next()   { return cur++; }
        };
    }
}
for (int i : new Range(0, 5)) ...              // 0 1 2 3 4
◆ Lockstep iteration of two collections
Iterator<Integer> ia = a.iterator(), ib = b.iterator();
while (ia.hasNext() && ib.hasNext()) {
    int x = ia.next(), y = ib.next();
    ...
}
// The merge step of merge sort, or comparing two sorted lists, when the
// containers do not support index access.
Chapter 29

Sequenced Collections Java 21

The headline collections change in a decade. Java 21 added three interfaces — SequencedCollection, SequencedSet, SequencedMap — that finally give every ordered collection a uniform way to get the first element, the last element, and a reversed view. Before this, "get the last element" was spelled five different ways depending on the class.

The problem it solves

CollectionFirst element, before Java 21Last element, before Java 21Java 21
Listlist.get(0)list.get(list.size()-1)getFirst() / getLast()
Dequedeque.getFirst()deque.getLast()unchanged
SortedSetset.first()set.last()getFirst() / getLast()
LinkedHashSetset.iterator().next()iterate the whole set (O(n))getFirst() / getLast()
LinkedHashMapmap.entrySet().iterator().next()iterate the whole map (O(n))firstEntry() / lastEntry()

Note the two O(n) rows. Getting the last element of a LinkedHashSet or LinkedHashMap used to require a full traversal even though the class maintains a doubly-linked list internally and always knew the answer. Java 21 exposes it.

SequencedCollection<E> — implemented by List, Deque, SortedSet, LinkedHashSet

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
getFirst() J21EO(1)First element. Throws NoSuchElementException when empty. Replaces list.get(0) and sortedSet.first() with one name.
getLast() J21 HOTEO(1)Last element. The big win: list.getLast() replaces list.get(list.size() - 1) — the single most common source of off-by-one errors in Java. Use it everywhere on Java 21.
addFirst(E e) J21voidO(n) List · O(1) DequePrepend. On a SortedSet it throws UnsupportedOperationException — position is determined by the ordering, not by you.
addLast(E e) J21voidO(1)*Append.
removeFirst() J21EO(n) List · O(1) DequeRemove and return the first element.
removeLast() J21 HOTEO(1)path.removeLast() replaces path.remove(path.size() - 1) — the backtracking undo step, finally readable.
reversed() J21 HOTSequencedCollection<E> (view)O(1)A live reversed view, not a copy. Mutations write through in the opposite direction. for (var x : list.reversed()) replaces the error-prone downward index loop, at zero allocation cost.

SequencedMap<K,V> — implemented by LinkedHashMap and SortedMap/TreeMap

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
firstEntry() / lastEntry() J21Map.Entry<K,V>, or nullO(1) LHM · O(log n) TreeMapEnd entries. On a LinkedHashMap in access order, firstEntry() is the least-recently-used entry — an LRU cache without the removeEldestEntry override.
pollFirstEntry() / pollLastEntry() J21Map.Entry, or nullO(1) LHMRetrieve and remove. LRU eviction in one line: cache.pollFirstEntry().
putFirst(K k, V v) / putLast(k, v) J21V (previous)O(1)Insert at a specific end, moving the key if it already exists. putLast on an insertion-ordered LinkedHashMap is "mark as most recently used" — previously impossible without remove-then-put.
reversed() J21SequencedMap (view)O(1)Reverse-ordered live view.
sequencedKeySet() / sequencedValues() / sequencedEntrySet() J21SequencedSet / SequencedCollectionO(1)Views that themselves have getFirst/getLast/reversed.
 
// Before Java 21 — noisy and easy to get wrong
List<Integer> path = new ArrayList<>();
int last = path.get(path.size() - 1);
path.remove(path.size() - 1);
for (int i = path.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) use(path.get(i));
Collections.reverse(path);                       // destroys the original

// Java 21 — the same operations, unambiguous
int last = path.getLast();
path.removeLast();
for (int x : path.reversed()) use(x);            // O(1) view, original untouched

// LinkedHashSet gets real end access for the first time
LinkedHashSet<String> seen = new LinkedHashSet<>();
seen.getFirst();      // oldest insertion — was iterator().next()
seen.getLast();       // newest insertion — was an O(n) full traversal

// A complete LRU cache on Java 21, using access order + pollFirstEntry
class LRU<K,V> {
    private final int cap;
    private final LinkedHashMap<K,V> m;
    LRU(int cap) { this.cap = cap; m = new LinkedHashMap<>(cap, 0.75f, true); }
    V get(K k) { return m.get(k); }               // access order promotes it automatically
    void put(K k, V v) {
        m.put(k, v);
        if (m.size() > cap) m.pollFirstEntry();     // J21: evict the LRU end directly
    }
}
▲ Version check before you rely on this

Everything in this chapter requires Java 21 or later. On Java 17 these methods do not exist and your code will not compile. LeetCode and Codeforces both run Java 21 at the time of writing, so you can use them there; many company assessment platforms and internal build systems are still on 17 or 11. The safe habit: know both spellings, and if you are unsure what the grader runs, write list.get(list.size() - 1).

One more caveat: reversed() returns a view. If you need an independent reversed copy, write new ArrayList<>(list.reversed()). Structurally modifying the backing list while holding the view behaves the same as with subList — it can invalidate the view.

Chapter 30

Stream: creation & intermediate operations

A stream is a lazy pipeline, not a data structure. Nothing happens until a terminal operation runs, and a stream can only be consumed once. For interviews, streams are a tool of expression, not of performance — this chapter is explicit about where they help and where they hurt.

◆ When to use streams in an interview — and when not to

Use them for: one-line preprocessing (Arrays.stream(a).max().getAsInt()), building frequency maps with groupingBy, converting between arrays and collections, and any final transformation of a result. Used here, they make you look fluent.

Do not use them for: the core algorithm. A sliding window, a two-pointer scan, a DP table, a BFS — all of these need indices, early exits, and mutable accumulators, all of which streams make awkward or impossible. A stream-based "solution" to a windowing problem is usually both slower and harder for the interviewer to follow, which costs you twice.

Performance reality: a stream pipeline over a primitive array is typically 2–5× slower than the equivalent for loop, because of lambda dispatch and (for object streams) boxing. On a 105-element input that is irrelevant; inside an O(n²) loop it can be the difference between passing and a timeout. Never put a stream inside a hot inner loop.

Creating a stream

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
collection.stream() HOTStream<E>O(1)From any Collection. Also map.entrySet().stream(), map.values().stream(), map.keySet().stream().
Arrays.stream(int[] a) HOTIntStreamO(1)From a primitive array — no boxing. Arrays.stream(nums).sum(), .max().getAsInt(), .distinct().count().
Arrays.stream(T[] a [, int from, int to])Stream<T>O(1)From an object array, optionally a sub-range.
Stream.of(T... values) HOTStream<T>O(1)From explicit values. Quick test data. Careful: Stream.of(intArray) gives a one-element stream of int[] — same trap as Arrays.asList.
IntStream.range(int lo, int hi) HOTIntStreamO(1)[lo, hi) — exclusive upper bound, matching a for loop. The indexed-stream idiom: IntStream.range(0, n).filter(i -> …) when you need positions, which plain stream() cannot give you.
IntStream.rangeClosed(int lo, int hi)IntStreamO(1)[lo, hi] — inclusive. Factorials, sums 1..n, iterating divisors.
s.chars() / s.codePoints() HOTIntStreamO(1)Characters of a String as ints. s.chars().filter(Character::isDigit).count().
s.lines() J11Stream<String>O(1)Lines of a multi-line string.
Stream.iterate(T seed, UnaryOperator<T> f)Stream<T> infiniteO(1)Unbounded successive application. Must be bounded with limit or it never terminates. Fibonacci, powers of two.
Stream.iterate(seed, Predicate hasNext, UnaryOperator f) J9Stream<T> finiteO(1)A three-argument form that is literally a for loop as a stream — self-terminating, no limit needed.
Stream.generate(Supplier<T> s)Stream<T> infiniteO(1)Repeated supplier calls. Random data: Stream.generate(rnd::nextInt).limit(n).
Stream.empty() / Stream.ofNullable(T) J9Stream<T>O(1)Zero elements / zero-or-one depending on nullness. ofNullable inside a flatMap silently drops nulls.
Stream.concat(Stream a, Stream b)Stream<T>O(1)Appends one stream to another. Merging two result sets without materialising a list.
random.ints(long n, int lo, int hi)IntStreamO(1)n random values in [lo, hi). Generating stress-test input.

Intermediate operations lazy — they build the pipeline, they do not run it

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Selecting
filter(Predicate<T> p) HOTStream<T>O(n) lazyKeeps elements where the predicate is true. The most-used stream operation.
distinct() HOTStream<T>O(n) + hashingRemoves duplicates using equals/hashCode. Stateful — it buffers a HashSet internally. Arrays.stream(nums).distinct().count() counts unique values.
limit(long n) HOTStream<T>O(n)First n elements — short-circuiting, so it is what tames an infinite stream. Top-K after a sorted.
skip(long n)Stream<T>O(n)Discards the first n. Pagination; dropping a header row.
takeWhile(Predicate<T> p) J9Stream<T>O(k)Takes the leading run that satisfies p, then stops. Different from filter: on [1,2,9,3] with x<5, takeWhile gives [1,2] while filter gives [1,2,3]. Use it on sorted data for early exit.
dropWhile(Predicate<T> p) J9Stream<T>O(n)Discards the leading run, keeps the rest. Stripping leading zeros from a digit list.
Transforming
map(Function<T,R> f) HOTStream<R>O(n) lazyOne-to-one transformation. .map(String::trim), .map(Integer::parseInt).
mapToInt(ToIntFunction<T> f) HOTIntStreamO(n)Unboxes into a primitive stream, which unlocks sum(), average(), max(). list.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray() is the standard List<Integer> → int[] conversion.
mapToLong / mapToDoubleLongStream / DoubleStreamO(n)mapToLong before summing is how you avoid the silent int overflow in IntStream.sum().
mapToObj(IntFunction<R> f) HOTStream<R>O(n)Primitive stream → object stream. s.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c) is how you get a Stream<Character>; IntStream.range(0,n).mapToObj(i -> …) builds objects from indices.
boxed() HOTStream<Integer>O(n)IntStream → Stream<Integer>. Needed before collect, sorted(cmp), or toList. Arrays.stream(nums).boxed().toList().
flatMap(Function<T,Stream<R>> f) HOTStream<R>O(total)One-to-many, then flatten. Flattening a List<List<Integer>>: .flatMap(List::stream). Flattening a int[][]: Arrays.stream(grid).flatMapToInt(Arrays::stream). Splitting sentences into words: .flatMap(s -> Arrays.stream(s.split(" "))).
flatMapToInt / flatMapToLong / flatMapToDoubleIntStream / …O(total)Flatten into a primitive stream.
mapMulti(BiConsumer<T,Consumer<R>>) J16Stream<R>O(total)Like flatMap but you push results into a consumer instead of allocating a sub-stream — faster when most elements produce zero or one output. Rare in interviews; good to recognise.
asLongStream() / asDoubleStream()LongStream / DoubleStreamO(n)Widening a primitive stream. Arrays.stream(nums).asLongStream().sum() — overflow-safe summation of an int[].
Ordering & inspecting
sorted() HOTStream<T>O(n log n)Natural order. Stateful — it buffers everything. Elements must be Comparable.
sorted(Comparator<T> c) HOTStream<T>O(n log n)Custom order. .sorted(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length)). Note IntStream.sorted() takes no comparator — boxed() first if you need one.
peek(Consumer<T> a)Stream<T>O(n)Side effect without consuming. Debugging only — .peek(System.out::println). It may be skipped entirely by the optimiser if the terminal operation does not need the elements, so never put logic in it.
unordered() / parallel() / sequential()Stream<T>O(1)Pipeline hints. Irrelevant to interviews.
▲ Three stream rules that bite

1 · A stream is consumed once. Reusing it throws IllegalStateException: stream has already been operated upon or closed. Materialise to a list if you need two passes.

Stream<Integer> s = list.stream();
long a = s.count();
long b = s.count();     // IllegalStateException

2 · Nothing runs without a terminal operation. A pipeline of filter/map/peek with no terminal call does absolutely nothing — a silent no-op that looks like working code.

3 · IntStream.sum() returns an int and overflows silently. This is the most damaging stream trap in competitive contexts.

int[] big = new int[100_000];
Arrays.fill(big, 1_000_000_000);
Arrays.stream(big).sum();                  // int — WRONG, wraps around
Arrays.stream(big).asLongStream().sum();   // long — correct
Arrays.stream(big).mapToLong(i -> i).sum(); // long — also correct
Chapter 31

Stream: terminal operations

The terminal operation is what actually runs the pipeline. Several of these short-circuit — they stop as soon as the answer is known, which is what makes anyMatch on a huge stream cheap.

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Collecting the result
toList() J16 HOTList<T> immutableO(n)The modern short form. Beware: the result is unmodifiable, so a later .sort() or .add() throws. When you need a mutable list use .collect(Collectors.toList()).
collect(Collector<T,A,R> c) HOTRO(n)The general-purpose reduction. See Chapter 33 for the full Collectors catalogue.
collect(Supplier, BiConsumer accum, BiConsumer combiner)RO(n)Three-argument mutable reduction — collect(ArrayList::new, List::add, List::addAll). Rarely needed.
toArray() / toArray(IntFunction<T[]> gen) HOTObject[] / T[]O(n).toArray(String[]::new) and .toArray(int[][]::new) are how you satisfy LeetCode signatures. On an IntStream, toArray() already returns int[].
Reducing to one value
reduce(T identity, BinaryOperator<T> op) HOTTO(n)Fold with a seed — never empty, so no Optional. .reduce(0, Integer::sum), .reduce(1, (a,b) -> a*b), .reduce(0, (a,b) -> a^b) for "Single Number".
reduce(BinaryOperator<T> op)Optional<T>O(n)No seed, so the result is empty when the stream is. .reduce(Integer::max).
reduce(identity, accumulator, combiner)UO(n)Three-argument form for changing the result type. Rare.
count() HOTlongO(n) or O(1)Element count. Returns long, so cast when you need an int. May be O(1) if the size is known and the pipeline has no filter.
min(Comparator) / max(Comparator) HOTOptional<T>O(n)Extremes. .max(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length)).orElse("").
Matching & finding — all short-circuiting
anyMatch(Predicate) HOTbooleanO(n) worst, short-circuitsTrue if at least one element matches. "Does any row contain a zero?" Stops at the first hit.
allMatch(Predicate) HOTbooleanO(n), short-circuitsTrue if every element matches — and true for an empty stream (vacuous truth), which is a real source of bugs.
noneMatch(Predicate)booleanO(n), short-circuitsTrue if no element matches. Also true when empty.
findFirst() HOTOptional<T>O(n), short-circuitsFirst element in encounter order. .filter(p).findFirst().orElse(null) is "first element satisfying p".
findAny()Optional<T>O(n), short-circuitsAny element — identical to findFirst for a sequential stream, potentially cheaper in parallel.
Iterating
forEach(Consumer<T> a)voidO(n)Side effects. You cannot break, continue, or return from the enclosing method inside the lambda — the single biggest reason a plain for loop is better for search.
forEachOrdered(Consumer)voidO(n)Guarantees encounter order even in parallel.
iterator() / spliterator()Iterator<T> / Spliterator<T>O(1)Escape hatch back to imperative iteration.
▲ stream().toList() is immutable; Collectors.toList() is not
List<Integer> a = list.stream().map(x -> x * 2).toList();                 // J16+, IMMUTABLE
a.sort(null);                          // UnsupportedOperationException
a.add(5);                              // UnsupportedOperationException

List<Integer> b = list.stream().map(x -> x * 2)
                        .collect(Collectors.toList());              // mutable ArrayList
b.sort(null);                          // fine

List<Integer> c = list.stream().map(x -> x * 2)
                        .collect(Collectors.toCollection(ArrayList::new)); // explicit

// Practical rule: use toList() for a value you return and never touch again.
// Use Collectors.toList() (or toCollection) for anything you will modify.
// This mistake is a very common cause of a passing local test and a failing submission.

Terminal-operation idioms worth memorising

// Preprocessing — the legitimate, high-value use of streams in interviews
int max = Arrays.stream(nums).max().getAsInt();
long sum = Arrays.stream(nums).asLongStream().sum();
double avg = Arrays.stream(nums).average().orElse(0);
long distinct = Arrays.stream(nums).distinct().count();
boolean hasNeg = Arrays.stream(nums).anyMatch(x -> x < 0);
boolean allPos = Arrays.stream(nums).allMatch(x -> x > 0);

// Conversions in every direction
List<Integer> boxed = Arrays.stream(nums).boxed().toList();
int[] unboxed  = list.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray();
String[] strs   = list.stream().map(String::valueOf).toArray(String[]::new);
int[][] pairs   = listOfPairs.toArray(new int[0][]);

// Flatten a 2-D array to a single sorted array — "Kth Smallest in a Sorted Matrix" (brute force)
int[] flat = Arrays.stream(grid).flatMapToInt(Arrays::stream).sorted().toArray();

// Sum of a column
int colSum = Arrays.stream(grid).mapToInt(row -> row[j]).sum();

// Indexed processing — the only way to get positions in a stream
List<String> labelled = IntStream.range(0, arr.length)
    .mapToObj(i -> i + ":" + arr[i])
    .toList();

// Indices of all elements matching a predicate
int[] idx = IntStream.range(0, n).filter(i -> nums[i] == target).toArray();

// String from a char stream
String upper = s.chars().map(Character::toUpperCase)
    .collect(StringBuilder::new, StringBuilder::appendCodePoint, StringBuilder::append)
    .toString();
// ...though s.toUpperCase() is obviously better. Shown because the 3-arg collect
// on a primitive stream is the one place you still need it.

// XOR-reduce — "Single Number" as a one-liner
int single = Arrays.stream(nums).reduce(0, (a, b) -> a ^ b);

// Product, guarding overflow by going through long
long product = Arrays.stream(nums).asLongStream().reduce(1, (a, b) -> a * b);
Chapter 32

IntStream, LongStream, DoubleStream

The primitive stream specialisations. They avoid boxing entirely and add numeric terminal operations that Stream<T> does not have. In DSA work IntStream is by far the most useful — it is how you do one-line array aggregation and how you get an index-bearing stream.

Methods unique to primitive streams

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Creation
IntStream.range(int a, int b) HOTIntStreamO(1)[a, b). The index generator. IntStream.range(0, n).boxed().toList() gives [0..n-1] as a List<Integer>.
IntStream.rangeClosed(int a, int b)IntStreamO(1)[a, b].
IntStream.of(int... v)IntStreamO(1)From literal values.
IntStream.iterate / generateIntStream infiniteO(1)As for object streams.
IntStream.concat(a, b)IntStreamO(1)Concatenation.
Numeric terminals — these are the payoff
sum() TRAPint (long for LongStream)O(n)Total. Overflows silently on an IntStream — call asLongStream() first whenever the total could exceed 2.1×109.
max() / min() HOTOptionalIntO(n)No comparator needed. Unwrap with .getAsInt() (throws if empty) or .orElse(0) (safe). Note this is OptionalInt, not Optional<Integer>.
average() HOTOptionalDoubleO(n)Arithmetic mean. .orElse(0.0) for the empty case.
summaryStatistics() HOTIntSummaryStatisticsO(n)Count, sum, min, max and average in a single pass. Strictly better than four separate traversals: var st = Arrays.stream(nums).summaryStatistics(); st.getMin(); st.getMax(); st.getSum(); st.getAverage(); st.getCount(); — and getSum() returns a long, so it does not overflow.
sorted()IntStreamO(n log n)Ascending only — there is no comparator overload. For descending, boxed().sorted(Comparator.reverseOrder()).
toArray() HOTint[]O(n)Directly to a primitive array — no generator function needed.
Conversions
boxed() HOTStream<Integer>O(n)To an object stream — required before collect or a comparator-based sorted.
mapToObj(IntFunction<R>) HOTStream<R>O(n)To objects with a transformation in the same step. .mapToObj(i -> new int[]{i, arr[i]}).
asLongStream() / asDoubleStream() HOTLongStream / DoubleStreamO(n)Widening. The overflow fix.
mapToLong / mapToDouble / mapToIntother primitive streamO(n)Cross-conversion with a function.
map(IntUnaryOperator)IntStreamO(n)Stays primitive. .map(x -> x * x).
filter(IntPredicate)IntStreamO(n)Stays primitive.

IntSummaryStatistics & friends

MethodReturnsNotes
getCount()longNumber of elements.
getSum()longAlways a long, even for IntSummaryStatistics — so this is an overflow-safe way to sum an int[].
getMin() / getMax()intReturns Integer.MAX_VALUE / MIN_VALUE when the stream was empty — not an Optional.
getAverage()double0.0 when empty.
 
// One pass, five answers — genuinely the best tool for "describe this array"
IntSummaryStatistics st = Arrays.stream(nums).summaryStatistics();
System.out.printf("n=%d sum=%d min=%d max=%d avg=%.3f%n",
    st.getCount(), st.getSum(), st.getMin(), st.getMax(), st.getAverage());

// Counting sort bounds without four loops
int lo = st.getMin(), hi = st.getMax();
int[] count = new int[hi - lo + 1];
for (int x : nums) count[x - lo]++;

// 1..n sum, factorial, power table
int triangular = IntStream.rangeClosed(1, n).sum();
long factorial  = LongStream.rangeClosed(1, n).reduce(1, (a, b) -> a * b);
int[] squares   = IntStream.rangeClosed(1, n).map(i -> i * i).toArray();

// Sieve of Eratosthenes, counting primes below n
boolean[] comp = new boolean[n];
for (int p = 2; (long) p * p < n; p++)
    if (!comp[p]) for (int q = p * p; q < n; q += p) comp[q] = true;
long primes = IntStream.range(2, n).filter(i -> !comp[i]).count();

// Digit sum of an integer, via its string form
int digitSum = String.valueOf(n).chars().map(c -> c - '0').sum();

// Frequency array in one expression (lowercase input)
int[] freq = new int[26];
s.chars().forEach(c -> freq[c - 'a']++);

// Matrix -> all values as a set
Set<Integer> vals = Arrays.stream(grid)
    .flatMapToInt(Arrays::stream).boxed()
    .collect(Collectors.toSet());

// Reverse an int[] via a stream (readable, not the fastest)
int[] rev = IntStream.range(0, n).map(i -> nums[n - 1 - i]).toArray();
◆ OptionalInt vs Optional<Integer>

Primitive streams return OptionalInt, OptionalLong and OptionalDouble — separate classes that avoid boxing. Their unwrap methods are named differently from Optional:

  • getAsInt() / getAsLong() / getAsDouble() — throws NoSuchElementException when empty
  • orElse(int) — safe default; use this unless you can prove the stream is non-empty
  • isPresent() / isEmpty() / ifPresent(IntConsumer) — same as Optional
  • There is no map or filter on OptionalInt. Call boxed() on the stream first if you need those.
Chapter 33

Collectors — the complete catalogue

All static factories for stream.collect(…). Two of them — groupingBy and toMap — are worth real study because they collapse a whole bucketing loop into one expression. The rest are worth recognising so you can read other people's code.

To collections

Method signature — inputsReturns a collector producing…TimeWhat it does & where you use it
toList() HOTList<T> — mutable ArrayListO(n)The mutable list collector. Use when you will sort or append afterwards; stream.toList() would be immutable.
toSet()Set<T> — mutable HashSetO(n)Dedupes. Order unspecified.
toCollection(Supplier<C> f) HOTC — any collection you nameO(n)The one to remember when the default is wrong: toCollection(TreeSet::new) for sorted-and-deduped, toCollection(LinkedHashSet::new) for deduped-in-order, toCollection(ArrayDeque::new) for a queue.
toUnmodifiableList / toUnmodifiableSet / toUnmodifiableMap J10immutable collectionO(n)Explicit immutability.
joining() / joining(sep) / joining(sep, prefix, suffix) HOTStringO(total)Concatenates a Stream<CharSequence>. .collect(Collectors.joining(", ", "[", "]")) renders [a, b, c] — handy for serialising a tree or a path.

To a map

Method signature — inputsProducesTimeWhat it does & where you use it
toMap(Function keyFn, Function valFn) TRAPMap<K,V>O(n)Throws IllegalStateException on a duplicate key — the most common surprise in this class. Also throws NPE if valFn produces null. Use the three-argument form unless keys are provably unique.
toMap(keyFn, valFn, BinaryOperator<V> merge) HOTMap<K,V>O(n)The safe form: the merge function resolves collisions. toMap(w -> w, w -> 1, Integer::sum) is a frequency map; toMap(k, v, (a, b) -> a) means "keep the first"; (a, b) -> b means "keep the last".
toMap(keyFn, valFn, merge, Supplier<M> mapFn)M (your map type)O(n)toMap(…, …, …, TreeMap::new) collects straight into a sorted map; LinkedHashMap::new preserves encounter order.
groupingBy(Function<T,K> classifier) HOTMap<K,List<T>>O(n)Buckets elements by a key, values collected into lists. "Group Anagrams" in one expression; grouping words by length, points by quadrant, entries by category. This is the single most useful collector.
groupingBy(classifier, Collector downstream) HOTMap<K,D>O(n)Bucket, then reduce each bucket. groupingBy(f, counting()) → Map<K,Long> frequency map. groupingBy(f, toSet()), groupingBy(f, mapping(g, toList())), groupingBy(f, summingInt(h)).
groupingBy(classifier, Supplier<M> mapFn, downstream)MO(n)Choose the map implementation. groupingBy(f, TreeMap::new, counting()) gives a frequency map with sorted keys.
partitioningBy(Predicate<T> p)Map<Boolean,List<T>>O(n)Splits into exactly two buckets, true and false. Both keys always exist, even if one list is empty — unlike groupingBy. Separating evens from odds, valid from invalid.
partitioningBy(p, downstream)Map<Boolean,D>O(n)With a per-partition reduction.

Reductions & statistics

Method signature — inputsProducesTimeWhat it does & where you use it
counting() HOTLongO(n)Element count. Almost always used as a groupingBy downstream: groupingBy(f, counting()).
summingInt(ToIntFunction) / summingLong / summingDoubleInteger / Long / DoubleO(n)Totals. summingLong for overflow safety.
averagingInt / averagingLong / averagingDoubleDoubleO(n)Means. Returns 0.0 for an empty stream, not an Optional.
minBy(Comparator) / maxBy(Comparator)Optional<T>O(n)Extremes. As a downstream: groupingBy(dept, maxBy(bySalary)) — highest earner per department.
summarizingInt(ToIntFunction) HOTIntSummaryStatisticsO(n)Count, sum, min, max, average in one pass. groupingBy(k, summarizingInt(f)) gives full statistics per bucket.
reducing(BinaryOperator) / reducing(identity, op) / reducing(identity, mapper, op)Optional<T> / T / UO(n)General fold, usable as a downstream collector.
Adapters — collectors that wrap other collectors
mapping(Function f, Collector downstream) HOTDO(n)Transforms elements before the downstream collector sees them. groupingBy(Person::dept, mapping(Person::name, toList())) → Map<String,List<String>> of names per department.
filtering(Predicate, downstream) J9DO(n)Filters within each group. Different from filtering before groupingBy: this keeps the empty groups, which matters when you need every key present.
flatMapping(Function<T,Stream<U>>, downstream) J9DO(total)Flattens within each group.
collectingAndThen(downstream, Function finisher) HOTRO(n)Post-processes a collector's result. Unwrapping an Optional inside a grouping: groupingBy(k, collectingAndThen(maxBy(cmp), Optional::get)). Also the idiom for an immutable result: collectingAndThen(toList(), List::copyOf).
teeing(c1, c2, BiFunction merger) J12RO(n)Two collectors in a single pass, then combine. teeing(counting(), summingInt(f), (n, s) -> s / (double) n) computes an average without traversing twice. Elegant and rarely known.
◆ The collector expressions actually worth memorising
// Frequency map — Map<T, Long>
Map<Character,Long> freq = s.chars().mapToObj(c -> (char) c)
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(c -> c, Collectors.counting()));

// Frequency map with INTEGER values (easier to work with than Long)
Map<String,Integer> freq2 = Arrays.stream(words)
    .collect(Collectors.toMap(w -> w, w -> 1, Integer::sum));

// Frequency map with SORTED keys
Map<String,Long> sortedFreq = Arrays.stream(words)
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(w -> w, TreeMap::new, Collectors.counting()));

// Group Anagrams — the whole problem in one expression
List<List<String>> groups = new ArrayList<>(Arrays.stream(strs)
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(w -> {
        char[] a = w.toCharArray(); Arrays.sort(a); return new String(a);
    })).values());

// Group by length, collecting only the words (not the whole object)
Map<Integer,List<String>> byLen = words.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(String::length));

// Partition — evens and odds, both keys guaranteed present
Map<Boolean,List<Integer>> parts = list.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.partitioningBy(x -> x % 2 == 0));
List<Integer> evens = parts.get(true), odds = parts.get(false);

// Sorted, deduped, in one collector
TreeSet<Integer> sorted = list.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.toCollection(TreeSet::new));

// Dedupe PRESERVING first-seen order
List<Integer> uniqueOrdered = new ArrayList<>(list.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.toCollection(LinkedHashSet::new)));

// Index map: value -> its first index
Map<Integer,Integer> firstIndex = IntStream.range(0, nums.length).boxed()
    .collect(Collectors.toMap(i -> nums[i], i -> i, (a, b) -> a));  // keep the first

// Join into a string
String csv  = list.stream().map(String::valueOf).collect(Collectors.joining(","));
String json = list.stream().map(String::valueOf)
                     .collect(Collectors.joining(", ", "[", "]"));

// Most frequent element (mode)
String mode = Arrays.stream(words)
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(w -> w, Collectors.counting()))
    .entrySet().stream()
    .max(Map.Entry.comparingByValue())
    .map(Map.Entry::getKey).orElse(null);

// Statistics per group — one pass
Map<String,IntSummaryStatistics> stats = people.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(Person::dept,
              Collectors.summarizingInt(Person::age)));
▲ Collectors.toMap throws on duplicate keys and on null values
List<String> words = List.of("cat", "dog", "cow");

// BOOM — "cat" and "cow" both have key 'c'
words.stream().collect(Collectors.toMap(w -> w.charAt(0), w -> w));
// IllegalStateException: Duplicate key c (attempted merging values cat and cow)

// FIXED — say what to do on a collision
words.stream().collect(Collectors.toMap(w -> w.charAt(0), w -> w, (a, b) -> a));      // first wins
words.stream().collect(Collectors.toMap(w -> w.charAt(0), w -> w, (a, b) -> b));      // last wins
words.stream().collect(Collectors.groupingBy(w -> w.charAt(0)));                    // keep BOTH

// Second trap — a null VALUE also throws, even with a merge function.
// This makes toMap unusable when values may be absent; use groupingBy or a manual loop.
Chapter 34

Functional interfaces & method references

Every lambda you write is an instance of one of these interfaces. Knowing which is which tells you at a glance what shape of lambda an API wants — and knowing the four method-reference forms makes your code noticeably tighter.

The core interfaces

InterfaceAbstract methodShapeWhere you meet it
Function<T,R> HOTR apply(T t)T → Rstream.map, computeIfAbsent, Comparator.comparing, Optional.map.
BiFunction<T,U,R> HOTR apply(T t, U u)(T,U) → Rmap.merge, map.compute, Collectors.teeing.
UnaryOperator<T>T apply(T t)T → Tlist.replaceAll, Stream.iterate. A Function<T,T> with a shorter name.
BinaryOperator<T> HOTT apply(T a, T b)(T,T) → Tstream.reduce, toMap's merge function. Integer::sum, Integer::max.
Predicate<T> HOTboolean test(T t)T → booleanstream.filter, removeIf, anyMatch, Collectors.partitioningBy.
BiPredicate<T,U>boolean test(T, U)(T,U) → booleanTwo-argument tests.
Consumer<T> HOTvoid accept(T t)T → voidforEach, ifPresent, peek.
BiConsumer<T,U>void accept(T, U)(T,U) → voidmap.forEach((k,v) -> …).
Supplier<T> HOTT get()() → TorElseGet, Collectors.toCollection, Stream.generate. ArrayList::new, TreeSet::new.
Comparator<T> HOTint compare(T, T)(T,T) → intEverywhere sorting happens. See Chapter 27.
Runnablevoid run()() → voidThreads, ifPresentOrElse's else-branch.

Primitive specialisations they exist to avoid boxing — use them in hot paths

InterfaceMethodShapeWhere it appears
IntFunction<R>R apply(int)int → RmapToObj, Arrays.setAll, toArray(String[]::new)
ToIntFunction<T>int applyAsInt(T)T → intmapToInt, Comparator.comparingInt, summingInt
IntUnaryOperatorint applyAsInt(int)int → intIntStream.map, Arrays.setAll(int[], f)
IntBinaryOperatorint applyAsInt(int,int)(int,int) → intIntStream.reduce, Arrays.parallelPrefix
IntPredicateboolean test(int)int → booleanIntStream.filter
IntConsumervoid accept(int)int → voidIntStream.forEach, OptionalInt.ifPresent
IntSupplierint getAsInt()() → intIntStream.generate
ObjIntConsumer<T>void accept(T,int)(T,int) → void3-arg collect on an IntStream
ToLongFunction / ToDoubleFunction / LongUnaryOperator / DoublePredicate / …——Same pattern for long and double. You rarely name these explicitly; the compiler infers them.

Default methods for composition

Method — on which interfaceReturnsWhat it does
Function.andThen(after)Functionf.andThen(g) = apply f, then g.
Function.compose(before)Functionf.compose(g) = apply g first, then f. The reverse of andThen.
Function.identity() HOTFunction<T,T>x -> x. Common in toMap(Function.identity(), …) — though the lambda w -> w is shorter and clearer.
Predicate.and / or / negate() HOTPredicateBoolean composition. isEven.negate(), isLong.and(startsWithA).
Predicate.not(p) J11PredicateStatic negation that works on a method reference: .filter(Predicate.not(String::isBlank)), where String::isBlank.negate() would not compile.
Predicate.isEqual(target)PredicateEquality test as a predicate.
Consumer.andThen(after)ConsumerRuns both consumers in order.

The four kinds of method reference

FormExampleEquivalent lambda
1 · Static methodInteger::parseInts -> Integer.parseInt(s)
2 · Instance method of a particular objectSystem.out::printlnx -> System.out.println(x)
3 · Instance method of an arbitrary object of a typeString::lengths -> s.length() — the receiver becomes the first argument
4 · ConstructorArrayList::new() -> new ArrayList<>(); String[]::new is n -> new String[n]
 
// Form 3 is the one that confuses people. Two arguments, one method:
Comparator<String> c = String::compareTo;      // (a, b) -> a.compareTo(b)
// The FIRST parameter becomes the receiver, the rest become arguments.

// The method references you will actually use
Integer::parseInt      Integer::sum        Integer::compare    Integer::max
String::valueOf        String::length      String::trim        String::isEmpty
String::toLowerCase    String::compareTo   Math::abs           Math::max
Character::isDigit     Character::isLetter Character::toLowerCase
ArrayList::new         HashMap::new        TreeSet::new        StringBuilder::new
Arrays::stream         List::stream        Map.Entry::getKey  Map.Entry::getValue
int[]::clone           String[]::new       int[][]::new

// Lambda syntax forms — all equivalent where types can be inferred
(String s) -> { return s.length(); }    // fully explicit
(s) -> s.length()                        // inferred type
s -> s.length()                          // single parameter, no parentheses
String::length                           // method reference — shortest

// Multi-statement lambdas need braces AND an explicit return
(a, b) -> {
    int d = Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]);
    return d != 0 ? d : Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]);
}
▲ Lambdas capture effectively final variables only
// WILL NOT COMPILE — count is reassigned, so it is not effectively final
int count = 0;
list.forEach(x -> count++);            // error: local variables referenced from a
                                       // lambda expression must be final or
                                       // effectively final

// FIX 1 — a plain loop. Honestly the right answer in an interview.
int count = 0;
for (int x : list) count++;

// FIX 2 — a one-element array as a mutable box (a known workaround, slightly ugly)
int[] count = {0};
list.forEach(x -> count[0]++);

// FIX 3 — an AtomicInteger (clearer intent, and thread-safe)
AtomicInteger count = new AtomicInteger();
list.forEach(x -> count.incrementAndGet());

// FIX 4 — let the stream do the counting (best when it fits)
long count = list.stream().filter(p).count();

// NOTE: capturing a mutable OBJECT is fine — it is the variable that must not be
// reassigned, not the object's contents. This is why the array trick works, and why
// you can freely mutate a captured List or Map inside a lambda:
List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
list.forEach(out::add);                // perfectly legal
Chapter 35

Optional

A container that holds either one value or nothing. In DSA you mostly meet it as the return type of stream operations you need to unwrap. You will rarely design with it, but you must be able to get values out of it cleanly.

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Creating
Optional.of(T v)Optional<T>O(1)Wraps a value. Throws NPE if v is null — that is deliberate.
Optional.ofNullable(T v) HOTOptional<T>O(1)Empty when v is null. Wrapping a map.get() result.
Optional.empty()Optional<T>O(1)The empty instance.
Getting the value out
orElse(T other) HOTTO(1)Value or a fallback. The default choice: .max(cmp).orElse(0). Note the fallback expression is always evaluated, even when the Optional is present.
orElseGet(Supplier<T> s)TO(1)Lazy fallback — the supplier runs only when empty. Use when computing the default is expensive.
orElseThrow() J10TO(1)Value or NoSuchElementException. The modern replacement for get(); it states the risk in the name.
orElseThrow(Supplier<X> ex)TO(1)Throw your own exception.
get() TRAPTO(1)Throws NoSuchElementException when empty. Only safe after an isPresent() check — and if you are writing that check, orElse is better. Prefer orElseThrow().
Testing & transforming
isPresent() / isEmpty() J11booleanO(1)Presence tests. isEmpty() is Java 11+.
ifPresent(Consumer<T>)voidO(1)Run only when present.
ifPresentOrElse(Consumer, Runnable) J9voidO(1)Both branches.
map(Function<T,R>) HOTOptional<R>O(1)Transform if present, stay empty otherwise. .max(cmp).map(Map.Entry::getKey).orElse(null) — chain instead of null-checking.
flatMap(Function<T,Optional<R>>)Optional<R>O(1)Transform with a function that itself returns an Optional, avoiding Optional<Optional<R>>.
filter(Predicate<T>)Optional<T>O(1)Becomes empty if the predicate fails.
or(Supplier<Optional<T>>) J9Optional<T>O(1)Fall back to another Optional. Chaining candidate lookups.
stream() J9Stream<T>O(1)Zero- or one-element stream. Inside flatMap it filters out empties: .flatMap(Optional::stream).
 
// The DSA reality: you mostly unwrap what streams hand you
int max = Arrays.stream(nums).max().orElse(Integer.MIN_VALUE);   // OptionalInt
double avg = Arrays.stream(nums).average().orElse(0.0);
String longest = words.stream()
    .max(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length)).orElse("");

// Chain rather than null-check
String mostFrequent = freq.entrySet().stream()
    .max(Map.Entry.comparingByValue())
    .map(Map.Entry::getKey)
    .orElse(null);

// orElse vs orElseGet — the difference matters when the default is costly
int v1 = opt.orElse(expensiveDefault());        // ALWAYS calls expensiveDefault()
int v2 = opt.orElseGet(() -> expensiveDefault()); // calls it only when empty
◆ Do not over-apply Optional in interview code

Optional is designed for return types that may legitimately have no value. It is not meant for fields, parameters, or collection elements — it adds an allocation and a layer of indirection for no benefit, and Optional<List<T>> is almost always worse than an empty list. In DSA code the right pattern is usually a plain sentinel (-1, null, Integer.MAX_VALUE) with a clearly documented meaning. Know the API so you can unwrap stream results; do not architect around it.

Chapter 36

BitSet

A growable vector of bits packed into a long[] — 64 flags per word, so 10 million booleans cost 1.25 MB instead of 10 MB. It also does whole-set AND/OR/XOR in O(n/64), which makes it about 64× faster than a loop for set algebra. Niche but decisive when it applies.

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Construction & single-bit access
new BitSet() / new BitSet(int nbits)BitSetO(1)Grows automatically; pre-sizing avoids reallocation. No upper bound other than memory.
set(int i) HOTvoidO(1)Turns bit i on.
set(int i, boolean v)voidO(1)Sets to an explicit value.
set(int from, int to [, boolean v])voidO((to−from)/64)Range set, to exclusive. Marking a whole interval as occupied in one call — far faster than a loop.
clear(int i) / clear(from, to) / clear()voidO(1) / O(k/64) / O(n/64)Turns bits off.
flip(int i) / flip(from, to)voidO(1) / O(k/64)Toggles.
get(int i) HOTbooleanO(1)Reads a bit. Returns false for indices beyond the current size — no exception, which makes sparse use safe.
get(int from, int to)BitSetO(k/64)A new BitSet holding that range, re-indexed from 0.
Bulk set algebra — the reason to use this class
and(BitSet o) HOTvoid (mutates this)O(n/64)Intersection. 64 bits compared per machine instruction. This is how bitset-accelerated DP and subset-matching problems hit their time limit.
or(BitSet o) HOTvoidO(n/64)Union. The transitive-closure inner loop: reach[u].or(reach[v]) — Floyd–Warshall reachability in O(V³/64).
xor(BitSet o)voidO(n/64)Symmetric difference.
andNot(BitSet o)voidO(n/64)Set difference — clears every bit that is set in o.
intersects(BitSet o)booleanO(n/64)Overlap test without building the intersection.
Inspection & iteration
cardinality() HOTintO(n/64)Count of set bits, using hardware popcount per word. "How many primes below n" after a sieve; window size in bitset DP.
nextSetBit(int from) HOTint, or −1O(1) amortisedIndex of the next set bit at or after from. The iteration idiom — see the code below. Skips whole empty words at a time.
nextClearBit(int from)intO(1) amortisedNext unset bit. Never returns −1 (the set grows conceptually forever). "Find the smallest available id" — the whole answer to allocator-style problems.
previousSetBit / previousClearBit(int) J7int, or −1O(1) amortisedBackwards scanning.
length()intO(1)Index of the highest set bit, plus one. 0 for an empty set.
size()intO(1)Allocated capacity in bits (a multiple of 64). Not the element count — that is cardinality(). A common confusion.
isEmpty()booleanO(n/64)True if no bit is set.
stream() J8 HOTIntStreamO(n/64) lazyThe indices of all set bits, ascending. sieve.stream().boxed().toList() gives every prime as a list in one expression.
toLongArray() / valueOf(long[])long[] / BitSetO(n/64)Raw word access, for hashing a bitset or serialising it.
clone()ObjectO(n/64)Independent copy. Required before an and/or, because those mutate in place.
 
// Iterating every set bit — memorise this loop
for (int i = bs.nextSetBit(0); i >= 0; i = bs.nextSetBit(i + 1)) {
    // ... use i
}
// Or as a stream:
bs.stream().forEach(i -> { ... });

// Sieve of Eratosthenes with a BitSet — memory-efficient up to 1e8
int n = 100_000_000;
BitSet composite = new BitSet(n + 1);
for (int p = 2; (long) p * p <= n; p++)
    if (!composite.get(p))
        for (int q = p * p; q <= n; q += p) composite.set(q);
int primeCount = n - 1 - composite.cardinality();

// Set algebra — remember these MUTATE the receiver, so clone first
BitSet inter = (BitSet) a.clone(); inter.and(b);       // A ∩ B
BitSet union = (BitSet) a.clone(); union.or(b);        // A ∪ B
BitSet diff  = (BitSet) a.clone(); diff.andNot(b);     // A \ B

// Subset-sum feasibility in O(n·target/64) — the classic BitSet speed-up.
// A plain boolean[] DP is O(n·target); shifting a BitSet does 64 states at once.
BitSet reachable = new BitSet(target + 1);
reachable.set(0);                                   // sum 0 is always reachable
for (int w : weights) {
    BitSet shifted = new BitSet(target + 1);
    for (int i = reachable.nextSetBit(0); i >= 0 && i + w <= target;
             i = reachable.nextSetBit(i + 1))
        shifted.set(i + w);
    reachable.or(shifted);
}
boolean canMake = reachable.get(target);

// Graph reachability (transitive closure) in O(V^3 / 64)
BitSet[] reach = new BitSet[n];
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { reach[i] = new BitSet(n); reach[i].set(i); }
for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        if (reach[i].get(k)) reach[i].or(reach[k]);    // 64 nodes per instruction
◆ BitSet vs boolean[] vs a bitmask int/long
  • boolean[] — up to a few million flags. Simplest, fastest single-bit access (one byte per flag, no shifting). Default choice.
  • int / long mask — up to 32 / 64 flags. Fits in a register, can be a map key or a DP state index. The right tool for bitmask DP.
  • BitSet — more than ~10 million flags (memory), or when you need whole-set AND/OR/XOR/popcount. The 64× speed-up on bulk operations is the real reason to reach for it.
Chapter 37

BigInteger & BigDecimal

Arbitrary-precision integers and exact decimals. Both are immutable, so every operation allocates. You need BigInteger when a problem's numbers exceed 64 bits — factorials, large powers, "Multiply Strings" verification — and BigDecimal when decimal arithmetic must be exact.

BigInteger

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
Creating
BigInteger.valueOf(long v) HOTBigIntegerO(1)From a long. Caches −16…16.
new BigInteger(String s) HOTBigIntegerO(len²)From decimal text of any length. The entry point for "Add Strings"/"Multiply Strings" when you want to verify your manual answer.
new BigInteger(String s, int radix)BigIntegerO(len²)Any base 2–36. Huge binary or hex inputs.
BigInteger.ZERO / ONE / TWO J9 / TENBigIntegerO(1)Constants. ZERO is the seed for a sum, ONE for a product.
Arithmetic — all return a NEW instance
add / subtract / multiply(BigInteger) HOTBigIntegerO(n) / O(n) / O(n1.585)Basic operations. Multiplication uses Karatsuba and then Toom–Cook for large operands.
divide / mod / remainder(BigInteger)BigIntegerO(n²)mod is always non-negative; remainder keeps the sign of the dividend — the same distinction as Math.floorMod vs %.
divideAndRemainder(BigInteger)BigInteger[2]O(n²)Both results in one pass.
pow(int exp) HOTBigIntegerO(n² log e)Exact integer exponentiation — unlike Math.pow. "Super Pow", large combinatorics.
modPow(BigInteger e, BigInteger m) HOTBigIntegerO(log e · n²)Fast modular exponentiation, for free. Every "answer modulo 10⁹+7" problem; RSA-style questions. Saves you writing the loop from Chapter 06 — though interviewers usually want to see the loop.
modInverse(BigInteger m)BigIntegerO(n²)Multiplicative inverse mod m. Modular division; binomial coefficients mod a prime.
gcd(BigInteger) HOTBigIntegerO(n²)The only GCD in the JDK. BigInteger.valueOf(a).gcd(BigInteger.valueOf(b)).intValue() works but allocates — write Euclid's loop instead.
abs / negate / signum()BigInteger / intO(n) / O(1)Sign handling. signum() returns −1/0/1.
sqrt() J9BigIntegerO(n²)Exact integer square root (floor). Perfect-square tests on huge numbers, with no floating-point error.
max / min(BigInteger)BigIntegerO(n)Extremes.
Bits & conversion
shiftLeft / shiftRight(int n)BigIntegerO(n)Multiply / divide by 2n, with no width limit.
and / or / xor / not / testBit(int) / setBit / bitCount / bitLengthBigInteger / boolean / intO(n)Arbitrary-width bit operations. bitLength() is the number of bits needed — effectively ⌊log₂⌋ + 1.
compareTo(BigInteger) TRAPintO(n)Use this, never ==. equals also works but compareTo(x) == 0 is the habit to build because BigDecimal.equals is scale-sensitive.
intValue / longValue()int / longO(1)Silently truncates if it does not fit.
intValueExact / longValueExact() J8int / longO(1)Throws ArithmeticException instead of truncating. Prefer these.
toString() / toString(int radix)StringO(n²)Decimal or any base.
isProbablePrime(int certainty) / nextProbablePrime()boolean / BigIntegerprobabilisticMiller–Rabin primality. Large-prime problems; certainty=20 is ample.
 
// Factorial of 100 — 158 digits, impossible in a long
BigInteger f = BigInteger.ONE;
for (int i = 2; i <= 100; i++) f = f.multiply(BigInteger.valueOf(i));

// Fibonacci without overflow
BigInteger a = BigInteger.ZERO, b = BigInteger.ONE;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { BigInteger t = a.add(b); a = b; b = t; }

// Modular exponentiation in one call
BigInteger r = BigInteger.valueOf(base)
    .modPow(BigInteger.valueOf(exp), BigInteger.valueOf(1_000_000_007));

// Adding two numeric strings — the shortcut, and what it is a shortcut FOR
String sum = new BigInteger(num1).add(new BigInteger(num2)).toString();
// LeetCode explicitly forbids this on "Add Strings" — you are meant to write the
// digit-by-digit carry loop. Use BigInteger only to CHECK your manual answer.

// Exact perfect-square test on a huge value
BigInteger s = big.sqrt();                      // J9+
boolean perfect = s.multiply(s).equals(big);

// Chaining is verbose — this is (a*b + c) / d
BigInteger res = a.multiply(b).add(c).divide(d);

BigDecimal

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
new BigDecimal(String s) HOTBigDecimalO(len)Always construct from a String, never from a double — new BigDecimal(0.1) captures the full binary error (0.1000000000000000055511…) whereas new BigDecimal("0.1") is exactly one tenth.
BigDecimal.valueOf(double d)BigDecimalO(1)Goes via Double.toString, so it gives the "expected" shortest decimal. Safer than the double constructor.
add / subtract / multiply(BigDecimal)BigDecimalO(n) / O(n1.585)Exact. The result's scale is the sum (for multiply) of the operand scales.
divide(BigDecimal) TRAPBigDecimalO(n²)Throws ArithmeticException for a non-terminating result such as 1/3. You must supply a scale and rounding mode.
divide(BigDecimal d, int scale, RoundingMode rm) HOTBigDecimalO(n²)The safe form. a.divide(b, 10, RoundingMode.HALF_UP).
setScale(int n, RoundingMode rm) HOTBigDecimalO(n)Fixes the number of decimal places. Formatting money or a required output precision.
compareTo(BigDecimal) TRAPintO(n)You must use this, not equals. new BigDecimal("1.0").equals(new BigDecimal("1.00")) is false — equals compares scale as well as value. compareTo returns 0 for both.
stripTrailingZeros() / toPlainString()BigDecimal / StringO(n)Normalisation and scientific-notation-free output.
scale() / precision() / unscaledValue()int / int / BigIntegerO(1)Internal representation: value = unscaledValue × 10^(−scale).
◆ When do you actually need these in DSA?

Honestly: rarely. Most problems are designed so that long suffices, and the instruction "return the answer modulo 10⁹+7" exists precisely so you never need arbitrary precision. Reach for BigInteger when: the problem explicitly involves numbers beyond 64 bits (factorials, huge powers, cryptography flavoured questions); you want a one-line reference implementation to validate your manual string arithmetic; or you need exact sqrt / gcd / modPow on large values. Reach for BigDecimal essentially only for money or a mandated output precision. Both are 10–100× slower than primitives, so never put them in a hot loop.

Chapter 38

Random & randomised algorithms

Randomness shows up in three interview contexts: designing a class that returns random elements ("Insert Delete GetRandom O(1)", "Random Pick with Weight"), randomised algorithms (quickselect, reservoir sampling), and defeating adversarial test data. All three need the same small API.

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
new Random() / new Random(long seed)RandomO(1)Unseeded, or reproducible with a fixed seed. Seed it when debugging so failures repeat.
nextInt() intO(1)Any int, positive or negative.
nextInt(int bound) HOTint ∈ [0, bound)O(1)Uniform, upper bound exclusive. Throws if bound <= 0. Random index into an array: nextInt(list.size()).
nextInt(int lo, int hi) J17int ∈ [lo, hi)O(1)A range in one call — on Java 17+. Before that: lo + nextInt(hi - lo).
nextLong() / nextDouble() / nextBoolean()long / double ∈ [0,1) / booleanO(1)nextDouble() for probability thresholds; nextBoolean() for coin flips.
nextGaussian()doubleO(1)Normal distribution, mean 0, σ 1. Simulation problems.
ints / longs / doubles(long n [, lo, hi]) J8IntStream / …O(1) lazyA stream of random values. Generating stress-test input: rnd.ints(100000, 0, 1000).toArray().
Math.random()double ∈ [0,1)O(1)Convenience wrapper over a shared Random. (int)(Math.random() * n) works but nextInt(n) is better distributed and clearer.
ThreadLocalRandom.current()Random-likeO(1)Faster in concurrent code, and has the nextInt(lo, hi) overload on all versions. A good default in competitive code.
Collections.shuffle(List [, Random]) HOTvoidO(n)Correct Fisher–Yates on a list. For an int[] you must write the loop — see below.
 
// Fisher-Yates shuffle on an int[] — Collections.shuffle does not accept primitives.
// Iterate DOWNWARD and swap with a random index in [0, i]. This is the only correct form.
Random rnd = new Random();
for (int i = a.length - 1; i > 0; i--) {
    int j = rnd.nextInt(i + 1);           // inclusive of i — crucial
    int t = a[i]; a[i] = a[j]; a[j] = t;
}
// The common WRONG version uses nextInt(a.length) for every i. That produces
// a biased distribution — not all n! permutations are equally likely. Interviewers
// specifically look for this.

// "Insert Delete GetRandom O(1)" — ArrayList for O(1) random access,
// HashMap for O(1) lookup, and swap-with-last for O(1) removal.
class RandomizedSet {
    private final List<Integer> vals = new ArrayList<>();
    private final Map<Integer,Integer> idx = new HashMap<>();   // value -> position
    private final Random rnd = new Random();

    public boolean insert(int v) {
        if (idx.containsKey(v)) return false;
        idx.put(v, vals.size());
        vals.add(v);
        return true;
    }
    public boolean remove(int v) {
        Integer i = idx.remove(v);
        if (i == null) return false;
        int last = vals.size() - 1;
        if (i != last) {                          // move the last element into the hole
            int moved = vals.get(last);
            vals.set(i, moved);
            idx.put(moved, i);
        }
        vals.remove(last);                        // removing the LAST index is O(1)
        return true;
    }
    public int getRandom() { return vals.get(rnd.nextInt(vals.size())); }
}
// The swap-with-last trick is the whole insight: List.remove(middle) is O(n),
// but List.remove(lastIndex) is O(1).

// Reservoir sampling — pick k items uniformly from a stream of unknown length
int[] res = new int[k];
int seen = 0;
while (stream.hasNext()) {
    int x = stream.next();
    if (seen < k) res[seen] = x;
    else {
        int j = rnd.nextInt(seen + 1);
        if (j < k) res[j] = x;               // replace with probability k/(seen+1)
    }
    seen++;
}

// Random Pick with Weight — prefix sums + binary search
int[] prefix;                                 // cumulative weights
int pickIndex() {
    int target = rnd.nextInt(prefix[prefix.length - 1]) + 1;
    int i = Arrays.binarySearch(prefix, target);
    return i >= 0 ? i : ~i;                  // lower bound on a miss
}

// Quickselect with a random pivot — expected O(n) for "Kth Largest Element"
int quickSelect(int[] a, int lo, int hi, int k) {
    while (true) {
        int p = lo + rnd.nextInt(hi - lo + 1);   // randomise to avoid O(n^2)
        int t = a[p]; a[p] = a[hi]; a[hi] = t;
        int store = lo;
        for (int i = lo; i < hi; i++)
            if (a[i] < a[hi]) { int s = a[i]; a[i] = a[store]; a[store++] = s; }
        int s2 = a[store]; a[store] = a[hi]; a[hi] = s2;
        if (store == k) return a[store];
        if (store < k) lo = store + 1; else hi = store - 1;
    }
}
Chapter 39

StringJoiner, Objects & formatting

Three small utilities that remove boilerplate: StringJoiner for delimited output without a trailing separator, Objects for null-safe helpers and correct hashCode, and String.format for output that must match an exact shape.

StringJoiner

Method signature — inputsReturnsTimeWhat it does & where you use it
new StringJoiner(CharSequence delim)StringJoinerO(1)Delimiter only.
new StringJoiner(delim, prefix, suffix) HOTStringJoinerO(1)new StringJoiner(",", "[", "]") produces [a,b,c] — the exact format many "serialize a tree/list" problems want.
add(CharSequence el)thisO(k)Appends with the delimiter inserted between elements only — no trailing separator to trim off, which is the whole point.
setEmptyValue(CharSequence v)thisO(1)What to render when nothing was added (otherwise you get prefix + suffix).
length() / toString()int / StringO(1) / O(n)Final output.

// The problem StringJoiner solves
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (String s : items) sb.append(s).append(",");
if (sb.length() > 0) sb.setLength(sb.length() - 1);   // trim the trailing comma — ugly

StringJoiner sj = new StringJoiner(",");
for (String s : items) sj.add(s);                       // no trailing comma, ever

// Three equivalent ways to join, pick by context
String.join(",", listOfStrings);                          // simplest, needs CharSequences
list.stream().map(String::valueOf).collect(Collectors.joining(","));  // in a pipeline
new StringJoiner(",", "[", "]");                              // imperative loops

Objects — null-safe helpers

Objects.equals(Object a, Object b) HOTbooleanO(1)+Null-safe equality — equals(null, null) is true and neither argument can cause an NPE. Comparing possibly-null tree values or map results.

Objects.requireNonNullElse(T o, T def) J9TO(1)Null coalescing — Java's nearest thing to ??.

Format specifiers you will actually use

SpecifierMeaningExample → output
%dIntegerformat("%d", 42) → 42
%5dRight-aligned, width 5format("%5d", 42) → ···42 (leading spaces)
%-5dLeft-aligned, width 5format("%-5d|", 42) → 42···|
%05dZero-paddedformat("%05d", 42) → 00042
%,dGrouping separatorsformat("%,d", 1234567) → 1,234,567
%fFloat, 6 decimals by defaultformat("%f", 3.14) → 3.140000
%.2fFixed 2 decimals, roundedformat("%.2f", 3.14159) → 3.14
%eScientific notationformat("%e", 31415.9) → 3.141590e+04
%sString (calls toString)format("%s", list) → [1, 2, 3]
