▲ String.format is slow — do not put it in a loop
// This is roughly 20x slower than concatenation, because it parses the format
// string and builds a Formatter on every single call.
for (int i = 0; i < 1_000_000; i++)
    keys.add(String.format("%d,%d", r, c));        // slow

for (int i = 0; i < 1_000_000; i++)
    keys.add(r + "," + c);                        // fast

// Better still for coordinate keys — no string at all:
keys.add(r * C + c);                             // an int key
keys.add(((long) r << 32) | (c & 0xFFFFFFFFL));    // a long key
Chapter 40

Fast I/O for online judges

On LeetCode you never read input — the method signature is given. On Codeforces, AtCoder, HackerRank and university judges you do, and with 106 input tokens the difference between Scanner and a buffered reader is the difference between 4 seconds and 0.2 seconds. This chapter is the template to memorise.

Why Scanner is too slow

ApproachRelative speed reading 10⁶ intsVerdict
Scanner.nextInt()~2000 msRegex-based tokenising, unbuffered, synchronised. Too slow past ~10⁵ tokens.
BufferedReader + split~300 msFine for most problems. Allocates a String per token.
BufferedReader + StringTokenizer~150 msThe standard competitive template. Good enough for almost everything.
DataInputStream, byte-level~40 msMaximum speed. Needed only for the largest inputs.
System.out.println in a loopvery slowFlushes on every call. Always buffer output.

The template memorise this shape; it covers 99% of judge problems

import java.io.*;
import java.util.*;

public class Main {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {
        BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(System.in));
        StringBuilder out = new StringBuilder();          // buffer ALL output

        int t = Integer.parseInt(br.readLine().trim());     // number of test cases
        while (t-- > 0) {
            StringTokenizer st = new StringTokenizer(br.readLine());
            int n = Integer.parseInt(st.nextToken());
            int k = Integer.parseInt(st.nextToken());

            int[] a = new int[n];
            st = new StringTokenizer(br.readLine());
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) a[i] = Integer.parseInt(st.nextToken());

            out.append(solve(a, k)).append('\n');         // never print inside the loop
        }
        System.out.print(out);                             // ONE write at the end
    }
}

Variations you will need

// Tokens that may span lines — a StreamTokenizer handles whitespace uniformly
StreamTokenizer in = new StreamTokenizer(
    new BufferedInputStream(System.in));
in.nextToken();
int n = (int) in.nval;              // numbers arrive in the nval field as a double

// Reading until EOF (no count given)
String line;
while ((line = br.readLine()) != null) {
    if (line.isBlank()) continue;
    // ... process
}

// All whitespace-separated ints on one line, functionally
int[] a = Arrays.stream(br.readLine().trim().split("\\s+"))
                  .mapToInt(Integer::parseInt).toArray();

// PrintWriter as an alternative output buffer — remember to flush!
PrintWriter pw = new PrintWriter(new BufferedWriter(
    new OutputStreamWriter(System.out)));
pw.println(answer);
pw.flush();                          // FORGETTING THIS PRODUCES NO OUTPUT AT ALL
// — and an "empty output" verdict that looks like a crash. Use try-with-resources
// or call flush() as the last statement of main.

// Reading a grid of characters
char[][] g = new char[R][];
for (int r = 0; r < R; r++) g[r] = br.readLine().toCharArray();
◆ The maximum-speed reader, for when 150 ms is still too slow
// Reads bytes directly, parses integers by hand. Roughly 4x faster than
// StringTokenizer. Worth having memorised for AtCoder-scale input.
static final DataInputStream IN = new DataInputStream(
    new BufferedInputStream(System.in, 1 << 16));

static int nextInt() throws IOException {
    int ret = 0, b = IN.read();
    while (b < '0' && b != '-') b = IN.read();     // skip whitespace
    boolean neg = b == '-';
    if (neg) b = IN.read();
    while (b >= '0' && b <= '9') { ret = ret * 10 + (b - '0'); b = IN.read(); }
    return neg ? -ret : ret;
}
◆ On LeetCode specifically

There is no I/O. You implement a method, and the harness supplies typed arguments. What matters instead is:

  • Return the exact declared type. int[], List<List<Integer>>, String[] — Chapter 17 covers every conversion.
  • Instance fields persist between calls in "design" problems, and are reused across test cases in normal problems on some platforms. Initialise everything inside the method, not in a field, unless the problem is a design problem.
  • Do not print. Debug output slows every test case and can exceed output limits.
  • Handle the empty/null input the constraints allow. A great many failed submissions are just a missing if (n == 0) guard.
Chapter 41

Records as tuples Java 16

Java has no Pair class, and for years that meant either int[]{a, b} (fast but opaque, and broken as a hash key) or a 30-line boilerplate class. Records solve this completely: one line gives you a final class with a constructor, accessors, and correct equals, hashCode and toString. For DSA this is the single most useful modern Java feature.

What one line generates

record Point(int x, int y) {}

// The compiler generates ALL of this for you:
//   private final int x;  private final int y;
//   Point(int x, int y)                 — the canonical constructor
//   int x()   int y()                   — accessors (note: NO "get" prefix)
//   boolean equals(Object)              — compares every component
//   int hashCode()                      — combines every component
//   String toString()                   — "Point[x=1, y=2]"
// The class is final, the fields are final, and it cannot extend anything.

Point p = new Point(1, 2);
p.x()                                   // 1  — accessor, not p.getX()
p.toString()                          // "Point[x=1, y=2]" — free debugging
new Point(1,2).equals(new Point(1,2))     // true — value equality, free
◆ Why this matters: records work correctly as hash keys
// THE BUG records fix — int[] in a HashSet uses reference identity
Set<int[]> broken = new HashSet<>();
broken.add(new int[]{1,2});
broken.add(new int[]{1,2});
broken.size()                         // 2 — the set never dedupes. Silent wrong answer.
broken.contains(new int[]{1,2})    // false — you can never look anything up

// With a record it simply works
record Cell(int r, int c) {}
Set<Cell> visited = new HashSet<>();
visited.add(new Cell(1,2));
visited.add(new Cell(1,2));
visited.size()                        // 1 — correct
visited.contains(new Cell(1,2))   // true — correct

// Also usable as a Map key, which int[] cannot be:
Map<Cell,Integer> dist = new HashMap<>();
dist.put(new Cell(0,0), 0);

This is the difference between a solution that works and one that mysteriously fails on the second test case. If you are writing Java 16+ and you need a compound key, use a record.

Record features you will use

FeatureSyntaxWhat it does & where you use it
Compact declaration HOTrecord Pair(int a, int b) {}The whole class. Coordinates, edges, intervals, (value, index) pairs, DP state keys.
Local record HOTDeclared inside a methodPerfect for interviews — declare it right where you need it, no extra top-level class. Legal since Java 16.
Compact constructorrecord R(int a) { R { if (a < 0) throw …; } }Validation or normalisation without repeating the parameter list. Assign to the parameter name to normalise.
Extra constructorsR(int a) { this(a, 0); }Must delegate to the canonical constructor.
Extra methodsAny instance or static methodAdd Cell.neighbours() or Interval.overlaps(other) to keep logic next to the data.
Implement interfaces HOTrecord Task(int p) implements Comparable<Task> { … }Makes a record directly usable in a TreeSet or a comparator-free PriorityQueue.
Override a generated memberWrite your own toString, equals, or an accessorOccasionally useful; the default is nearly always what you want.
Static factorystatic R of(int a) { return new R(a); }Shorter call sites.
Record pattern J21if (o instanceof Point(int x, int y))Destructuring — see Chapter 43.
Cannot: extend a class, add instance fields, be non-final—By design. A record is data, not a hierarchy.
 
// A LOCAL record — declared inside the method that uses it. Ideal in interviews.
public int networkDelayTime(int[][] times, int n, int k) {
    record Edge(int to, int weight) {}
    record State(int node, int dist) {}

    List<List<Edge>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] t : times) adj.get(t[0]).add(new Edge(t[1], t[2]));

    int[] dist = new int[n + 1];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
    dist[k] = 0;

    PriorityQueue<State> pq = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingInt(State::dist));
    pq.offer(new State(k, 0));
    while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
        State s = pq.poll();
        if (s.dist() > dist[s.node()]) continue;          // lazy deletion
        for (Edge e : adj.get(s.node())) {
            int nd = s.dist() + e.weight();
            if (nd < dist[e.to()]) { dist[e.to()] = nd; pq.offer(new State(e.to(), nd)); }
        }
    }
    // Compare this to int[]{node, dist} everywhere — the record version is
    // self-documenting, and Comparator.comparingInt(State::dist) reads as English.
    ...
}

// A record with validation and a helper method
record Interval(int start, int end) implements Comparable<Interval> {
    Interval {                                        // compact constructor
        if (start > end) throw new IllegalArgumentException("bad interval");
    }
    boolean overlaps(Interval o) { return start < o.end && o.start < end; }
    int length()                  { return end - start; }
    @Override public int compareTo(Interval o) { return Integer.compare(start, o.start); }
}
List<Interval> iv = ...;
Collections.sort(iv);                                 // uses compareTo — no comparator needed

// Memoisation keyed on a multi-dimensional state — where records really shine
record Key(int i, int j, boolean flag) {}
Map<Key,Integer> memo = new HashMap<>();
int solve(int i, int j, boolean flag) {
    Key k = new Key(i, j, flag);
    Integer cached = memo.get(k);
    if (cached != null) return cached;
    int ans = ...;
    memo.put(k, ans);
    return ans;
}
// Previously this needed a string key ("i,j,flag") or manual bit-packing.
◆ Record vs int[] — which to use when
  • Use a record when the value goes into a HashSet/HashMap key, when there are 3+ components and the code is getting unreadable, or when you want a Comparable with meaning.
  • Use int[] in the hottest loops (no object header, no accessor call), and when the problem itself gives you int[][] so converting would cost an O(n) pass.
  • Never use int[] as a hash key or set element. That is not a performance trade-off, it is a bug.
Chapter 42

switch expressions Java 14

The arrow form removes fall-through bugs, lets switch produce a value, and — when switching on an enum or a sealed type — makes the compiler verify that you handled every case. All of it is standard in Java 17.

FeatureSyntaxWhat it does & why it matters
Arrow labels HOTcase 'a' -> doThing();No fall-through, so no break to forget. This alone eliminates a whole class of bug.
Multiple labels HOTcase '+', '-' -> …Comma-separated, replacing stacked case lines.
Expression form HOTint v = switch (x) { … };switch returns a value. Operator dispatch in a calculator, direction lookup, state transitions — as a single assignment.
yieldcase 1 -> { …; yield 42; }Returns a value from a multi-statement block. Note: yield, not return — return would exit the whole method.
Exhaustivenessover an enum or sealed typeThe compiler rejects a switch expression that misses a case, so adding an enum constant produces a compile error rather than a silent runtime fall-through.
default requiredfor non-exhaustive typesMandatory for int, String etc., because the compiler cannot prove coverage.
Switch on String J7case "add" -> …Compiles to a hash-based lookup, so it is O(1), not a chain of equals calls.
 
// OLD form — three chances to introduce a bug (missing break, missing default,
// and a mutable local that the compiler cannot verify is always assigned)
int result;
switch (op) {
    case '+': result = a + b; break;
    case '-': result = a - b; break;
    default: throw new IllegalArgumentException();
}

// NEW form — an expression, exhaustive, immutable result
int result = switch (op) {
    case '+' -> a + b;
    case '-' -> a - b;
    case '*' -> a * b;
    case '/' -> b == 0 ? 0 : a / b;
    default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException("bad op: " + op);
};

// Multiple labels + a block with yield
int daysInMonth = switch (month) {
    case 1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 10, 12 -> 31;
    case 4, 6, 9, 11            -> 30;
    case 2 -> {
        boolean leap = (year % 4 == 0 && year % 100 != 0) || year % 400 == 0;
        yield leap ? 29 : 28;                // yield, not return
    }
    default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException();
};

// Direction dispatch — replaces a 4-way if/else chain in robot and spiral problems
int[] delta = switch (dir) {
    case 'U' -> new int[]{-1, 0};
    case 'D' -> new int[]{ 1, 0};
    case 'L' -> new int[]{ 0, -1};
    case 'R' -> new int[]{ 0,  1};
    default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException();
};

// Roman numeral value — clean and O(1)
int value(char c) {
    return switch (c) {
        case 'I' -> 1;    case 'V' -> 5;    case 'X' -> 10;
        case 'L' -> 50;   case 'C' -> 100;  case 'D' -> 500;
        case 'M' -> 1000;
        default -> 0;
    };
}

// Exhaustive over an enum — no default needed, and adding a constant breaks the build
enum Dir { UP, DOWN, LEFT, RIGHT }
int dr = switch (d) {
    case UP -> -1;  case DOWN -> 1;  case LEFT, RIGHT -> 0;
};  // compiles with no default — the compiler proved all four cases are covered
◆ The old colon form still works, and still fall-throughs

You can mix neither style within one switch — it is arrows or colons, not both. The colon form retains fall-through, which is occasionally what you want (grouping cases that share trailing logic), but in interview code the arrow form is almost always clearer and safer. If you see a colon-form switch without a break, assume it is a bug until proven otherwise.

Chapter 43

Pattern matching J16 J21

Three related features: instanceof with a binding variable (Java 16, so available on 17), switch over types (Java 21), and record deconstruction (Java 21). Together they replace the cast-and-check boilerplate that used to dominate any code handling heterogeneous data — which in DSA means nested-structure problems.

Pattern instanceof Java 16 — usable on Java 17

// BEFORE — declare, check, cast. Three mentions of the type.
if (o instanceof String) {
    String s = (String) o;
    if (s.length() > 3) ...
}

// AFTER — one mention, and `s` is in scope exactly where it is valid
if (o instanceof String s && s.length() > 3) ...
// Note `s` is usable in the SAME condition after && — flow scoping makes this safe.

// The idiomatic equals() implementation
@Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
    return o instanceof Point p && p.x == x && p.y == y;
}

// Negated form — the binding is in scope in the ELSE branch and after an early return
if (!(o instanceof Point p)) return false;
// p is usable from here on — the compiler knows the method returned otherwise
return p.x == x && p.y == y;

Pattern switch Java 21

FeatureSyntaxWhat it does
Type patterncase Integer i -> …Matches by type and binds the value, no cast.
Guarded pattern (when)case Integer i when i > 10 -> …An extra boolean condition on the case. when is a contextual keyword, new in 21.
Record pattern HOTcase Point(int x, int y) -> …Destructures the record into its components in the pattern itself.
Nested record patterncase Line(Point(var x1, var y1), Point p2) -> …Arbitrary depth. Use var to let types be inferred.
case nullcase null -> …Explicitly handles null. Without it, a null selector throws NPE — the old switch behaviour is preserved by default.
defaultdefault -> …Required unless the type is sealed and every permitted subtype is covered.
 
// Type switch — replaces an if/else-if chain of instanceof checks
String describe = switch (obj) {
    case null              -> "nothing";
    case Integer i when i < 0 -> "negative int " + i;
    case Integer i           -> "int " + i;
    case String s when s.isBlank() -> "blank string";
    case String s            -> "string of length " + s.length();
    case int[] a             -> "int array of " + a.length;
    case List<?> l            -> "list of " + l.size();
    default                  -> "something else";
};
// ORDER MATTERS — a more specific / guarded case must come before the general one,
// otherwise the compiler reports the later case as unreachable ("dominated").

// Record deconstruction — no accessor calls at all
record Point(int x, int y) {}
record Line(Point a, Point b) {}

String where = switch (shape) {
    case Point(int x, int y) when x == 0 && y == 0 -> "origin";
    case Point(int x, int y)                   -> "point " + x + "," + y;
    case Line(Point(var x1, var y1), Point(var x2, var y2))
        -> "line from " + x1 + "," + y1 + " to " + x2 + "," + y2;
    default -> "unknown";
};

// Record patterns also work with instanceof
if (o instanceof Point(int x, int y) && x == y) ...    // on the diagonal

// A genuinely useful DSA application: "Flatten Nested List Iterator", where each
// element is either an integer or another nested list.
sealed interface Nested permits Num, Lst {}
record Num(int value) implements Nested {}
record Lst(List<Nested> items) implements Nested {}

void flatten(Nested n, List<Integer> out) {
    switch (n) {
        case Num(int v)              -> out.add(v);
        case Lst(List<Nested> items) -> items.forEach(i -> flatten(i, out));
    }   // NO default needed — Nested is sealed and both subtypes are covered.
}       // Adding a third implementation would break the build. That is the point.
▲ Availability

Pattern instanceof is Java 16, so it works on Java 17 and you should use it freely. Pattern switch and record patterns are Java 21 — they were preview features in 17–20 and will not compile on a Java 17 grader. LeetCode runs 21; be careful elsewhere. The Java 17 fallback for a type switch is an if / else if chain of pattern instanceof checks, which is only slightly more verbose.

Chapter 44

Sealed types Java 17

A sealed class or interface lists exactly which types may extend it. The payoff is exhaustiveness checking: a switch over a sealed type needs no default, and adding a new subtype turns every incomplete switch into a compile error instead of a runtime surprise.

FeatureSyntaxRules
sealed + permitssealed interface Expr permits Num, Add {}Only the listed types may implement it. They must be in the same package (or the same module).
Implicit permitsOmit the clause when all subtypes are in the same fileThe compiler infers the permitted set. The form you will write in a single-file interview solution.
Subtype must be final, sealed, or non-sealedrecord Num(int v) implements Expr {}A record is implicitly final, so records are the natural partners for sealed interfaces.
Exhaustive switchNo default branch neededThe compiler verifies coverage. This is the whole reason to seal.
 
// An algebraic data type in Java — the sealed interface + records combination.
// This is the clean way to model an expression tree, which shows up in
// "Basic Calculator", "Different Ways to Add Parentheses" and parser questions.
sealed interface Expr {}
record Num(int value)                implements Expr {}
record Add(Expr left, Expr right)     implements Expr {}
record Mul(Expr left, Expr right)     implements Expr {}
record Neg(Expr inner)               implements Expr {}

static int eval(Expr e) {
    return switch (e) {                            // J21 pattern switch
        case Num(int v)            -> v;
        case Add(Expr l, Expr r)   -> eval(l) + eval(r);
        case Mul(Expr l, Expr r)   -> eval(l) * eval(r);
        case Neg(Expr i)           -> -eval(i);
    };   // no default — and if you add record Sub, THIS LINE fails to compile
}

Expr tree = new Add(new Num(3), new Mul(new Num(4), new Num(5)));
eval(tree)                                         // 23

// Java 17 version (no pattern switch) — still benefits from sealing for documentation,
// but you must use instanceof patterns and the compiler cannot prove exhaustiveness.
static int eval17(Expr e) {
    if (e instanceof Num n) return n.value();
    if (e instanceof Add a) return eval17(a.left()) + eval17(a.right());
    if (e instanceof Mul m) return eval17(m.left()) * eval17(m.right());
    if (e instanceof Neg g) return -eval17(g.inner());
    throw new IllegalStateException();              // unreachable, but required
}
◆ Is this worth knowing for interviews?

For coding rounds: rarely decisive, but it makes tree- and expression-shaped problems genuinely cleaner, and typing it fluently signals current Java knowledge. For design and "tell me about modern Java" rounds: yes. Sealed types plus records plus pattern matching are the three features that together bring algebraic data types to Java, and being able to explain that connection — and why exhaustiveness checking shifts errors from runtime to compile time — is a strong answer.

Chapter 45

Text blocks, var & small wins

A handful of smaller features that reduce typing. In a timed interview, less typing means more thinking — and var in particular removes the worst of Java's generic verbosity.

var — local type inference Java 10

Where var worksWhere it does not
Local variables with an initialiserFields (instance or static)
for and enhanced-for loop variablesMethod parameters
try-with-resources variablesReturn types
Lambda parameters J11 (all or none)Anything initialised to null, or with no initialiser
 
// The verbosity var removes
Map<String, List<Map.Entry<Integer, String>>> index = new HashMap<>();
var index = new HashMap<String, List<Map.Entry<Integer, String>>>();

// Where var genuinely helps in DSA code
for (var e : map.entrySet()) { ... }            // instead of Map.Entry<K,V> e
var pq = new PriorityQueue<int[]>(Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[1]));
var stats = Arrays.stream(nums).summaryStatistics();
var memo = new HashMap<String, List<Integer>>();

// Where var HURTS readability — do not do this
var x = compute();                    // what type is x? the reader cannot tell
var n = 0;                            // int — but just write int, it is shorter
var list = new ArrayList<>();          // ArrayList<Object> — the diamond infers Object!

// The rule of thumb: use var when the initialiser makes the type OBVIOUS
// (a constructor call or a well-known factory), and spell the type out otherwise.
// Note that declaring to the interface is impossible with var — `var list = new
// ArrayList<Integer>()` gives you the concrete type, not List<Integer>.

Text blocks Java 15

// Multi-line string literals — no escaping, no concatenation
String grid = """
    ..#..
    .###.
    ..#..
    """;
// Rules: the opening """ must be followed by a newline. Incidental leading
// whitespace is stripped based on the LEAST-indented line (including the closing """),
// so you can indent the block to match your code.

// Practical use in DSA: hand-writing test input
String[] rows = """
    1 2 3
    4 5 6
    7 8 9
    """.lines().toArray(String[]::new);

int[][] matrix = """
    1 2 3
    4 5 6
    """.lines()
     .map(l -> Arrays.stream(l.trim().split("\\s+"))
                    .mapToInt(Integer::parseInt).toArray())
     .toArray(int[][]::new);

// Escape sequences unique to text blocks:
//   \    at end of line = suppress the newline (join lines)
//   \s   = a literal space that survives whitespace stripping

Other quality-of-life features worth knowing

FeatureVersionExample & DSA relevance
Diamond on anonymous classesJ9new Comparator<>() { … } — the type argument is inferred.
Numeric underscoresJ71_000_000_007 — write the modulus constant readably. Use this every time; it prevents miscounting zeros.
Binary literalsJ70b1010_1010 — for masks where the bit pattern is the point.
Enhanced instanceofJ16See Chapter 43. Available on Java 17.
Helpful NullPointerExceptionsJ14The message names the exact expression that was null — Cannot invoke "String.length()" because "<local1>" is null. Enormously faster debugging; on by default since 15.
Stream.toList()J16Shorter than collect(Collectors.toList()) — but immutable. See Chapter 31.

Chapter 46

The full Java 8 → 21 feature list

A version-by-version reference so you always know what is safe to type on a given grader. The Relevance column says whether it matters for DSA specifically — most language features do not, and it is worth knowing which do.

Language features

VersionFeatureRelevance to DSA
8Lambdas, method references, streams, default methods, Optional, Comparator factories, Map.merge / computeIfAbsent / getOrDefaultEssential. The single biggest release for everyday Java.
9List.of / Set.of / Map.of, takeWhile / dropWhile, Stream.iterate (3-arg), Optional.stream, Matcher.results, diamond on anonymous classes, private interface methodsHigh. The immutable factories and takeWhile are used constantly.

◆ What to type on which platform
  • LeetCode — Java 21. Everything in this document is available. Use records, getLast(), pattern switch.
  • Codeforces — Java 21 (and an older 8 option). Records and Sequenced Collections are fine; use fast I/O from Chapter 40.
  • HackerRank / most company assessment platforms — commonly Java 8, 11 or 17. Assume 17 unless told otherwise, which means: records yes, pattern instanceof yes, pattern switch and getLast() no.
  • A whiteboard or a shared doc — nothing compiles, so write whatever is clearest and say which version you are assuming. Mentioning "on 21 I would write list.getLast() here" is a small, cheap signal of currency.

The safe default for practice: write Java 17 by habit, and know the Java 21 shortcuts as improvements you can apply when the platform allows. That way you are never blocked, and you are never out of date.

Chapter 47

ListNode & TreeNode

Linked-list and tree problems never use java.util classes. You are handed a bare node with pointers, and every solution is pointer manipulation. The techniques below — dummy heads, fast/slow pointers, and the three traversal orders — cover essentially the entire genre.

The two class definitions

// Singly-linked list node — the LeetCode standard definition
class ListNode {
    int val;
    ListNode next;
    ListNode()                          { }
    ListNode(int val)                  { this.val = val; }
    ListNode(int val, ListNode next)   { this.val = val; this.next = next; }
}

// Binary tree node
class TreeNode {
    int val;
    TreeNode left, right;
    TreeNode()                                           { }
    TreeNode(int val)                                   { this.val = val; }
    TreeNode(int val, TreeNode left, TreeNode right)    {
        this.val = val; this.left = left; this.right = right;
    }
}

Linked-list techniques

◆ Technique 1 — the dummy head

Whenever the head itself might change (removal, insertion at the front, merging), allocate a dummy node in front. It eliminates every special case for "what if this is the first node", which is where linked-list bugs live.

// Remove all nodes with a given value — no head special-casing at all
ListNode removeElements(ListNode head, int val) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
    ListNode cur = dummy;
    while (cur.next != null) {
        if (cur.next.val == val) cur.next = cur.next.next;   // unlink
        else cur = cur.next;                                // advance
    }
    return dummy.next;        // NOT head — head may have been removed
}

// Merge two sorted lists
ListNode merge(ListNode a, ListNode b) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(), tail = dummy;
    while (a != null && b != null) {
        if (a.val <= b.val) { tail.next = a; a = a.next; }
        else                { tail.next = b; b = b.next; }
        tail = tail.next;
    }
    tail.next = (a != null) ? a : b;      // attach whatever remains — no loop needed
    return dummy.next;
}
◆ Technique 2 — fast & slow pointers (Floyd)
// Middle of the list. When fast reaches the end, slow is at the midpoint.
ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
while (fast != null && fast.next != null) { slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next; }
// For an even-length list this lands on the SECOND middle node. To get the first,
// use `while (fast.next != null && fast.next.next != null)`.

// Cycle detection — O(1) space, the answer interviewers want
boolean hasCycle(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next;
        fast = fast.next.next;
        if (slow == fast) return true;      // reference equality — correct here
    }
    return false;
}

// Find the cycle's ENTRY point. After they meet, reset one pointer to head and
// advance both one step at a time; they meet at the entry. (Floyd's second phase.)
ListNode detectCycle(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next;
        if (slow == fast) {
            slow = head;
            while (slow != fast) { slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next; }
            return slow;
        }
    }
    return null;
}

// Nth node from the end — gap of n, then walk both to the end
ListNode lead = head;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) lead = lead.next;
ListNode trail = head;
while (lead != null) { lead = lead.next; trail = trail.next; }
// trail is now the nth node from the end
◆ Technique 3 — in-place reversal
// Reverse a whole list iteratively. Memorise this exact four-line body.
ListNode reverse(ListNode head) {
    ListNode prev = null, cur = head;
    while (cur != null) {
        ListNode next = cur.next;     // 1. remember where we were going
        cur.next = prev;               // 2. flip the pointer backwards
        prev = cur;                    // 3. advance prev
        cur = next;                    // 4. advance cur
    }
    return prev;                      // prev is the new head; cur is null
}

// Recursive version — elegant, O(n) stack
ListNode reverseRec(ListNode head) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return head;
    ListNode newHead = reverseRec(head.next);
    head.next.next = head;
    head.next = null;
    return newHead;
}

// Palindrome check in O(1) space: find the middle, reverse the second half, compare
boolean isPalindrome(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) { slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next; }
    ListNode second = reverse(slow);
    ListNode p = head, q = second;
    while (q != null) { if (p.val != q.val) return false; p = p.next; q = q.next; }
    return true;
}

Tree traversals — all six forms

// ── PRE-ORDER: node, left, right ──────────────────────────────────────
void preorder(TreeNode n, List<Integer> out) {
    if (n == null) return;
    out.add(n.val);
    preorder(n.left, out);
    preorder(n.right, out);
}
// Iterative — push RIGHT first so LEFT pops first
Deque<TreeNode> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
if (root != null) st.push(root);
while (!st.isEmpty()) {
    TreeNode n = st.pop();
    out.add(n.val);
    if (n.right != null) st.push(n.right);
    if (n.left  != null) st.push(n.left);
}

// ── IN-ORDER: left, node, right ───────────────────────────────────────
// On a BST this yields SORTED order — the single most useful tree fact.
void inorder(TreeNode n, List<Integer> out) {
    if (n == null) return;
    inorder(n.left, out);
    out.add(n.val);
    inorder(n.right, out);
}
// Iterative — dive left, visit, go right
Deque<TreeNode> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
TreeNode cur = root;
while (cur != null || !st.isEmpty()) {
    while (cur != null) { st.push(cur); cur = cur.left; }
    cur = st.pop();
    out.add(cur.val);
    cur = cur.right;
}

// ── POST-ORDER: left, right, node ─────────────────────────────────────
// The order for anything that needs CHILD results first: height, diameter, deletion,
// "is this subtree a BST", sum of subtree values.
void postorder(TreeNode n, List<Integer> out) {
    if (n == null) return;
    postorder(n.left, out);
    postorder(n.right, out);
    out.add(n.val);
}
// Iterative trick: do a REVERSED pre-order (node, right, left) then reverse the result
Deque<TreeNode> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
LinkedList<Integer> out = new LinkedList<>();
if (root != null) st.push(root);
while (!st.isEmpty()) {
    TreeNode n = st.pop();
    out.addFirst(n.val);                   // prepend instead of append
    if (n.left  != null) st.push(n.left);
    if (n.right != null) st.push(n.right);
}

// ── LEVEL-ORDER (BFS) ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
List<List<Integer>> levelOrder(TreeNode root) {
    List<List<Integer>> res = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return res;
    Deque<TreeNode> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
    q.offer(root);
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int sz = q.size();                  // snapshot the level boundary
        List<Integer> level = new ArrayList<>(sz);
        for (int i = 0; i < sz; i++) {
            TreeNode n = q.poll();
            level.add(n.val);
            if (n.left  != null) q.offer(n.left);
            if (n.right != null) q.offer(n.right);
        }
        res.add(level);
    }
    return res;
}
◆ The post-order return-value pattern

An enormous family of tree problems ("Diameter", "Balanced Binary Tree", "Maximum Path Sum", "Longest Univalue Path", "House Robber III") uses the same shape: the recursion returns one thing to the parent while updating a global answer on the way up. Recognising this shape is worth more than memorising the individual problems.

private int best = 0;                    // the global answer

public int diameterOfBinaryTree(TreeNode root) {
    depth(root);
    return best;
}
// returns the DEPTH to the parent, but updates `best` with the PATH THROUGH this node
private int depth(TreeNode n) {
    if (n == null) return 0;
    int l = depth(n.left), r = depth(n.right);
    best = Math.max(best, l + r);            // path through n — not returned
    return 1 + Math.max(l, r);                // depth — what the parent needs
}
// The insight: what the parent needs and what the answer needs are DIFFERENT values.
// Return one, accumulate the other. Say this out loud in an interview.

// A mutable holder avoids the field when you prefer a pure function:
int[] best = {0};                        // or use an int[1] parameter
◆ BST-specific facts worth memorising
  • In-order traversal of a BST is sorted. This single fact solves "Validate BST" (check the sequence is strictly increasing), "Kth Smallest Element in a BST" (stop after k), and "Convert BST to Sorted Doubly Linked List".
  • Validate BST with bounds, not with parent comparison. Checking only node.left.val < node.val is wrong — every node must be inside the range inherited from its ancestors. Pass (long min, long max) down; use long so Integer.MIN_VALUE as a node value does not break the bounds.
  • Search / insert / delete are O(h), which is O(log n) only if the tree is balanced. A BST built from sorted input degenerates into a linked list.
  • Lowest Common Ancestor in a BST is O(h) and needs no recursion: walk down, going left while both targets are smaller, right while both are larger; the first node that splits them is the LCA.
Chapter 48

Trie (prefix tree)

Java has no trie. This implementation is a memorisation target: it appears directly in "Implement Trie", "Word Search II", "Design Add and Search Words", "Replace Words", "Longest Word in Dictionary", and it is the accelerating structure behind several otherwise-quadratic problems.

The canonical array-based implementation

class Trie {
    private static class Node {
        Node[] kids = new Node[26];       // index with c - 'a'
        boolean isWord;                    // does a word END here?
        int passCount;                     // optional: how many words pass through
    }
    private final Node root = new Node();

    public void insert(String word) {
        Node cur = root;
        for (char c : word.toCharArray()) {
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (cur.kids[i] == null) cur.kids[i] = new Node();
            cur = cur.kids[i];
            cur.passCount++;
        }
        cur.isWord = true;
    }
    public boolean search(String word) {
        Node n = find(word);
        return n != null && n.isWord;       // the isWord check is the whole difference...
    }
    public boolean startsWith(String prefix) {
        return find(prefix) != null;         // ...between search and startsWith
    }
    private Node find(String s) {
        Node cur = root;
        for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
            cur = cur.kids[c - 'a'];
            if (cur == null) return null;
        }
        return cur;
    }
}
// insert / search / startsWith are all O(L) where L is the word length —
// INDEPENDENT of how many words are stored. That is the whole point of a trie.

Variations you will need

VariationChangeWhen
HashMap childrenMap<Character,Node> kidsAlphabet is large or unknown (Unicode, mixed case, arbitrary symbols). Slower constant factor, much less memory when sparse.
Store the word at the terminal nodeString word; instead of boolean isWord"Word Search II" — when you reach a terminal you want the word itself without reconstructing it from the path.
Pass / word countsint passCount, endCount"How many words start with this prefix", and correct deletion.
Binary trie (0/1)Node[] kids = new Node[2], insert bits high→lowMaximum XOR problems — "Maximum XOR of Two Numbers in an Array" becomes O(32n) by greedily walking toward the opposite bit at each level.
Suffix insertionInsert every suffix of every wordSubstring queries rather than prefix queries.
Reversed insertionInsert words backwards"Stream of Characters" — match a suffix of the incoming stream against stored words.
 
// Wildcard search — "Design Add and Search Words Data Structure".
// '.' matches any single character, so you must branch over all 26 children.
private boolean dfs(String w, int i, Node n) {
    if (n == null) return false;
    if (i == w.length()) return n.isWord;
    char c = w.charAt(i);
    if (c == '.') {
        for (Node k : n.kids) if (dfs(w, i + 1, k)) return true;
        return false;
    }
    return dfs(w, i + 1, n.kids[c - 'a']);
}

// Word Search II — the classic trie + grid backtracking combination.
// Without the trie you would run a separate DFS per word: O(W * R * C * 4^L).
// With it, ONE DFS explores the grid while walking the trie in parallel.
void dfs(char[][] board, int r, int c, Node node, List<String> out) {
    if (r < 0 || r >= R || c < 0 || c >= C) return;
    char ch = board[r][c];
    if (ch == '#') return;                      // already on the current path
    Node nxt = node.kids[ch - 'a'];
    if (nxt == null) return;                     // PRUNE — no word has this prefix
    if (nxt.word != null) { out.add(nxt.word); nxt.word = null; }  // dedupe

    board[r][c] = '#';                          // mark
    dfs(board, r+1, c, nxt, out);
    dfs(board, r-1, c, nxt, out);
    dfs(board, r, c+1, nxt, out);
    dfs(board, r, c-1, nxt, out);
    board[r][c] = ch;                            // restore
}
// The `if (nxt == null) return;` line is the entire speed-up: it abandons a branch
// the instant the path stops being a prefix of any dictionary word.

// Binary trie for Maximum XOR — insert 32 bits from most significant down
class XorTrie {
    XorTrie[] kids = new XorTrie[2];
    void insert(int num) {
        XorTrie cur = this;
        for (int b = 31; b >= 0; b--) {
            int bit = (num >> b) & 1;
            if (cur.kids[bit] == null) cur.kids[bit] = new XorTrie();
            cur = cur.kids[bit];
        }
    }
    int maxXor(int num) {
        XorTrie cur = this;
        int res = 0;
        for (int b = 31; b >= 0; b--) {
            int bit = (num >> b) & 1, want = bit ^ 1;   // greedily take the OPPOSITE bit
            if (cur.kids[want] != null) { res |= 1 << b; cur = cur.kids[want]; }
            else cur = cur.kids[bit];
        }
        return res;
    }
}
◆ When a trie beats a HashSet

A HashSet<String> gives O(L) exact lookup too, so a trie is not faster for "is this word in the dictionary". The trie wins when you need prefix operations: enumerate everything with a given prefix, count words with a prefix, find the longest stored prefix of a query, or — most importantly — prune a search the moment the current path stops being any word's prefix. That last use is why "Word Search II" is a trie problem and not a set problem.

Chapter 49

Union-Find / Disjoint Set Union

Answers "are these two things in the same group?" in effectively constant amortised time. Short enough to write from memory in ninety seconds, and it is the intended solution for a whole class of problems: connected components, Kruskal's MST, account merging, redundant-connection detection, and "number of islands" with dynamic merging.

The implementation to memorise

class DSU {
    private final int[] parent, rank;
    private int components;

    DSU(int n) {
        parent = new int[n];
        rank   = new int[n];
        Arrays.setAll(parent, i -> i);        // every node is its own parent
        components = n;
    }
    // PATH COMPRESSION — flatten the tree on the way back up
    int find(int x) {
        if (parent[x] != x) parent[x] = find(parent[x]);
        return parent[x];
    }
    // UNION BY RANK — always attach the shorter tree under the taller one
    boolean union(int a, int b) {
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) return false;             // already connected — a CYCLE
        if (rank[ra] < rank[rb]) { int t = ra; ra = rb; rb = t; }
        parent[rb] = ra;
        if (rank[ra] == rank[rb]) rank[ra]++;
        components--;
        return true;
    }
    boolean connected(int a, int b) { return find(a) == find(b); }
    int     count()                     { return components; }
}
// With both path compression AND union by rank, m operations on n elements cost
// O(m * α(n)) where α is the inverse Ackermann function — under 5 for any n you
// will ever see. Treat it as O(1) amortised, but SAY "near-constant, O(α(n))".
◆ The three details interviewers listen for
  1. Path compression — parent[x] = find(parent[x]). Without it, find is O(n) in the worst case. This one line is the difference between a passing and a timing-out solution.
  2. Union by rank (or size) — attaching the smaller tree under the larger keeps the depth logarithmic even before compression. Either heuristic works; rank tracks height, size tracks node count, and size is often more useful because problems frequently ask for the size of the largest component.
  3. union returns a boolean. false means "already in the same set", which is cycle detection. "Redundant Connection" is literally "return the first edge whose union returns false"; Kruskal's algorithm is "sort edges by weight, keep the ones where union returns true".

Common adaptations

// Track component SIZES instead of rank — needed for "Largest Component"
class DSU {
    int[] parent, size;
    DSU(int n) {
        parent = new int[n]; size = new int[n];
        Arrays.setAll(parent, i -> i);
        Arrays.fill(size, 1);
    }
    int find(int x) { while (parent[x] != x) { parent[x] = parent[parent[x]]; x = parent[x]; } return x; }
    // ^ iterative "path halving" — no recursion, no stack-overflow risk for huge n
    boolean union(int a, int b) {
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) return false;
        if (size[ra] < size[rb]) { int t = ra; ra = rb; rb = t; }
        parent[rb] = ra;
        size[ra] += size[rb];
        return true;
    }
    int sizeOf(int x) { return size[find(x)]; }
}

// STRING keys — map each label to an integer id, then use the int DSU
Map<String,Integer> id = new HashMap<>();
int idOf(String s) { return id.computeIfAbsent(s, k -> id.size()); }
// computeIfAbsent with id.size() as the value assigns 0, 1, 2, ... automatically.
// This is the standard "Accounts Merge" / "Sentence Similarity" / currency-conversion setup.

// GRID cells — flatten (r, c) to r * C + c
DSU dsu = new DSU(R * C);
dsu.union(r * C + c, nr * C + nc);

// Number of Islands with DSU (instead of DFS)
DSU dsu = new DSU(R * C + 1);           // +1 for a virtual "water" node
int land = 0;
for (int r = 0; r < R; r++)
  for (int c = 0; c < C; c++) {
    if (grid[r][c] == '0') continue;
    land++;
    if (r > 0 && grid[r-1][c] == '1' && dsu.union(r*C+c, (r-1)*C+c)) land--;
    if (c > 0 && grid[r][c-1] == '1' && dsu.union(r*C+c, r*C+c-1)) land--;
  }
return land;
// Each successful union merges two components, so decrementing on a successful
// union counts the components without a second pass.

// Kruskal's Minimum Spanning Tree — DSU's headline application
Arrays.sort(edges, Comparator.comparingInt(e -> e[2]));   // by weight
DSU dsu = new DSU(n);
int total = 0, used = 0;
for (int[] e : edges)
    if (dsu.union(e[0], e[1])) { total += e[2]; if (++used == n - 1) break; }
return used == n - 1 ? total : -1;        // -1 if the graph is disconnected

// DSU with WEIGHTS / offsets — "Evaluate Division", "Satisfiability of Equations"
// Store a ratio to the parent alongside the parent pointer, and multiply through
// during path compression. This is the advanced variant; know that it exists.
◆ DSU vs DFS/BFS for connectivity

If the graph is static and you just need the components once, DFS or BFS is simpler and equally fast — use it. DSU wins when edges arrive incrementally and you must answer connectivity queries as you go ("Number of Islands II", "Connecting Cities"), or when you need the cycle-detection signal from union. DSU cannot easily handle edge deletion — if a problem removes edges, the usual trick is to process the whole thing in reverse.

Chapter 50

Fenwick Tree (Binary Indexed Tree)

Prefix sums that support updates: both update and query are O(log n), where a plain prefix-sum array gives O(1) query but O(n) update. Twelve lines of code, and the structure behind "Range Sum Query — Mutable", "Count of Smaller Numbers After Self", and counting inversions.

The implementation

class Fenwick {
    private final long[] tree;                // 1-INDEXED — index 0 is unused
    private final int n;

    Fenwick(int n) { this.n = n; tree = new long[n + 1]; }

    // Add delta at position i (0-based input, converted internally)
    void update(int i, long delta) {
        for (int p = i + 1; p <= n; p += p & -p)   // move to the next responsible node
            tree[p] += delta;
    }
    // Sum of [0, i] inclusive
    long prefix(int i) {
        long s = 0;
        for (int p = i + 1; p > 0; p -= p & -p)     // strip the lowest set bit
            s += tree[p];
        return s;
    }
    // Sum of [l, r] inclusive
    long range(int l, int r) { return prefix(r) - (l > 0 ? prefix(l - 1) : 0); }

    // O(n) bulk construction — better than n separate updates (O(n log n))
    Fenwick(int[] a) {
        n = a.length;
        tree = new long[n + 1];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            tree[i + 1] += a[i];
            int parent = (i + 1) + ((i + 1) & -(i + 1));
            if (parent <= n) tree[parent] += tree[i + 1];
        }
    }
}
◆ Why p & -p is the whole algorithm

p & -p isolates the lowest set bit of p (it is exactly Integer.lowestOneBit(p), from Chapter 03). Node p in the tree is responsible for the p & -p elements ending at p.

  • p += p & -p (update) walks up to every node whose range covers position p. Each step clears one bit and sets a higher one, so there are at most log n steps.
  • p -= p & -p (query) walks down, peeling off one set bit at a time and accumulating disjoint ranges. Since p has at most log n set bits, the sum needs at most log n additions.

The 1-indexing is not optional. With p = 0, p & -p is 0 and both loops spin forever. Every real implementation is 1-indexed internally; convert at the boundary, as above.

Applications

// Range Sum Query — Mutable (LeetCode 307). The textbook use.
class NumArray {
    private final Fenwick bit;
    private final int[] a;
    NumArray(int[] nums) { a = nums.clone(); bit = new Fenwick(nums); }
    void update(int i, int val) { bit.update(i, val - a[i]); a[i] = val; }
    int  sumRange(int l, int r) { return (int) bit.range(l, r); }
}
// Note update takes a DELTA, so you must remember the old value to compute it.

// Counting inversions in O(n log n) — the classic non-obvious application.
// Sweep right to left; for each element, ask how many already-seen values are smaller.
long countInversions(int[] a) {
    int[] sorted = a.clone();
    Arrays.sort(sorted);
    Fenwick bit = new Fenwick(a.length);
    long inv = 0;
    for (int i = a.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        int rank = Arrays.binarySearch(sorted, a[i]);    // coordinate compression
        inv += bit.prefix(rank - 1);                      // strictly smaller, already seen
        bit.update(rank, 1);
    }
    return inv;
}
// "Count of Smaller Numbers After Self" is the same sweep, recording the count
// per index instead of accumulating a total.

// COORDINATE COMPRESSION — the companion technique. A Fenwick tree is indexed by
// POSITION, so when your values are huge or sparse you must first map them to
// dense ranks 0..k-1.
int[] sorted = Arrays.stream(a).distinct().sorted().toArray();
int rank = Arrays.binarySearch(sorted, value);          // 0-based dense rank
// Or with a map:
Map<Integer,Integer> rankOf = new HashMap<>();
for (int i = 0; i < sorted.length; i++) rankOf.put(sorted[i], i);

// 2-D Fenwick — for "Range Sum Query 2D — Mutable"
class Fenwick2D {
    long[][] t; int R, C;
    Fenwick2D(int R, int C) { this.R = R; this.C = C; t = new long[R+1][C+1]; }
    void update(int r, int c, long d) {
        for (int i = r+1; i <= R; i += i & -i)
            for (int j = c+1; j <= C; j += j & -j) t[i][j] += d;
    }
    long prefix(int r, int c) {
        long s = 0;
        for (int i = r+1; i > 0; i -= i & -i)
            for (int j = c+1; j > 0; j -= j & -j) s += t[i][j];
        return s;
    }
    long range(int r1, int c1, int r2, int c2) {   // inclusion-exclusion
        return prefix(r2,c2) - prefix(r1-1,c2) - prefix(r2,c1-1) + prefix(r1-1,c1-1);
    }
}
◆ Prefix sums vs Fenwick vs Segment Tree — pick correctly
StructureBuildPoint updateRange queryUse when
Prefix-sum arrayO(n)O(n)O(1)No updates. Always first choice if the array is static.
Difference arrayO(n)O(1) range addO(n) to finaliseMany range updates, then read everything once.
Fenwick / BITO(n)O(log n)O(log n)Interleaved point updates and prefix/range sums. Shortest code.
Segment TreeO(n)O(log n)O(log n)You need min / max / gcd, or range updates with lazy propagation. More code.

A Fenwick tree only works for operations with an inverse (addition, XOR) because range(l,r) is computed as prefix(r) − prefix(l−1). You cannot do range-minimum with one — that needs a segment tree.

Chapter 51

Segment Tree

The general-purpose range-query structure: any associative operation (sum, min, max, gcd, OR) over any range, in O(log n), with updates. Longer than a Fenwick tree, but strictly more capable. Rare in a 45-minute interview, standard in competitive programming and in "hard" tier problems.

Iterative segment tree the short, fast form — memorise this one

// Array-based, 1-indexed internal nodes, leaves stored at [n, 2n).
// No recursion, no lazy propagation, about 20 lines.
class SegTree {
    private final int n;
    private final long[] t;

    SegTree(int[] a) {
        n = a.length;
        t = new long[2 * n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) t[n + i] = a[i];           // leaves
        for (int i = n - 1; i > 0; i--) t[i] = combine(t[2*i], t[2*i+1]);
    }
    private long combine(long a, long b) { return a + b; }     // swap for min/max/gcd

    // Set position i to value v
    void set(int i, long v) {
        for (t[i += n] = v; i > 1; i >>= 1)
            t[i >> 1] = combine(t[i], t[i ^ 1]);                // i^1 is the sibling
    }
    // Query [l, r) — LEFT INCLUSIVE, RIGHT EXCLUSIVE
    long query(int l, int r) {
        long resL = 0, resR = 0;                              // identity for the operation
        for (l += n, r += n; l < r; l >>= 1, r >>= 1) {
            if ((l & 1) == 1) resL = combine(resL, t[l++]);      // l is a right child
            if ((r & 1) == 1) resR = combine(t[--r], resR);      // r is a right child
        }
        return combine(resL, resR);
    }
}
// For MIN: combine = Math.min, identity = Long.MAX_VALUE.
// For MAX: combine = Math.max, identity = Long.MIN_VALUE.
// For GCD: combine = gcd, identity = 0.
// Keeping resL and resR separate matters for NON-COMMUTATIVE operations
// (like matrix multiplication or string concatenation) — order is preserved.

Recursive segment tree with lazy propagation when you need RANGE updates

// Supports "add v to every element in [l, r]" and "sum of [l, r]", both O(log n).
class LazySegTree {
    private final int n;
    private final long[] sum, lazy;

    LazySegTree(int n) { this.n = n; sum = new long[4*n]; lazy = new long[4*n]; }
    // 4n is the safe size for a recursive segment tree — not 2n.

    private void push(int node, int lo, int hi) {
        if (lazy[node] == 0) return;
        sum[node] += lazy[node] * (hi - lo + 1);
        if (lo != hi) {                                // propagate to children
            lazy[2*node]   += lazy[node];
            lazy[2*node+1] += lazy[node];
        }
        lazy[node] = 0;
    }
    void update(int node, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, long v) {
        push(node, lo, hi);
        if (r < lo || hi < l) return;                 // no overlap
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) {                      // total cover — stop here
            lazy[node] += v;
            push(node, lo, hi);
            return;
        }
        int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
        update(2*node, lo, mid, l, r, v);
        update(2*node+1, mid+1, hi, l, r, v);
        sum[node] = sum[2*node] + sum[2*node+1];
    }
    long query(int node, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) {
        push(node, lo, hi);
        if (r < lo || hi < l) return 0;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return sum[node];
        int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
        return query(2*node, lo, mid, l, r)
             + query(2*node+1, mid+1, hi, l, r);
    }
    // Call as: update(1, 0, n-1, l, r, v)  and  query(1, 0, n-1, l, r)
}
◆ Is a segment tree worth learning for interviews?

For most company interviews: no. Nobody expects a lazy-propagating segment tree in 45 minutes. What is expected is that you can recognise when a problem needs one and say so: "this needs range queries with updates, so a segment tree or a Fenwick tree — Fenwick if it is just sums, segment tree if I need range minimum." That recognition scores nearly as well as the implementation.

For competitive programming: yes, absolutely. Learn the iterative version cold; it is short enough to type in two minutes and covers most needs. Add lazy propagation once the iterative form is automatic.

The pragmatic middle ground: a TreeMap handles a surprising number of "range" problems (Chapter 22), and a plain prefix-sum array handles all of the static ones. Try those first.

Chapter 52

Graph representations & traversals

Java has no graph class, so the first decision in every graph problem is the representation. Get this right and the algorithm writes itself; get it wrong and you fight the data structure for twenty minutes.

The four representations

RepresentationDeclarationSpaceUse when
Adjacency list — array of ListsList<Integer>[] adj = new List[n];O(V+E)The default. Node ids are 0..n−1, which is nearly always the case.
Adjacency list — List of ListsList<List<Integer>> adjO(V+E)Same thing without the unchecked-cast warning. Slightly more verbose to initialise.
Adjacency mapMap<String,List<String>> adjO(V+E)Nodes are strings or sparse ids — word ladders, course names, currency codes.
Adjacency matrixboolean[][] or int[][]O(V²)Dense graphs, V ≤ ~1000, or when you need O(1) "is there an edge u→v". Also the natural input format for Floyd–Warshall.
 
// Building an adjacency list — the idiom to have automatic
List<Integer>[] adj = new List[n];              // unchecked warning is expected and fine
Arrays.setAll(adj, i -> new ArrayList<>());        // J8 one-liner initialisation
for (int[] e : edges) {
    adj[e[0]].add(e[1]);
    adj[e[1]].add(e[0]);                         // omit this line for a DIRECTED graph
}

// Weighted — store {neighbour, weight} pairs, or use a record
List<int[]>[] wadj = new List[n];
Arrays.setAll(wadj, i -> new ArrayList<>());
for (int[] e : edges) wadj[e[0]].add(new int[]{e[1], e[2]});

// String-keyed — computeIfAbsent builds the lists as it goes
Map<String,List<String>> g = new HashMap<>();
for (List<String> p : pairs) {
    g.computeIfAbsent(p.get(0), k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(p.get(1));
    g.computeIfAbsent(p.get(1), k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(p.get(0));
}

The traversal templates

// ── BFS — shortest path in an UNWEIGHTED graph ────────────────────────
int[] dist = new int[n];
Arrays.fill(dist, -1);                          // -1 doubles as "unvisited"
Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
q.offer(src);
dist[src] = 0;
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    int u = q.poll();
    for (int v : adj[u])
        if (dist[v] == -1) {                     // mark visited WHEN ENQUEUEING...
            dist[v] = dist[u] + 1;
            q.offer(v);
        }
}
// ...NOT when dequeueing. Marking on dequeue lets a node be enqueued many times,
// which turns O(V+E) into something much worse. This is the #1 BFS bug.

// ── MULTI-SOURCE BFS — seed the queue with every source ───────────────
for (int s : sources) { dist[s] = 0; q.offer(s); }
// Then run the identical loop. Solves "Rotting Oranges", "01 Matrix",
// "Walls and Gates" — all of which look hard until you see they are one BFS.

// ── DFS recursive ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
void dfs(int u, boolean[] seen) {
    seen[u] = true;
    for (int v : adj[u]) if (!seen[v]) dfs(v, seen);
}
// WARNING: recursion depth equals the longest path. With n = 1e5 in a chain this
// throws StackOverflowError. Use the iterative form for large graphs.

// ── DFS iterative ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
st.push(src);
while (!st.isEmpty()) {
    int u = st.pop();
    if (seen[u]) continue;                      // check on POP for DFS
    seen[u] = true;
    for (int v : adj[u]) if (!seen[v]) st.push(v);
}

// ── CYCLE DETECTION in a DIRECTED graph — three-colour DFS ────────────
// 0 = unvisited, 1 = in the current recursion stack, 2 = fully explored
boolean hasCycle(int u, int[] state) {
    state[u] = 1;
    for (int v : adj[u]) {
        if (state[v] == 1) return true;           // back edge to the current path = cycle
        if (state[v] == 0 && hasCycle(v, state)) return true;
    }
    state[u] = 2;
    return false;
}
// A plain boolean[] visited is NOT enough — a node seen on a DIFFERENT branch
// is not a cycle. You must distinguish "on the current path" from "done".

// ── TOPOLOGICAL SORT — Kahn's algorithm (BFS on in-degrees) ───────────
int[] indeg = new int[n];
for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) for (int v : adj[u]) indeg[v]++;
Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) if (indeg[u] == 0) q.offer(u);
List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    int u = q.poll();
    order.add(u);
    for (int v : adj[u]) if (--indeg[v] == 0) q.offer(v);
}
if (order.size() != n) /* there is a cycle — no valid ordering */;
// That final size check IS the cycle detection. "Course Schedule" is exactly this.

// ── DIJKSTRA — see Chapter 24 ─────────────────────────────────────────

// ── BELLMAN-FORD — handles NEGATIVE weights, O(V*E) ───────────────────
long[] dist = new long[n];
Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE / 4);
dist[src] = 0;
for (int iter = 0; iter < n - 1; iter++)
    for (int[] e : edges)
        if (dist[e[0]] + e[2] < dist[e[1]]) dist[e[1]] = dist[e[0]] + e[2];
// One more pass that still improves anything means a negative cycle exists.
// "Cheapest Flights Within K Stops" is Bellman-Ford limited to K+1 iterations.

// ── FLOYD-WARSHALL — all-pairs shortest paths, O(V^3) ─────────────────
for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
  for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
    for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
      if (d[i][k] + d[k][j] < d[i][j]) d[i][j] = d[i][k] + d[k][j];
// The k loop MUST be outermost. Getting that wrong is the classic error.
// Use it when V <= ~400 and you need every pair.
◆ Choosing the algorithm from the problem statement
The problem says…UseComplexity
Shortest path, all edges cost 1 (or unweighted)BFSO(V+E)
Shortest path, non-negative weightsDijkstra + PriorityQueueO(E log V)
Shortest path, some negative weightsBellman–FordO(V·E)
Shortest path between every pair, small VFloyd–WarshallO(V³)
Edges cost 0 or 10-1 BFS — deque, addFirst for 0, addLast for 1O(V+E)
Valid ordering respecting dependenciesTopological sort (Kahn or DFS)O(V+E)
Is there a cycle? (directed)3-colour DFS, or topo-sort size checkO(V+E)
Is there a cycle? (undirected)DSU (union returns false), or DFS tracking the parentO(E·α)
Connected components / dynamic connectivityDSU, or DFS/BFS if staticO(E·α)
Cheapest set of edges connecting everythingKruskal (DSU) or Prim (heap)O(E log E)
Can the graph be 2-coloured?BFS/DFS 2-colouring — bipartite checkO(V+E)
Count paths / longest path in a DAGTopological order + DPO(V+E)
Strongly connected componentsTarjan or KosarajuO(V+E)
Chapter 53

Other structures you must be able to build

Java gives you no Pair, no multiset, no ordered-statistic tree and no min-max heap. Here is how to fake each one with what the JDK does provide — the composition tricks are exactly what "design a data structure" questions test.

Pair / Tuple

No JDK Pair exists (javafx.util.Pair and AbstractMap.SimpleEntry both exist but are awkward). Four real options:

record Pair<A,B>(A first, B second) {}   // J16 — best
Map.entry(a, b)                          // J9, immutable, getKey/getValue
new int[]{a, b}                          // fastest; NEVER as a hash key
long packed = ((long) a << 32) | (b & 0xFFFFFFFFL);
Multiset / Counter

No Multiset. Use a map of counts, and remember to remove the entry at zero so size() means "distinct elements present".

Map<T,Integer> ms = new HashMap<>();
ms.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);            // add
ms.merge(x, -1,
    (a,b) -> a+b == 0 ? null : a+b);     // remove one
// Use TreeMap instead for a SORTED multiset
// with firstKey()/lastKey() in O(log n).
Ordered statistics ("k-th smallest")

TreeSet has no "element at rank k" in O(log n) — headSet(x).size() is O(n). Options: a Fenwick tree over compressed values (Chapter 50), two heaps for the median specifically, or a segment tree over the value range.

Min-max heap

PriorityQueue gives you one end only. For both ends use a TreeMap multiset (firstKey/lastKey, plus pollFirstEntry/pollLastEntry), or two heaps kept in sync with lazy deletion.

Design problems worth building once

// ── MIN STACK — O(1) push, pop, top AND getMin ────────────────────────
class MinStack {
    private final Deque<int[]> st = new ArrayDeque<>();   // {value, minSoFar}
    void push(int x) {
        int min = st.isEmpty() ? x : Math.min(x, st.peek()[1]);
        st.push(new int[]{x, min});
    }
    void pop()    { st.pop(); }
    int  top()    { return st.peek()[0]; }
    int  getMin() { return st.peek()[1]; }
}
// The insight: store the running minimum WITH each element. A second "min stack"
// that only pushes on a new minimum is the O(1)-extra-space variant.

// ── QUEUE FROM TWO STACKS — amortised O(1) ────────────────────────────
class MyQueue {
    private final Deque<Integer> in = new ArrayDeque<>(), out = new ArrayDeque<>();
    void push(int x) { in.push(x); }
    int  pop()       { shift(); return out.pop(); }
    int  peek()      { shift(); return out.peek(); }
    boolean empty() { return in.isEmpty() && out.isEmpty(); }
    private void shift() {
        if (out.isEmpty()) while (!in.isEmpty()) out.push(in.pop());
    }
}
// Only shift when `out` is EMPTY. Each element moves at most once, so n operations
// cost O(n) total — amortised O(1). Shifting every time would be O(n) per call.

// ── TIME-BASED KEY-VALUE STORE ────────────────────────────────────────
class TimeMap {
    private final Map<String, TreeMap<Integer,String>> m = new HashMap<>();
    void set(String k, String v, int t) {
        m.computeIfAbsent(k, x -> new TreeMap<>()).put(t, v);
    }
    String get(String k, int t) {
        var tm = m.get(k);
        if (tm == null) return "";
        var e = tm.floorEntry(t);                // latest value at or before t
        return e == null ? "" : e.getValue();
    }
}
// HashMap for the key, TreeMap for the timeline, floorEntry for the lookup.
// Composition of two JDK structures — exactly what these questions reward.

// ── HIT COUNTER (sliding window of 300 seconds) ───────────────────────
class HitCounter {
    private final Deque<int[]> q = new ArrayDeque<>();     // {timestamp, count}
    private int total = 0;
    void hit(int t) {
        if (!q.isEmpty() && q.peekLast()[0] == t) q.peekLast()[1]++;
        else q.offerLast(new int[]{t, 1});
        total++;
    }
    int getHits(int t) {
        while (!q.isEmpty() && q.peekFirst()[0] <= t - 300)
            total -= q.pollFirst()[1];               // expire old buckets
        return total;
    }
}

// ── CIRCULAR QUEUE (ring buffer) ──────────────────────────────────────
class MyCircularQueue {
    private final int[] a;
    private int head = 0, size = 0;
    MyCircularQueue(int k) { a = new int[k]; }
    boolean enQueue(int v) {
        if (size == a.length) return false;
        a[(head + size++) % a.length] = v;           // modular arithmetic does the wrapping
        return true;
    }
    boolean deQueue() {
        if (size == 0) return false;
        head = (head + 1) % a.length; size--;
        return true;
    }
    int Front() { return size == 0 ? -1 : a[head]; }
    int Rear()  { return size == 0 ? -1 : a[(head + size - 1) % a.length]; }
}
// Tracking (head, size) is cleaner than (head, tail) — no ambiguity between
// "full" and "empty" when the two pointers coincide.
Chapter 54

40 traps that fail your submission

Every entry here is a real, specific defect that compiles cleanly and produces a wrong answer or an exception at runtime. If a solution of yours is failing and you cannot see why, read this list — the cause is very likely on it.

Boxing & equality

#The trapThe fix
1== on Integer works for −128…127 then silently failsAlways .equals(), or unbox to int first.
2== on String works for literals then fails for computed valuesAlways .equals().
3int x = map.get(k) throws NPE when k is absentmap.getOrDefault(k, 0).
4list.remove(2) removes the index, not the value 2list.remove(Integer.valueOf(2)).
5Integer.valueOf(1).equals(1L) is falseNever mix wrapper types; compare as primitives.
6Set<int[]> never dedupes — arrays use reference identityUse a record, a String key, or pack into a long.
7a.equals(b) on two arrays compares referencesArrays.equals(a, b) / Arrays.deepEquals.
8Custom class in a HashSet with only equals overriddenOverride hashCode too — or use a record.

Arithmetic

#The trapThe fix
9(lo + hi) / 2 overflows on large indiceslo + (hi - lo) / 2 or (lo + hi) >>> 1.
10long x = a * b with two ints wraps before assignment(long) a * b or Math.multiplyFull(a, b).
11Summing an int[] into an int overflowsUse a long accumulator, or .asLongStream().sum().
12IntStream.sum() returns int and overflows silently.asLongStream().sum() or .mapToLong(i -> i).sum().
13Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE) is still negativeWiden to long first, or use Math.absExact.
14-1 % 5 == -1, which crashes as an array indexMath.floorMod(-1, 5) → 4.
15n % 2 == 1 is false for negative odd numbers(n & 1) == 1, or n % 2 != 0.
16(int) Math.pow(10, 2) can be 99Write a fast-power loop, or use BigInteger.pow.
170.1 + 0.2 != 0.3Compare with an epsilon, or reformulate in integers.
181 << 35 equals 8, not 235 — shift is mod 321L << 35.
19Double.MIN_VALUE is the smallest positive valueUse -Double.MAX_VALUE or NEGATIVE_INFINITY.
20x & 1 == 0 does not compile (precedence)(x & 1) == 0 — parenthesise every bit expression.

Collections

#The trapThe fix
21ConcurrentModificationException when removing inside a for-eachremoveIf, an explicit Iterator, or iterate backwards by index.
22List.of(…) and stream().toList() are immutable — sort/add thrownew ArrayList<>(…) or collect(Collectors.toList()).
23Arrays.asList(intArray) gives a 1-element listArrays.stream(a).boxed().toList().
24Arrays.asList(…).add(x) throws UnsupportedOperationExceptionWrap it: new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(…)).
25subList is a live view — mutating either side affects the othernew ArrayList<>(list.subList(a, b)) for a copy.
26Adding the live path list to results — every entry aliases one objectres.add(new ArrayList<>(path)) at the leaf.
27Printing a PriorityQueue shows heap order, not sorted orderDrain it with repeated poll().
28PriorityQueue.remove(Object) is O(n)Use lazy deletion, or a TreeMap multiset.
29new PriorityQueue<>(0, cmp) throws — capacity must be ≥ 1Math.max(1, n), or use the no-capacity constructor.
30A TreeSet comparator that is not total silently drops elementsAdd a tie-breaker so no two distinct items compare equal.
31ArrayDeque throws NPE on nullUse a sentinel object, or the size() loop for BFS levels.
32Stack iterates bottom-to-top; ArrayDeque top-to-bottomUse ArrayDeque consistently and reverse deliberately.
33TreeMap/TreeSet throw NPE on a null keyGuard, or use HashMap if null keys are needed.
34list.contains inside a loop is an accidental O(n²)If you are testing membership, the container should be a Set.
35Collectors.toMap throws on a duplicate key, and on a null valuePass a merge function, or use groupingBy.
36grid.clone() on a 2-D array is shallowClone each row, or Arrays.stream(g).map(int[]::clone).toArray(int[][]::new).

Strings, streams & control flow

#The trapThe fix
37s += c in a loop is O(n²)StringBuilder.append.
38"a.b".split(".") returns an empty arraysplit("\\.") or Pattern.quote(".").
39split drops trailing empty stringssplit(regex, -1).
40allMatch/noneMatch return true for an empty streamCheck isEmpty() first when vacuous truth is wrong for your case.
41A stream with no terminal operation silently does nothingEvery pipeline needs a terminal call.
42Reusing a consumed stream throws IllegalStateExceptionMaterialise to a list if you need two passes.
43You cannot break out of forEachUse a plain for loop, or anyMatch/findFirst.
44A lambda cannot modify a captured localUse an int[1], an AtomicInteger, a field, or a loop.
45Recursive DFS on a 10⁵-node chain overflows the stackConvert to an explicit stack.
46Marking BFS nodes visited on dequeue instead of enqueueMark when you enqueue — otherwise nodes are queued many times.
47System.out.println(charArray) prints the characters; "" + charArray prints an object idnew String(arr) when you want the text.
48PrintWriter output vanishes without flush()Call flush(), or use try-with-resources.
◆ The five-minute debugging checklist
  1. Print the state. Arrays.toString for 1-D, Arrays.deepToString for 2-D, plain println for collections and records.
  2. Check the boundaries. n = 0, n = 1, all-equal elements, all-negative elements, and the maximum constraint value.
  3. Check the types. Is anything int that should be long? Did you unbox a possible null?
  4. Check the inclusivity. substring(a, b) and subList(a, b) are end-exclusive; rangeClosed is inclusive; floor/ceiling are inclusive while lower/higher are not.
  5. Check aliasing. Did you add a live mutable object to your results instead of a copy?
Chapter 55

Complexity master table

Every operation you will use, in one place. The second table — input size to expected complexity — is the one that tells you which algorithm the problem wants before you have written a line.
