Input size → expected complexity read the constraints, then read this table

Constraint on nTarget complexityWhat the problem almost certainly wants
n ≤ 10O(n!) or O(nn)Full permutation enumeration. Brute-force backtracking.
n ≤ 20O(2n · n)Bitmask DP. This constraint is a deliberate signal — stop hunting for a greedy solution.
n ≤ 100O(n³) or O(n⁴)Interval DP, Floyd–Warshall, matrix chain multiplication.
n ≤ 500O(n³)Floyd–Warshall, 3-nested-loop DP.
n ≤ 2,000O(n²)2-D DP (edit distance, LCS), all-pairs scan.
n ≤ 10,000O(n²) borderline · O(n log n) safePrefer O(n log n) if the constant factor is unclear.
n ≤ 100,000O(n log n)Sorting, heap, binary search, TreeMap, divide and conquer. The most common tier.
n ≤ 1,000,000O(n) or O(n log n)Single pass with a hash map, two pointers, sliding window, prefix sums.
n ≤ 108O(n) with a tiny constantSimple array scan. No boxing, no streams, no maps. Use fast I/O.
n up to 1018O(log n) or O(1)Binary search on the answer, maths, fast exponentiation, digit DP.
◆ The rule of 10⁸

A modern judge executes roughly 10⁸ simple operations per second for Java (a little less than C++; allow perhaps 5×10⁷ to be safe, and much less if you are boxing or allocating). Multiply your candidate complexity by the maximum n and check it against that budget:

  • n = 10⁵ with O(n²) → 1010 operations → way over. Rejected.
  • n = 10⁵ with O(n log n) → about 1.7×10⁶ → trivially fine.
  • n = 10⁴ with O(n²) → 10⁸ → borderline; it will pass in Java only with a clean inner loop.
  • n = 20 with O(2n·n) → about 2×10⁷ → fine.

Do this arithmetic before you write code. It is the single highest-value habit in competitive and interview programming, and it frequently tells you the intended approach outright.

Sorting algorithm reference

AlgorithmBestAverageWorstSpaceStableWhere it is in the JDK
Dual-pivot quicksortO(n log n)O(n log n)O(n²)O(log n)NoArrays.sort(int[]) and all primitives
TimSortO(n)O(n log n)O(n log n)O(n)YesArrays.sort(T[]), Collections.sort, List.sort
Heap sortO(n log n)O(n log n)O(n log n)O(1)NoConceptually, draining a PriorityQueue
Merge sortO(n log n)O(n log n)O(n log n)O(n)YesThe basis of TimSort; write it by hand for linked lists
Counting sortO(n+k)O(n+k)O(n+k)O(k)YesHand-rolled. Use when the value range k is small — "Sort Colors", character sorting.
Bucket sortO(n+k)O(n+k)O(n²)O(n)YesHand-rolled. "Top K Frequent Elements" in O(n).
Radix sortO(d·n)O(d·n)O(d·n)O(n+k)YesHand-rolled. "Maximum Gap" in linear time.
QuickselectO(n)O(n)O(n²)O(1)NoHand-rolled. "Kth Largest Element" without a heap.
◆ Why the JDK uses two different sorts

Primitives get quicksort because stability is meaningless for values with no identity, and quicksort sorts in place with excellent cache behaviour. Objects get TimSort because stability is observable and matters (sort by name, then stably by age, and the name order survives), and because real-world object data often contains sorted runs that TimSort exploits to reach O(n).

The practical consequences: Arrays.sort(int[]) has an O(n²) adversarial worst case, so box to Integer[] or shuffle first on hostile judges. And you can rely on stability for object sorts, which is what makes multi-key sorting by repeated single-key passes correct.

Chapter 56

Pattern → API playbook

The bridge between "I recognise the pattern" and "I know which methods to type". Each row names the signal in the problem statement, the structure to reach for, and the exact API calls that implement it.

Pattern & recognition signalStructureComplexityThe API calls you will actually type
Array & string scanning
Two Sum / pair lookup
"find two elements that…"		HashMapO(n)map.containsKey(target - x), map.put(x, i). One pass: check before you insert, so you never pair an element with itself.
Two pointers, opposite ends
sorted array, palindrome, "container"		int[] + two indicesO(n)while (l < r), s.charAt(l++), s.charAt(r--). Sort first if the input is unsorted.
Two pointers, same direction
"remove duplicates in place", partition		int[] + read/write indicesO(n)A write index that advances only on a keeper: if (keep) a[write++] = a[read];
Sliding window, fixed size k
"every subarray of length k"		running sum / ArrayDequeO(n)Add a[r], subtract a[r-k]. For the maximum, a monotonic ArrayDeque with peekFirst/pollLast.
Sliding window, variable size
"longest/shortest substring such that…"		HashMap or int[128] countsO(n)for (r…) { add a[r]; while (invalid) remove a[l++]; best = max(best, r-l+1); }
Prefix sums
"sum of range", "subarray sums to k"		long[] prefix + HashMapO(n)pre[i+1] = pre[i] + a[i]; then map.getOrDefault(sum - k, 0) to count subarrays.
Difference array
many range updates, one final read		int[] diffO(n + q)diff[l] += v; diff[r+1] -= v; then one prefix-sum pass to materialise.
Monotonic stack
"next greater/smaller", histogram, rain water		ArrayDeque of indicesO(n)while (!st.isEmpty() && a[st.peek()] < a[i]) res[st.pop()] = i; then st.push(i).
Cyclic sort / index-as-hash
"array of 1..n, find missing/duplicate"		the input array itselfO(n), O(1) spaceSwap a[i] to index a[i]-1, or negate a[abs(a[i])-1] as a visited marker.
Searching
Binary search on an index
sorted, or rotated-sorted		int[]O(log n)int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2; Decide lo = mid + 1 vs hi = mid and keep the invariant consistent.
Binary search on the answer
"minimise the maximum", "least capacity"		a monotone predicateO(n log range)while (lo < hi) { mid = lo + (hi-lo)/2; if (feasible(mid)) hi = mid; else lo = mid+1; } → lo.
Lower / upper bound
"first element ≥ x", counting		sorted array or TreeMapO(log n)Arrays.binarySearch then ~ret; or TreeMap.ceilingKey / floorKey.
Quickselect
"kth largest", no full sort needed		int[] + RandomO(n) expectedRandomised pivot partition, recurse into one side only.
Heaps & ordering
Top K
"k largest / k most frequent / k closest"		PriorityQueue size kO(n log k)MIN-heap for the k largest; pq.offer(x); if (pq.size() > k) pq.poll();
Merge K sortedPriorityQueue of headsO(N log k)Seed with each list's head; after each poll, push that list's next element.
Running mediantwo PriorityQueuesO(log n) per addMax-heap lo + min-heap hi; push to lo, transfer lo.poll() to hi, rebalance.
Interval scheduling
"merge intervals", "meeting rooms"		int[][] + sort (+ heap)O(n log n)Arrays.sort(iv, comparingInt(a -> a[0])); merge when cur[0] <= end. For room count, a min-heap of end times.
Sweep line / event counting
"maximum concurrent", "car pooling"		TreeMap or int[] deltaO(n log n)delta.merge(start, 1, Integer::sum), delta.merge(end, -1, Integer::sum), then a running sum over values().
Trees & graphs
Tree level-order
"by level", "right side view", "min depth"		ArrayDequeO(n)int sz = q.size(); then an inner loop of exactly sz iterations.
Tree post-order aggregation
diameter, balance, max path sum		recursion + a fieldO(n)Return one value to the parent, update a global best with a different value.
BST ordered access
"kth smallest", "validate"		in-order traversalO(h + k)Iterative in-order with an ArrayDeque; stop after k, or check the sequence increases.
Grid BFS
"shortest path in a maze", "rotting oranges"		ArrayDeque + DR/DC arraysO(RC)Mark visited on enqueue. Seed all sources for multi-source.
Grid DFS / flood fill
"number of islands", "area of region"		recursion or explicit stackO(RC)Bounds check first, then validity; mark in place with a sentinel character.
Topological order
"course schedule", "build order"		int[] indegree + ArrayDequeO(V+E)Enqueue the zero-indegree nodes; if (--indeg[v] == 0) q.offer(v); a short result means a cycle.
Weighted shortest pathPriorityQueue + dist[]O(E log V)Dijkstra with lazy deletion: if (d > dist[u]) continue;
Dynamic connectivity
"are these connected", "merge accounts"		DSUO(E·α)find with path compression, union by size; union returning false is cycle detection.
Recursion & DP
Backtracking
"generate all…", permutations, subsets, N-Queens		List/StringBuilder pathexponentialChoose → recurse → un-choose. Snapshot with new ArrayList<>(path) only at a leaf.
Top-down DP (memoisation)int[] / int[][] / HashMapO(states)Arrays.fill(memo, -1); return the cached value if set. Use a record key for complex state.
Bottom-up DPint[] / int[][]O(states)Identify the state, the transition, the base case, and the iteration order — that last one is where most bugs are.
Space-optimised DP
each row depends only on the previous		two int[] rowsO(n) spaceKeep prev and cur and swap; or iterate one array backwards for 0/1-knapsack.
Bitmask DP
n ≤ 20 in the constraints		int[1<<n][n]O(2n·n²)for (mask…), test with (mask >> i & 1), extend with mask | (1 << j).
Interval DP
"burst balloons", "matrix chain"		int[n][n]O(n³)Loop over length outermost, then the left endpoint, then the split point.
Counting & grouping
Frequency countingint[26] / int[128] / HashMapO(n)freq[c - 'a']++, or map.merge(k, 1, Integer::sum).
Grouping by a key
"group anagrams", bucketing		HashMap of ListsO(n·L)map.computeIfAbsent(key, x -> new ArrayList<>()).add(v).
DedupeHashSet / LinkedHashSetO(n)if (!seen.add(x)) for duplicate detection; LinkedHashSet when order must survive.
Prefix matching
autocomplete, word search		TrieO(L)Node[] kids = new Node[26]; prune the search when a child is null.
Range sum with updatesFenwick treeO(log n)p += p & -p to update, p -= p & -p to query. 1-indexed.

Copy-paste snippet vault

The lines you will type most often, gathered in one place so you can drill them until they are automatic. Everything here appears somewhere above with full explanation; this chapter exists purely for speed of recall.

Setup & initialisation

// Collections
List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();
Map<Character,Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
Deque<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
PriorityQueue<Integer> minHeap = new PriorityQueue<>();
PriorityQueue<Integer> maxHeap = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
TreeMap<Integer,Integer> sorted = new TreeMap<>();

// Arrays
int[] freq  = new int[26];                    // lowercase letters
int[] freqA = new int[128];                   // all ASCII
int[] memo  = new int[n];      Arrays.fill(memo, -1);
int[] dist  = new int[n];      Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
int[] parent= new int[n];      Arrays.setAll(parent, i -> i);   // DSU
int[][] dp  = new int[n+1][m+1];
for (int[] row : dp) Arrays.fill(row, -1);
boolean[] visited = new boolean[n];
boolean[][] seen2 = new boolean[R][C];

// Adjacency list
List<Integer>[] adj = new List[n];
Arrays.setAll(adj, i -> new ArrayList<>());
for (int[] e : edges) { adj[e[0]].add(e[1]); adj[e[1]].add(e[0]); }

// Grid directions
static final int[] DR = {-1,1,0,0}, DC = {0,0,-1,1};

// Sentinels & constants
int best = Integer.MIN_VALUE;                // maximising
int best = Integer.MAX_VALUE;                // minimising
static final int MOD = 1_000_000_007;
static final int INF = Integer.MAX_VALUE / 2;  // headroom for dist + w

Conversions

String  → char[]        : s.toCharArray()
char[]  → String        : new String(a)
String  → int           : Integer.parseInt(s)
int     → String        : String.valueOf(i)
char    → int digit     : c - '0'
int     → char digit    : (char)(d + '0')
char    → int index     : c - 'a'
int[]   → List<Integer>  : Arrays.stream(a).boxed().toList()
List<Integer> → int[]  : list.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray()
List<String>  → String[]: list.toArray(new String[0])
List<int[]>   → int[][]: list.toArray(new int[0][])
int[]   → sorted copy : int[] b = a.clone(); Arrays.sort(b);
String  → sorted String : char[] c = s.toCharArray(); Arrays.sort(c); new String(c)
Collection → deduped : new HashSet<>(c)
Collection → sorted  : new TreeSet<>(c)  // also dedupes
(r,c)   → int key     : r * C + c
(a,b)   → long key    : ((long) a << 32) | (b & 0xFFFFFFFFL)

Sorting

Arrays.sort(nums);                                              // ascending primitives
Arrays.sort(iv, Comparator.comparingInt(a -> a[0]));             // 2-D by column 0
Arrays.sort(iv, Comparator.<int[]>comparingInt(a -> a[0])
                       .thenComparingInt(a -> -a[1]));           // two keys
list.sort(null);                                                // natural order
list.sort(Comparator.reverseOrder());                           // descending
list.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(String::length));              // by a field
entries.sort(Map.Entry.<String,Integer>comparingByValue().reversed());
Collections.reverse(list);                                      // reverse in place
// descending int[] — sort ascending then reverse manually:
Arrays.sort(a);
for (int i = 0, j = a.length-1; i < j; i++, j--) { int t=a[i]; a[i]=a[j]; a[j]=t; }

The core loops

// Sliding window, variable size
int l = 0, best = 0;
for (int r = 0; r < n; r++) {
    // add a[r] to the window
    while (/* window invalid */) { /* remove a[l] */ l++; }
    best = Math.max(best, r - l + 1);
}

// Binary search on an index
int lo = 0, hi = n - 1;
while (lo <= hi) {
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
    if (a[mid] == target) return mid;
    if (a[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid - 1;
}
return -1;

// Binary search on the answer
int lo = minPossible, hi = maxPossible;
while (lo < hi) {
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
    if (feasible(mid)) hi = mid; else lo = mid + 1;
}
return lo;

// BFS with levels
Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
q.offer(src); visited[src] = true;
int depth = 0;
while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    for (int i = q.size(); i > 0; i--) {
        int u = q.poll();
        for (int v : adj[u]) if (!visited[v]) { visited[v] = true; q.offer(v); }
    }
    depth++;
}

// Monotonic stack
Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    while (!st.isEmpty() && a[st.peek()] < a[i]) res[st.pop()] = i;
    st.push(i);
}

// Backtracking
void dfs(int start, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> res) {
    res.add(new ArrayList<>(path));
    for (int i = start; i < n; i++) {
        path.add(nums[i]);
        dfs(i + 1, path, res);
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);
    }
}

// Grid DFS
void dfs(char[][] g, int r, int c) {
    if (r < 0 || r >= g.length || c < 0 || c >= g[0].length || g[r][c] != '1') return;
    g[r][c] = '0';
    dfs(g, r+1, c); dfs(g, r-1, c); dfs(g, r, c+1); dfs(g, r, c-1);
}

// Prefix sums + subarray counting
long[] pre = new long[n + 1];
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) pre[i+1] = pre[i] + a[i];
Map<Long,Integer> count = new HashMap<>();
count.put(0L, 1);                          // the empty prefix — do not forget this
long sum = 0; int ans = 0;
for (int x : a) {
    sum += x;
    ans += count.getOrDefault(sum - k, 0);
    count.merge(sum, 1, Integer::sum);
}

if (!seen.add(x)) /* duplicate */;                     // dedupe check
new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString();              // reverse a string
s.replaceAll("[^a-zA-Z0-9]", "").toLowerCase();         // clean a string
s.strip().split("\\s+");                               // split into words
String.join(",", list);                                // join
Arrays.stream(a).max().getAsInt();                       // max of int[]
Arrays.stream(a).asLongStream().sum();                   // overflow-safe sum
Arrays.stream(a).summaryStatistics();                   // min/max/sum/avg/count
Collections.max(map.values());                         // highest count
Collections.max(map.entrySet(),
    Map.Entry.comparingByValue()).getKey();             // most frequent key
Arrays.toString(a);  Arrays.deepToString(grid);         // debugging
Math.floorMod(i - 1, n);                              // circular index
31 - Integer.numberOfLeadingZeros(n);                   // exact floor(log2 n)
Integer.bitCount(x);                                  // popcount
(n & (n - 1)) == 0 && n > 0;                             // power of two
x & -x;                                             // lowest set bit
setA.retainAll(setB);                                 // intersection
new ArrayList<>(new LinkedHashSet<>(list));              // dedupe, keep order
new ArrayList<>(Collections.nCopies(n, 0));             // zero-filled list
list.getLast();  list.removeLast();  list.reversed();  // J21
Chapter 58

An eight-week practice plan

Reading a reference does not build recall; solving problems does. This plan turns the codex into practice. It assumes roughly ten hours a week and is ordered so that each week's APIs are the ones the previous week's problems exposed as gaps.

WeekChapters to internaliseWhat to practiseThe drill that proves it
102, 03, 08, 09, 12, 13Arrays & strings. Two pointers, in-place reversal, palindromes, frequency counting with int[26].Write Arrays.sort/fill/copyOfRange/binarySearch and the 20 most common String methods from memory, no autocomplete.
216, 17, 18, 20HashMap and HashSet problems. Two Sum, group anagrams, longest substring without repeats, subarray sum equals k.Solve a counting problem four ways: getOrDefault, merge, compute, and int[26]. Explain when each is best.
323, 25, 47ArrayDeque as stack and queue. Bracket matching, monotonic stack, BFS on a grid, linked-list manipulation.Write next-greater-element, sliding-window-maximum, and iterative in-order traversal without looking. All three from the same class.
424, 27, 19, 22Heaps, comparators, TreeMap. Top-K, merge K lists, running median, merge intervals, calendar booking.Build six comparators from spoken descriptions in under a minute each. Recite when to use a min-heap versus a max-heap for top-K, and why.
552, 49Graphs. BFS/DFS, topological sort, Dijkstra, union-find, cycle detection, bipartite checking.Write the DSU class and Kahn's topological sort from scratch in under five minutes each.
607, 14, 56DP. 1-D, 2-D, knapsack, LCS, edit distance, bitmask DP, interval DP.For ten DP problems, state the recurrence out loud before writing code. Identify state, transition, base case, iteration order.
748, 50, 51, 53Tries, Fenwick trees, design problems. LRU/LFU, min stack, time map, word search II.Write the trie and the Fenwick tree from memory. Design three data structures by composing JDK classes.
854, 55, 41–46Mixed timed practice. Random problem selection, 30-minute limit, no hints.Read all 48 traps and confirm you have hit — and now understand — at least fifteen. Do a final pass on the Java 17→21 differences.
◆ How to practise so it actually sticks
  1. Estimate the complexity before writing code. Read the constraints, do the 10⁸ arithmetic from Chapter 55, and commit to a target. This single habit prevents most wasted attempts.
  2. Say the approach out loud first. "I'll use a sliding window with a frequency map, expanding right and shrinking left while the window is invalid." If you cannot say it, you cannot code it — and in a real interview, saying it is half the assessment.
  3. Write it without autocomplete at least once a week. Autocomplete hides exactly the gaps this document exists to close. On a whiteboard or a shared doc there is no autocomplete.
  4. Re-solve anything you had to look up, three days later, from scratch. Spaced repetition on problems works the same way it does on vocabulary. A problem you solved with a hint is not yet a problem you can solve.
  5. Keep a personal trap list. Every time a submission fails, write down the one-line cause. After thirty entries you will see that you have four recurring mistakes, not thirty — and those four are what to drill.
  6. Time yourself from week 5. Easy in 15 minutes, medium in 30, hard in 45. Speed comes from recall, and recall is what this codex is for.
◆ What "knowing the API" actually buys you

Interviewers are not grading your memory of method names. They are grading whether you can get from an idea to correct working code without friction. Knowing that computeIfAbsent exists saves you three lines and, more importantly, saves you the twenty seconds of hesitation that breaks your train of thought about the algorithm. Knowing that TreeMap.floorKey exists means you recognise a problem as a floor-query problem instead of reaching for a sort you do not need.

That is the whole argument for this document. The APIs are not the skill — they are the friction you remove so the skill has room to show.
